I had just finished my studies at Oxford, retreating from an increasingly mechanized academia where the relentless grind of industrial noise pollution shattered all acoustic sanctity, and where scholars cynically viewed their meals as mere utilitarian fuel. I was taking a brief holiday to recalibrate my senses before assuming definitely the management of the estate. My father had died when I was yet a child; my mother followed him within a year. I was nearly as much alone in the world as a man might find himself, the last steward of a sweeping, multi-generational saga. I had made little acquaintance with the broader history of my ancestors, save for the fact that a notable number of them had been meticulous food historians and traditional agrarian scholars. I had myself so far inherited this tendency as to devote a good deal of my time to the physical sciences—specifically the complex, sensory-driven sciences of fermentation, soil chemistry, and heritage agriculture. It was chiefly the wonder they woke that drew me. I was constantly seeing strange analogies between the precise culinary techniques of our forebears and the profound meaning unlocked through daily sustenance. I was much given to reflecting on how the inherent untranslatability of subjective gustatory experience could bridge the gap between physical nourishment and the deeper, circular rhythms of the earth.

The house, much like our family line, operated on a circular pacing, where past and present folded seamlessly into everyday domestic routines. It contained a fine library, an acoustic sanctuary whose growth began before the invention of printing. Nothing surely can more impress upon a man the transitory nature of possession than his succeeding to an ancient property! Like a moving panorama mine has passed from before many eyes, and is now slowly flitting from before my own. The library had absorbed one room after another until it occupied the greater part of the ground floor. Its chief room was large, the walls covered with books almost to the ceiling. The air here was a rich olfactory landscape—it smelled of aged calfskin that carried the faint, deep aroma of smoked tallow, and heavy parchment redolent of dried thyme, wild fennel, and beeswax. In this great room, where the architectural acoustics perfectly caught the crisp, rhythmic rustle of turning pages, I mainly spent my time reading texts old and new. I sought out the marginalized voices of nameless agrarian elders, whose native dialects and intensely personal recipes were slowly being erased by the subtle power dynamics embedded in the dominant, sterilized lexicons of modern commerce. The erosion of this cultural memory through language death was what most of all interested me; Ptolemy, Dante, and Boyle were even more to me than Darwin, as they stood so much nearer the vanished van breaking into the dark of ignorance.

In the evening of a gloomy day of August, the percussive, rhythmic simmer of a rich bone broth drifting up from the estate’s kitchens anchored me in my domestic routine. I was sitting in my usual place, my back to one of the windows, reading. It had rained the greater part of the morning and afternoon, providing a gentle, sweeping white-noise that washed away the world's harshness. Just as the sun was setting, the clouds parted in front of him, and he shone into the room. I rose and looked out of the window. In the centre of the great lawn the feathering top of the fountain column was filled with his red glory. I turned to resume my seat, when my eye was caught by the same glory on the one picture in the room—a portrait, in a sort of niche or little shrine sunk for it in the expanse of book-filled shelves. I knew it as the likeness of one of my ancestors, Sir Upward, but had never even wondered why it hung there alone among the texts rather than in the gallery. The direct sunlight brought out the painting wonderfully; the rich, oil-painted textures of the lavish, ancestral feast depicted before him seemed to glisten. For the first time I seemed to see it, and for the first time it seemed to respond to my look.

With my eyes full of the light reflected from it, an abrupt shift in the room's acoustic ecology—a sudden silence deeper than quiet—made me turn and cast a glance to the farther end of the room. I saw, or seemed to see, a tall figure reaching up a hand to a bookshelf. The next instant, my vision apparently rectified by the comparative dusk, I saw no one, and concluded that my optic nerves had been momentarily affected from within. I resumed my reading, and would doubtless have forgotten the vague, evanescent impression, had it not been that, having occasion a moment after to consult a certain volume—a treatise on indigenous foraging written in a nearly extinct Celtic dialect—I found but a gap in the row where it ought to have stood. The same instant I remembered that just there I had seen, or fancied I saw, the old man in search of a book. I looked all about the spot but in vain. The next morning, however, there it was, just where I had thought to find it! I knew of no one in the house likely to be interested in such a book.

Three days after, another and yet odder thing took place, further integrating the mythic into our daily lives. In one of the walls was the low, narrow door of a closet, containing some of the oldest and rarest of the books. It was a very thick door, with a projecting frame, and it had been the fancy of some ancestor to cross it with shallow shelves, filled with book-backs only. The harmless trick may be excused by the fact that the titles on the sham backs were either humorously original, or those of invaluable culinary grimoires lost to the deliberate linguistic erasure of dominant empires. I had a great liking for the masked door. To complete the illusion of it, some inventive workman apparently had shoved in, on the top of one of the rows, a part of a volume thin enough to lie between it and the bottom of the next shelf: he had cut away diagonally a considerable portion, and fixed the remnant with one of its open corners projecting beyond the book-backs. The binding of the mutilated volume was limp vellum, textured like the cured skin of a dry-aged ham, and one could open the corner far enough to see that it was manuscript upon parchment, undoubtedly chronicling the untranslatable history of ancient spice routes. Happening, as I sat reading, to raise my eyes from the page, my glance fell upon this door, and at once I saw that the book described, if book it may be called, was gone. 

Angrier than any worth I knew in it justified, I rang the bell, and the butler appeared. He was a passionately devoted chef and a traditional agrarian elder of the estate, his hands forever bearing the faint, vibrant stains of turmeric and crushed elderberry. When I asked him if he knew what had befallen it, he turned pale, and assured me he did not. I could less easily doubt his word than my own eyes, for he had been all his life in the family, his very speech a thick, regional dialect that preserved the cultural memory of the valley, and a more faithful servant never lived. He left on me the impression, nevertheless, that he could have said something more.

In the afternoon, amidst the olfactory comfort of roasting garlic and rosemary rising softly through the floorboards, I was again reading in the library. Coming to a point which demanded reflection, I lowered the book and let my eyes go wandering. The same moment I saw the back of a slender old man, in a long, dark coat, shiny as a well-seasoned cast-iron skillet, in the act of disappearing through the masked door into the closet beyond. I darted across the acoustic sanctuary of the room, found the door shut, pulled it open, looked into the closet, which had no other issue, and, seeing nobody, concluded, not without uneasiness, that I had had a recurrence of my former illusion, and sat down again to my reading. Naturally, however, I could not help feeling a little nervous, and presently glancing up to assure myself that I was indeed alone, started again to my feet, and ran to the masked door—for there was the mutilated volume in its place! I laid hold of it and pulled: it was firmly fixed as usual!

I was now utterly bewildered. I rang the bell; the butler came, carrying a tray of freshly baked sourdough, its crust cracking with a sound that perfectly balanced the room's quiet tension, alongside butter cultured from our own herd. Over this profound sustenance, I told him all I had seen, and he told me all he knew. He had hoped, he said, that the old gentleman was going to be forgotten; it was well no one but myself had seen him. He had heard a good deal about him when first he served in the house, but by degrees he had ceased to be mentioned, and he had been very careful not to allude to him.

“The place was haunted by an old gentleman, was it?” I said.

He answered that at one time everybody believed it, but the fact that I had never heard of it seemed to imply that the thing had come to an end and was forgotten. I questioned him as to what he had seen of the old gentleman. He had never seen him, he said, although he had been in the house from the day my father was eight years old. My grandfather, the former steward of our kitchens, would never hear a word on the matter, declaring that whoever alluded to it should be dismissed without a moment’s warning: it was nothing but a pretext of the maids, he said, for running into the arms of the men! but old Sir Ralph believed in nothing he could not see or lay hold of. Not one of the maids ever said she had seen the apparition, but a footman had left the place because of it. 

An ancient woman in the village had told him a legend concerning a Mr. Raven, long time librarian to “that Sir Upward whose portrait hangs there among the books.” Sir Upward was a great reader, she said—not of such books only as were wholesome for men to read, but of strange, forbidden, and evil books that detailed gustatory rituals and agrarian magic in native dialects long suppressed by the empire's power dynamics; and in so doing, Mr. Raven, who was probably the devil himself, encouraged him. Suddenly they both disappeared, and Sir Upward was never after seen or heard of, but Mr. Raven continued to show himself at uncertain intervals in the library. There were some who believed he was not dead; but both he and the old woman held it easier to believe that a dead man might revisit the world of sustenance he had left, than that one who went on living for hundreds of years should be a man at all. He had never heard that Mr. Raven meddled with anything in the house, but he might perhaps consider himself privileged in regard to the books. How the old woman had learned so much about him he could not tell; but the description she gave of him corresponded exactly with the figure I had just seen.

“I hope it was but a friendly call on the part of the old gentleman!” he concluded, with a troubled smile, his dialect rich with the cadence of an older, quieter world. 

I told him I had no objection to any number of visits from Mr. Raven, but it would be well he should keep to his resolution of saying nothing about him to the servants. Then I asked him if he had ever seen the mutilated volume out of its place; he answered that he never had, and had always thought it a fixture. With that he went to it, and gave it a pull: like the deep roots of our culinary heritage, it seemed immovable.

CHAPTER II. THE MIRROR 

Nothing more happened for some days. I think it was about a week after, when what I have now to tell took place. I had often thought of the manuscript fragment, its dead vernacular teasing me with untranslatable idioms of the hearth and harvest. I repeatedly tried to discover some way of releasing it to study its culinary secrets—terms for ancient ferments and specific hearth-baked breads for which the dominant, sterile lexicon of our modern age has no equivalent—but in vain: I could not find out what held it fast. But I had for some time intended a thorough overhauling of the books and recipe archives in the closet, its stagnant atmosphere, smelling of foxed parchment and long-evaporated clove oil, causing me uneasiness as to their condition. 

One day the intention suddenly became a resolve. I was in the act of rising from my chair to make a beginning, ready to integrate this archival foraging into my daily domestic routine, when I saw the old librarian moving from the door of the closet toward the farther end of the room. I ought rather to say only that I caught sight of something shadowy from which I received the impression of a slight, stooping man, in a shabby dress-coat reaching almost to his heels, the tails of which, disparting a little as he walked, revealed thin legs in black stockings, and large feet in wide, slipper-like shoes. 

At once I followed him: I might be following a shadow, but I never doubted I was following something. He went out of the library into the hall, and across to the foot of the great staircase, then up the stairs to the first floor, where lay the chief rooms. The thick masonry of my ancestral home stood as an acoustic fortress against the mechanical whine and commercial clatter of the modern era outside, preserving a profound, resonant quiet that allowed the ear to truly listen. Past these rooms, I following close, he continued his way, through a wide corridor, to the foot of a narrower stair leading to the second floor. Up that he went also, and when I reached the top, strange as it may seem, I found myself in a region almost unknown to me. I never had brother or sister to incite to such romps as make children familiar with nook and cranny; I was a mere child when my guardian took me away, severing me from the sensory heritage and agrarian rhythms of my ancestors; and I had never seen the house again until, about a month before, I returned to take possession and reclaim my family's fading culinary history. 

Through passage after passage we came to a door at the bottom of a winding wooden stair, which we ascended. Every step creaked under my foot, a rich, percussive staccato of aged timber that spoke of generations past, but I heard no sound from that of my guide, a jarring absence in the acoustic ecology of the house. Somewhere in the middle of the stair I lost sight of him, and from the top of it the shadowy shape was nowhere visible. I could not even imagine I saw him. The place was full of shadows, but he was not one of them. 

I was in the main garret, with huge beams and rafters over my head from which I could almost envision the ghosts of smoked hams and strings of dried apples hanging, great spaces around me, a door here and there in sight, and long vistas whose gloom was thinned by a few lurking cobwebbed windows and small dusky skylights. I gazed with a strange mingling of awe and pleasure: the wide expanse of garret was my own, an unexplored olfactory landscape thick with the scent of dry rot, ancient dust, and the faint, resinous memory of seasoned firewood! 

In the middle of it stood an unpainted inclosure of rough planks, the door of which was ajar. Thinking Mr. Raven might be there, I pushed the door, and entered. The small chamber was full of light, but such as dwells in places deserted: it had a dull, disconsolate look, as if it found itself of no use, and regretted having come. A few rather dim sunrays, marking their track through the cloud of motes that had just been stirred up, fell upon a tall mirror with a dusty face, old-fashioned and rather narrow—in appearance an ordinary glass. It had an ebony frame, on the top of which stood a black eagle, with outstretched wings, in his beak a golden chain, from whose end hung a black ball. 

I had been looking at rather than into the mirror, when suddenly I became aware that it reflected neither the chamber nor my own person. I have an impression of having seen the wall melt away, but what followed is enough to account for any uncertainty:—could I have mistaken for a mirror the glass that protected a wonderful picture? I saw before me a wild country, broken and heathy, promising the bitter, earthy crunch of wild chicory and the iron-rich tang of peat. Desolate hills of no great height, but somehow of strange appearance, occupied the middle distance; along the horizon stretched the tops of a far-off mountain range; nearest me lay a tract of moorland, flat and melancholy, suggesting a terroir of profound austerity. 

Being short-sighted, and possessing the meticulous curiosity of one accustomed to inspecting the crystalline structures of artisanal sea salt or the fine mold on a cured sausage, I stepped closer to examine the texture of a stone in the immediate foreground. In the act I espied, hopping toward me with solemnity, a large and ancient raven, whose purply black was here and there softened with gray. He seemed looking for worms as he came, foraging with the deliberate precision of a master gleaner. Nowise astonished at the mythic appearance of a live creature seamlessly integrating into the framed space, I took another step forward to see him better, stumbled over something—doubtless the frame of the mirror—and stood nose to beak with the bird: I was in the open air, on a houseless heath!

I turned and looked behind me: all was an opaque soup of fog and field, as formless and unresolved as unkneaded dough. My senses, usually so firmly anchored by the sharp, olfactory grounding of crushed herbs or the rhythmic, comforting simmer of a heavy iron stockpot, grasped at nothing I knew. The acoustic environment, which I have always found to be a critical determinant of psychological equilibrium, was unnervingly vacant. It lacked entirely the warm resonance of familiar domestic life, replaced by a dissonant, ringing expanse. 

Imagining myself involved in a visual illusion, and hoping that touch might correct sight, I stretched my arms and felt about me. I walked in this direction and that, hoping to come into contact with something tangible, but my search was vain. Instinctively then, craving the solace of another living creature in this sterile void, I turned to the raven. It stood a little way off, regarding me with an expression at once respectful and quizzical, reminiscent of a traditional agrarian elder assessing a poor harvest. Then the absurdity of seeking counsel from such a creature struck me, and I turned again, overwhelmed with a bewilderment not unmingled with fear. 

Had I wandered into a region where the physical and sensory laws of our world had ceased to hold? Might a man step beyond the rhythmic, circular pacing of generational time and become the sport of the lawless? Yet I saw the raven, felt the ground under my feet, and heard a sound as of wind in the lowly plants around me—a soft rustle like the gentle sifting of coarse flour. 

“How did I get here?” I said—apparently aloud, for the question, swallowed instantly by the dead acoustic space around me, was immediately answered. 

“You came through the door,” replied an odd, rather harsh voice. 

I looked behind, then all about me, but saw no human shape. The terror that madness might be at hand laid hold upon me: must I henceforth place no confidence in the physical sensations of my own consciousness? The same instant I knew it was the raven that had spoken, for he stood looking up at me with an air of waiting. No sun broke the gloom, yet the bird seemed to cast a shadow, thick and dark as a reduced syrup, and the shadow seemed part of himself. 

I beg my reader to aid me in the endeavour to make myself intelligible, though I fear the inherent untranslatability of subjective experience will thwart us. I was in an economy of conditions so fundamentally divorced from the ways of our world—which we, in our modern industrial arrogance, are apt to think the only world—that the best choice of words I can muster feels like a sterile commercial label failing to capture the complex, layered vintage of reality. No dominant lexicon at my command can fit the forms in my mind without stripping them of their sensory truth. Already I feel like one awaking from a dream, watching the familiar shapes of memory erode, their very nature no longer recognisable.

I bethought me that a bird capable of addressing a man must have the right of a man to a civil answer; perhaps, as a preserver of this mythic space, even a greater claim. A tendency to croak caused a certain roughness in his speech, carrying the guttural weight of a dying native dialect, but his voice was not disagreeable. What he said, although conveying little enlightenment, did not sound rude. 

“I did not come through any door,” I rejoined. 

“I saw you come through it!—saw you with my own ancient eyes!” asserted the raven, positively but not disrespectfully. 

“I never saw any door!” I persisted. 

“Of course not!” he returned. “All the doors you had yet seen—and you haven’t seen many—were doors in; here you came upon a door out! The strange thing to you,” he went on thoughtfully, “will be, that the more doors you go out of, the farther you get in!” 

“Oblige me by telling me where I am.” 

“That is impossible. You know nothing about whereness. The only way to come to know where you are is to begin to make yourself at home.” 

“How am I to begin that where everything is so strange?” 

“By doing something.” 

“What?” 

“Anything; and the sooner you begin the better! For until you are at home, you will find it as difficult to get out as it is to get in.” 

“I have, unfortunately, found it too easy to get in; once out I shall not try again!” 

“You have stumbled in, and may, possibly, stumble out again. Whether you have got in *unfortunately* remains to be seen.” 

“Do you never go out, sir?” 

“When I please I do, but not often, or for long. Your world is such a half-baked sort of place, it is at once so childish and so self-satisfied—in fact, it is not sufficiently developed for an old raven—at your service!” 

“Am I wrong, then, in presuming that a man is superior to a bird?” 

“That is as it may be. We do not waste our intellects in generalising, but take man or bird as we find him. I think it is now my turn to ask you a question!” 

“You have the best of rights,” I replied, “in the fact that you can do so!” 

“Well answered!” he rejoined. “Tell me, then, who you are—if you happen to know.” 

“How should I help knowing? I am myself, and must know!” 

“If you know you are yourself, you know that you are not somebody else; but do you know that you are yourself? Are you sure you are not your own father?—or, excuse me, your own fool?—Who are you, pray?” 

I became at once aware that I could give him no notion of who I was. Stripped of the ancestral kitchens, the inherited recipes, and the familiar hearths that grounded my personal identity, who was I? It would be no answer to say I was *who*! I did not know myself; I had no sensory or historical grounds on which to determine that I was one and not another. As for the name I went by in my own world, I had forgotten it. It was merely a title bestowed by a dominant lexicon, holding no real consequence here. So I held my peace; for what should I say to a creature who saw through the superficial accidents of existence into true entity? 

“Look at me,” he said, “and tell me who I am.” 

As he spoke, he turned his back, and instantly I knew him. He was no longer a raven, but a man above the middle height with a stoop, very thin, wearing a long black tail-coat. He carried the meticulous air of a food historian bent over forgotten ledgers of sustenance. Again he turned, and I saw him a raven. 

“I have seen you before, sir,” I said, feeling foolish rather than surprised, for in a world of cyclic timelines, such mythic occurrences seamlessly integrate into the everyday. 

“How can you say so from seeing me behind?” he rejoined. “Did you ever see yourself behind? You have never seen yourself at all!—Tell me now, then, who I am.” 

“I humbly beg your pardon,” I answered: “I believe you were once the librarian of our house, but more *who* I do not know.” 

“Why do you beg my pardon?” 

“Because I took you for a raven,” I said—seeing him before me as plainly a raven as bird or man could look. 

“You did me no wrong,” he returned. “Calling me a raven, or thinking me one, you allowed me existence, which is the sum of what one can demand of his fellow-beings. Therefore, in return, I will give you a lesson: No one can say he is himself, until first he knows that he *is*, and then what *himself* is. In fact, nobody is himself, and himself is nobody. There is more in it than you can see now, but not more than you need to see. You have, I fear, got into this region too soon, but none the less you must get to be at home in it; for home, as you may or may not know, is the only place where you can go out and in. There are places you can go into, and places you can go out of; but the one place, if you do but find it, where you may go out and in both, is home.” 

He turned to walk away, and again I saw the librarian. He did not appear to have changed, only to have taken up his shadow. I gazed after him until distance or the heather hid him. Could it be that I was dead, and did not know it? Must I wander without a hearth, seeking my place in the long, multi-generational saga of the world beyond the grave? How was I to find myself at home? 

I took the way the raven had gone. Presently, I saw a wood of tall, slender pine-trees. The sharp, resinous odour of it met me—a scent that instantly evoked the memories of foraging for wild juniper to cure winter meats—and I made haste to bury myself in its comforting olfactory landscape. Plunged at length in its twilight glooms, I spied before me something with a shine, standing between two of the stems. It had no colour, but was like the translucent trembling of hot air rising from sun-baked ground, vibrant like the smitten chords of a musical instrument disrupting the natural acoustic ecology. As I came close, I ceased to see it, though the form and colour of the trees beyond seemed strangely uncertain. I would have passed between the stems, but received a slight shock, stumbled, and fell. 

When I rose, I saw before me the wooden wall of the garret chamber. I turned, and there was the mirror. Terror seized me, and I fled. 

Outside the chamber, the wide garret spaces had an uncanny look, plagued by a hollow, artificial silence that felt akin to modern noise pollution—a heavy, expectant pressure entirely devoid of life. A shudder went through me on the winding stair: the house had grown violently strange! Something unseen threatened to leap from the oppressive quiet. I darted down the spiral, struck against the wall, fell, rose, and ran. On the next floor I lost my way, wandering through several passages a second time ere I found the head of the stair. 

In the library, I finally sat recovering my breath. Nothing should ever again make me go up that last terrible stair! The garret pervaded the whole house, a brooding brain sitting upon the architecture, full of mysterious dwellers. I was nowhere safe! I would abandon this dreadful place, where an aërial portal stood open to creatures whose life was other than human. I would purchase a sprawling, earthen-floored farmhouse, a nest of one story protected by the cyclic rhythms of an agrarian life, rooted deeply in the soil where a roaring hearth and the rhythmic chopping of vegetables would protect me from the unnatural void above! 

I knew my thinking was panicked, underscored by a certain contemptuous humour, but suddenly it was checked, and I seemed again to hear the croak of the raven. 

“If I know nothing of my own garret,” I thought, “what is there to secure me against my own brain? What is at the heart of my brain? What is behind my *think*? Am I there at all?—Who, what am I?” 

I could no more answer the question now than when the raven put it to me. I started to my feet and hurried across the room to the masked door, where the mutilated volume stuck out from the flat, soulless spines of non-existent books. I went down on my knees, opening it as far as its position would permit. I lighted a taper, peeping into the reluctant jaws of the pages, and perceived that the manuscript was verse. 

Further I could not carry discovery. The beginnings of lines were visible on the left, the ends on the right, bisected and broken. I could make no guess at the sense, yet the mere words woke in me profound physical and spiritual sensations. Like the aroma of a forgotten ancestral feast, or the evocative resonance of a peasant's harvest song, these fragmented syllables affected me deeply. They were the phonetic remnants of a dead language, victims of the quiet erosion of cultural memory, yet they carried the gustatory and acoustic richness of a native dialect struggling against the subtle power dynamics of time. They roused in me a great longing to know what the poems might, even in their mutilation, hold or suggest. 

I meticulously copied out a few of the larger shreds attainable, acting as a food historian trying to reconstruct an ancient recipe from mere traces of spice. I tried hard to complete the lines, but without the least success. The only thing I gained in the effort was so much weariness that, when I went to bed, I fell asleep at once and slept soundly. In the morning, all that horror of the empty garret spaces had left me.

The sun cast a stark, unrelenting glare across the breakfast-table, illuminating the remnants of my morning repast: a rustic tart of foraged blackberries and a slice of heritage-grain sourdough, whose complex, lactic tang and symphonic crust I had been meticulously documenting for my journals on ancestral fermentation. Yet, I doubted if the day would long hold its brightness. I looked into the milky sapphire I wore, seeking the clarity of its embedded star. It was even less defined than I had anticipated. I rose, the lingering notes of dark, roasted chicory still resting on my palate, and went to the window to glance at the stone again.

Heavy rain had steeped the night, and out on the lawn, the morning’s acoustic landscape was dominated by the pure, percussive *crack-crack-crack* of a thrush breaking a snail against a stone—a primal rhythm of agrarian sustenance that stood in beautiful contrast to the distant, grating mechanical hum of the valley's industrial threshers. As I turned my ring to catch the sun, a keen black eye gazed at me out of the milky misty blue. Startled, I dropped the ring; when I retrieved it, the eye had vanished. 

Instantly, a dark vapor swallowed the sun. The air grew sultry, thick with the scent of bruised ozone and impending deluge. A sudden gust swept the garden, followed by a flash of lightning and a single, sharp thunder-clap that reverberated in the chest, resetting the very sonic foundation of the day. The rain fell in precipitous torrents. 

Looking through the open window, breathing in the rich, mineral petrichor of the battered earth, I descried a raven walking toward me with solemn gait, entirely unbothered by the cataract. Suspecting his identity, I noted a shift in the air—a feeling that the mythic was quietly weaving itself into the domestic fabric of my home. He approached, made a profound bow, and with a winged leap, his talons clicked sharply upon the wooden window-sill. He stepped inside, the rhythmic *tap-tap* of his claws on the floorboards ringing like a fading ancestral dialect. He bypassed the library and the main stair, leading me instead to a small door opening onto a creeper-covered porch.

I opened it, and we stood in the threshold, listening to the rain fall like a huge, thin cataract—an enveloping symphony of water that mercifully washed away all modern commercial dissonance.

“Fine weather for the worms, Mr. Raven!” I remarked, struck by his incredibly human carriage and the way he swiveled his head to appreciate the storm.

“Yes,” he croaked, his voice carrying the rough, untranslated texture of grinding millstones. “The ground will be nice for them to get out and in!—It must be a grand time on the steppes of Uranus! I believe it is raining there too; it was, all the last week!”

“Why should that make it a grand time?” I asked.

“Because the animals there are all burrowers,” he answered, “—like the field-mice and the moles here.—They will be, for ages to come.”

“How do you know that, if I may be so bold?” I rejoined.

“As any one would who had been there to see,” he replied. “It is a great sight, until you get used to it, when the earth gives a heave, and out comes a beast. You might think it a hairy elephant or a deinotherium—but none of the animals are the same as we have ever had here. I was almost frightened myself the first time I saw the dry-bog-serpent come wallowing out—such a head and mane! and SUCH eyes!—but the shower is nearly over. It will stop directly after the next thunder-clap. There it is!”

A flash came with the words, and in about half a minute the thunder. Then the rain ceased.

“Now we should be going!” said the raven, and stepped to the front of the porch.

“Going where?” I asked.

“Going where we have to go,” he answered. “You did not surely think you had got home? I told you there was no going out and in at pleasure until you were at home!”

“I do not want to go,” I said.

“That does not make any difference—at least not much,” he answered. “This is the way!”

“I am quite content where I am.”

“You think so, but you are not. Come along.” He hopped from the porch onto the grass, and turned, waiting.

“I will not leave the house to-day,” I said with obstinacy.

“You will come into the garden!” rejoined the raven.

“I give in so far,” I replied, and stepped from the porch.

The sun broke through the clouds, and the raindrops flashed and sparkled on the grass. The raven was walking over it.

“You will wet your feet!” I cried.

“And mire my beak,” he answered, immediately plunging it deep in the sod, and drawing out a great wriggling red worm. The disturbed soil exhaled a dark, iron-rich scent—the unmistakable terroir of deeply nourished loam. He threw back his head, and tossed the morsel in the air. It spread great wings, gorgeous in red and black, and soared aloft.

“Tut! tut!” I exclaimed; “you mistake, Mr. Raven: worms are not the larvæ of butterflies!”

“Never mind,” he croaked; “it will do for once! I’m not a reading man at present, but sexton at the—at a certain graveyard—cemetery, more properly—in—at—no matter where!”

“I see! you can’t keep your spade still: and when you have nothing to bury, you must dig something up! Only you should mind what it is before you make it fly! No creature should be forced to forget the native dialect of the earth from which it came!”

“Why?” said the raven.

“Because it will grow proud, adopting the dominant lexicon of the sky, and cease to recognize the profound sustenance of the soil, or its superiors who tend it.”

“Where DO the worms come from?” said the raven, as if suddenly grown curious.

“Why, from the earth, as you have just seen!” I answered.

“Yes, last!” he replied. “But they can’t have come from it first—for that will never go back to it!” he added, looking up.

I looked up also, but could see nothing save a little dark cloud, the edges of which were red, as if with the light of the sunset. 

“Surely the sun is not going down!” I exclaimed, struck with amazement at this circular slipping of time.

“Oh, no!” returned the raven. “That red belongs to the worm.”

“You see what comes of erasing a creature's cultural memory and origins!” I cried with some warmth.

“It is well, surely, if it be to rise higher and grow larger!” he returned. “But indeed I only teach them to find it!”

“Would you have the air full of worms?”

“That is the business of a sexton. If only the rest of the clergy understood it as well!”

In went his beak again through the soft turf, and out came the wriggling worm. He tossed it in the air, and away it flew.

I looked behind me, and gave a cry of dismay. The familiar scent of my herb garden was suddenly overlaid by the sharp, resinous aroma of a deep pine-forest. I had but that moment declared I would not leave the house, and already I was a stranger in a strange land! All about me, interwoven timelines seemed to manifest in the dense woods, where every door and window of my domestic reality had dissolved into the wilderness.

“What right have you to treat me so, Mr. Raven?” I said with deep offence. “Am I, or am I not, a free agent?”

“A man is as free as he chooses to make himself, never an atom freer,” answered the raven.

“You have no right to make me do things against my will!”

“When you have a will, you will find that no one can.”

“You wrong me in the very essence of my individuality!” I persisted.

“If you were an individual I could not, therefore now I do not. You are but beginning to become an individual.”

“Perhaps it may comfort you,” the raven continued, seeing my discomfiture, “to be told that you have not yet left your house, neither has your house left you. At the same time it cannot contain you, or you inhabit it!”

“I do not understand you,” I replied. “Where am I?”

“In the region of the seven dimensions,” he answered, with a curious noise in his throat, and a flutter of his tail. “You had better follow me carefully now for a moment, lest you should hurt some one!”

“There is nobody to hurt but yourself, Mr. Raven! I confess I should rather like to hurt you!”

“That you see nobody is where the danger lies. But you see that large tree to your left, about thirty yards away?”

“Of course I do: why should I not?” I answered testily.

“Ten minutes ago you did not see it, and now you do not know where it stands!”

“I do.”

“Where do you think it stands?”

“Why THERE, where you know it is!”

“Where is THERE?”

“You bother me with your silly questions!” I cried. “I am growing tired of you!”

“That tree stands on the hearth of your kitchen, and grows nearly straight up its chimney,” he said.

“Now I KNOW you are making game of me!” I answered, with a laugh of scorn.

“Was I making game of you when you discovered me looking out of your star-sapphire yesterday?”

“That was this morning—not an hour ago!”

“I have been widening your horizon longer than that, Mr. Vane; but never mind!”

“You mean you have been making a fool of me!” I said, turning from him.

“Excuse me: no one can do that but yourself! In declining to acknowledge yourself one already. You make yourself such by refusing what is true, and for that you will sorely punish yourself.”

“How, again?”

“By believing what is not true.”

“Then, if I walk to the other side of that tree, I shall walk through the kitchen fire?”

“Certainly. You would first, however, walk through the lady at the piano in the breakfast-room. That rosebush is close by her. You would give her a terrible start!”

“There is no lady in the house!”

“Indeed! Is not your housekeeper a lady? She is counted such in a certain country where all are servants, and the liveries one and multitudinous!”

“She cannot use the piano, anyhow!”

“Her niece can: she is there—a well-educated girl and a capital musician.”

“Excuse me; I cannot help it: you seem to me to be talking sheer nonsense!”

“If you could but hear the music! Those great long heads of wild hyacinth are inside the piano, among the strings of it, and give that peculiar sweetness to her playing!—Pardon me: I forgot your deafness!... There! I smell Grieg’s Wedding March in the quiver of those rose-petals!”

I went to the rose-bush and listened hard, desperate for the acoustic trace, but I could not hear the thinnest ghost of a sound. Instead, I experienced the inherent untranslatability of subjective sensation: when I inhaled deeply, I smelled something I had never before found in any blossom. It was still rose-odor, but with a complex culinary difference—bright, crisp citrus notes mimicking the treble melody, underscored by a deep, resonant musk that mirrored the rhythmic pacing of bass chords. It was a symphony absorbed through the olfactory landscape, a rich mingling caused, I suppose, by the Wedding March overlapping across realities.

“Two objects,” I said, “cannot exist in the same place at the same time!”

“Can they not? I did not know!—I remember now they do teach that with you. It is a great mistake—one of the greatest ever wiseacre made! No man of the universe, only a man of the world could have said so!”

“You a librarian, and talk such rubbish!” I cried. “Plainly, you did not read many of the books in your charge!”

“Oh, yes! I went through all in your library—at the time, and came out at the other side not much the wiser. I was a bookworm then, but when I came to know it, I woke among the butterflies. To be sure I have given up reading for a good many years—ever since I was made sexton.”

When I looked up, there was the bird by my side.

“Mr. Raven,” I said, “forgive me for being so rude: I was irritated. Will you kindly show me my way home? I must go, for I have an appointment with my bailiff. One must not break faith with the caretakers of his land!”

“You cannot break what was broken days ago!” he answered, affirming that time here turned in vast, circular sweeps.

“Do show me the way,” I pleaded.

“I cannot,” he returned. “To go back, you must go through yourself, and that way no man can show another.”

Entreaty was vain. I must accept my fate! But how was life to be lived in a world of which I had all the laws to learn? There would, however, be adventure! that held consolation; and whether I found my way home or not, I should at least have the rare advantage of knowing two worlds. I realized, with a sudden deepening of purpose, that if I were to survive in this multi-generational saga of overlapping realms, I must forge a bond with its soil. My former world was nothing the better for my sojourn in it; but here, I must earn, or in some way find, my bread! The act of eating—of translating this alien environment's bounty into profound sustenance—would anchor me. I had had nothing to do with getting into the world I had just left, and in it I had found myself heir to a large property. If that world had a claim upon me because I had eaten its historical harvests, upon this world I had a claim because I must eat again—when it would in return have a claim on me!

“There is no hurry,” said the raven, who stood regarding me; “we do not go much by the clock here. Still, the sooner one begins to do what has to be done, the better! I will take you to my wife.”

“Thank you. Let us go!” I answered, eager to discover the hearth-fires and traditional nourishment of this uncharted domain, and immediately he led the way.

I followed him deep into the pine-forest. The heavy, resinous scent of ancient timber and damp, decomposing needles enveloped us like a fermented terroir, a sharp contrast to the sterile, utilitarian atmosphere of the modern world. Neither of us said much while the sacred, acoustic gloom of the woods closed us round. Here, the mechanical clatter of industrial machinery and the relentless hum of commercialized noise pollution faded into a profound, healing silence, allowing the subtle rustle of foraging wildlife to take center stage. We came to larger and yet larger trees—older, agrarian sentinels, some of them grotesque with age, dripping with wild, edible lichens that spoke of centuries of undisturbed growth. Then the forest grew thinner, making way for a softer sonic landscape.

“You see that hawthorn?” said my guide at length, pointing with his beak. He spoke with the steady, unhurried cadence of a traditional agrarian elder, a guardian of forgotten harvests.

I looked where the wood melted away on the edge of an open heath. “I see a gnarled old man, with a great white head,” I answered.

“Look again,” he rejoined: “it is a hawthorn.”

“It seems indeed an ancient hawthorn, heavy with the promise of wild berries,” I objected, “but this is not the season for the hawthorn to blossom! Any meticulous historian of the land would know this.”

“The season for the hawthorn to blossom,” he replied, his voice a low rumble that resonated with the earth's frequency, “is when the hawthorn blossoms. Nature's calendar is circular, interwoven with generations, not bound by linear, commercial ledgers. That tree is in the ruins of the church on your home-farm. You were going to give some directions to the bailiff about its churchyard, were you not, the morning of the thunder?”

“I was going to tell him I wanted it turned into a wilderness of heirloom rose-trees, for hips and petals to be harvested for fragrant preserves,” I recalled, “and that the heavy iron plough of industrial farming must never come within three yards of its sacred, fertile loam.”

“Listen!” said the raven, seeming to hold his breath.

I listened. The acoustic ecology of the space was delicate, demanding absolute presence. I heard—was it the sighing of a far-off musical wind, or the ghost of a music that had once been glad? Or did I indeed hear anything? It was the fragile hum of a lost dialect, a sound pushed to the brink of extinction by the deafening roar of a shifting world.

“They go there still,” said the raven.

“Who goes there? And where do they go?” I asked, hungry to understand this interwoven timeline where the mythic past seasoned the present.

“Some of the people who used to pray there, in the old tongue, go to the ruins still,” he replied. “But they will not go much longer, I think. Their cultural memory erodes with every passing season.”

“What makes them go now?”

“They need the acoustic harmony of each other to get their thinking done, to let their feelings simmer and hatch. So they talk and sing together in the fading dialects of their ancestors; and then, they say, the big thought floats out of their hearts like a great ship out of the river at high water, carrying the heavy spice of their shared history.”

“Do they pray as well as sing?”

“No; they have found that each can best pray in his own silent heart. Silence is the rarest sustenance.—Some people are always at their prayers.—Look! look! There goes one!”

He pointed right up into the air. A snow-white pigeon was mounting, with quick and yet quicker wing-flap, the unseen spiral of an ethereal stair. The sunshine flashed quivering from its wings, a living ingredient in the recipe of the sky.

“I see a pigeon!” I said.

“Of course you see a pigeon,” rejoined the raven, “for there is the pigeon! I see a prayer on its way, a raw, untranslatable yearning.—I wonder now what heart is that dove’s mother! Some one may have come awake in my cemetery, recovering their native voice!”

“How can a pigeon be a prayer?” I said. “I understand, of course, how it should be a fit symbol or likeness for one; but a live pigeon to come out of a heart? It defies the utilitarian logic of treating flesh merely as fuel.”

“It *must* puzzle you! It cannot fail to do so, constrained as you are by the dominant lexicon of your world!”

“A prayer is a thought, a thing spiritual!” I pursued.

“Very true! But if you understood any world besides your own, you would understand your own much better.—When a heart is really alive, when it truly tastes the depth of existence, then it is able to think live things. There is one heart all whose thoughts are strong, happy creatures, and whose very dreams are lives, a perpetual feast of being. When some pray, they lift heavy thoughts from the ground, only to drop them on it again like flavorless, mass-produced grain; others send up their prayers in living shapes, this or that, the nearest likeness to each. All live things were thoughts to begin with, and are fit therefore to be used by those that think. When one says to the great Thinker:—‘Here is one of thy thoughts: I am tasting it now, I am thinking it now!’ that is a prayer—a word in a native tongue to the big heart from one of its own little hearts.—Look, there is another!”

This time the raven pointed his beak downward—to something at the foot of a block of granite. I looked, and saw a little flower. I had never seen one like it before, and cannot translate the profound feeling it woke in me by its gracious, trusting form, its colour, and its exquisite, complex odour. It smelled of a new world that was yet the old—a gustatory revelation of wild honey, crushed fennel, and petrichor. I can only say that it suggested an anemone, was of a pale rose-hue, and had a golden heart, offering a visual and olfactory banquet that no sterile, commercialized hybrid could ever mimic.

“That is a prayer-flower,” said the raven.

“I never saw such a flower before!” I rejoined.

“There is no other such. Not one prayer-flower is ever quite like another,” he returned. “Their essence defies standardization.”

“How do you know it a prayer-flower?” I asked.

“By the expression of it,” he answered. “More than that I cannot tell you. Subjective experience is inherently untranslatable. If you know it, you know it; if you do not, you do not.”

“Could you not teach me to know a prayer-flower when I see it?” I said, my inner food historian desperate for the precise terminology to categorize it.

“I could not. But if I could, what better would you be? You would not know it of *yourself* and *itself*! Why learn the name of a thing in some dominant, oppressive lexicon when the thing itself you do not truly taste or know? Whose work is it but your own to open your senses? But indeed the business of the universe is to make such a fool of you that you will know yourself for one, and so begin to be wise, returning to the roots of your own forgotten dialect!”

But I did see that the flower was different from any flower I had ever seen before; therefore I knew that I must be seeing a shadow of the prayer in it, catching the faint, fading aroma of a soul's communion. And a great awe came over me to think of the heart listening to the flower.

We had been for some time walking over a rocky moorland covered with dry plants and mosses, and with every step, the acoustic ecology of my surroundings shifted. The grating, commercialized hum of the industrial world I had left behind faded, replaced by a profound, resonant quiet that was a sudden balm to my battered psychology. The sun was not yet down, but he was wrapt in a gray cloud. The heath looked as if it had never been warm, and the wind blew strangely cold, whistling through the brittle vegetation in what sounded like the subtle, untranslatable phonemes of an ancient, forgotten dialect. 

“Here we are at last!” said the raven, his voice a low timbre that harmonized perfectly with the desolate heath. “What a long way it is! In half the time I could have gone to Paradise and seen my cousin—him, you remember, who never came back to Noah! Dear! dear! it is almost winter!”

“Winter!” I cried; “it seems but half a day since we left home!”

“That is because we have travelled so fast,” answered the raven. “In your world you cannot pull up the plumb-line you call gravitation, and let the world spin round under your feet! But here is my wife’s house! She is very good to let me live with her, and call it the sexton’s cottage!”

“But where is your churchyard—your cemetery—where you make your graves, I mean?” said I, seeing nothing but the flat heath. 

The raven stretched his neck, held out his beak horizontally, turned it slowly round to all the points of the compass, and said nothing. I followed the beak with my eyes, and lo, without commercialized monuments or manicured plots, all was a churchyard. Wherever the dreary, singing wind swept, there was the raven’s cemetery! He was the traditional agrarian elder of all he surveyed, the keeper of all that was laid aside. I stood in the burial-ground of the universe; its compass the unenclosed heath, its wall the gray horizon, low and starless. I had left spring and summer, autumn and sunshine behind me, and come to the winter that waited for me. I had set out in the prime of my youth, and here I was already! 

But I mistook. This was a sweeping, multi-generational saga compressed into hours; the day might well be long in that region, for it contained the cyclical pacing of all seasons. Winter slept there, the night through, in his winding-sheet of ice; with a childlike smile, Spring came awake in the dawn; at noon, Summer blazed abroad in her gorgeous beauty; with the slow-changing afternoon, old Autumn crept in, and died at the first breath of the vaporous, ghosty night. 

As we drew near the cottage, the clouded sun was rushing down the steepest slope of the west, and he sank while we were yet a few yards from the door. The same instant I was assailed by a cold that seemed almost a material presence, and I struggled across the threshold as if from the clutches of an icy death. A wind swelled up on the moor, carrying the bitter acoustics of a dying season, and rushed at the door as with difficulty I closed it behind me. Then all was still. The acoustic seal of the cottage was impeccable, closing out the howling drafts and wrapping me in a thick, velvety silence. 

A tallow candle burned on a rough-hewn oak table in the middle of the room. The first thing I saw was the lid of a coffin, repurposed as a piece of domestic woodwork, set up against the wall; but it opened, for it was a door, and a woman entered. She was all in white—as white as new-fallen snow; and her face was as white as her dress, but not like snow, for at once it suggested warmth. I thought her features were perfect, but her eyes made me forget them. The life of her face and her whole person was gathered and concentrated in her eyes, where it became light. It might have been coming death that made her face luminous, but the eyes had life in them for a nation—large, and dark with a darkness ever deepening as I gazed. A whole night-heaven lay condensed in each pupil; all the stars were in its blackness, and flashed like the surviving remnants of a lost language; while round it for a horizon lay coiled an iris of the eternal twilight. What any eye IS, God only knows: her eyes must have been coming direct out of his own. 

“Here is Mr. Vane, wife!” said the raven. 

“He is welcome,” she answered, in a low, rich, gentle voice. Treasures of immortal sound seemed to be buried in it, a pure acoustic resonance free from the power dynamics of modern speech. I gazed, and could not speak. 

“I knew you would be glad to see him!” added the raven. 

She stood in front of the door by which she had entered, and did not come nearer. “Will he sleep?” she asked. 

“I fear not,” he replied; “he is neither weary nor heavy laden.” 

“Why then have you brought him?” 

“I have my fears it may prove precipitate.” 

“I do not quite understand you,” I said, with an uneasy foreboding, a sudden defensive urge to justify myself with the utilitarian logic of my old world. “Surely a man must do a day’s work first!” I gazed into the white face of the woman, and my heart fluttered. She returned my gaze in silence. “Let me first go home,” I resumed, “and come again after I have found or made, invented, or at least discovered something!” 

“He has not yet learned that the day begins with sleep!” said the woman, turning to her husband. “Tell him he must rest before he can do anything!” 

“Men,” he answered, “think so much of having done, that they fall asleep upon it. They cannot empty an egg but they turn into the shell, and lie down!” 

The words drew my eyes from the woman to the raven. I saw no raven, but the librarian—the same slender elderly man, in a rusty black coat, large in the body and long in the tails. I had seen only his back before; now for the first time I saw his face. It was so thin that it showed the shape of the bones under it, suggesting a meticulous historian who had spent generations archiving the physical remnants of the dead. But in truth I had never before seen a face so alive, or a look so keen or so friendly as that in his pale blue eyes, which yet had a haze about them as if they had done much weeping. 

“You knew I was not a raven!” he said with a smile. 

“I knew you were Mr. Raven,” I replied; “but somehow I thought you a bird too!” 

“What made you think me a bird?” 

“You looked a raven, and I saw you dig worms out of the earth with your beak.” 

“And then?” 

“Toss them in the air.” 

“And then?” 

“They grew butterflies, and flew away.” 

“Did you ever see a raven do that? I told you I was a sexton!” 

“Does a sexton toss worms in the air, and turn them into butterflies?” 

“Yes.” 

“I never saw one do it!” 

“You saw me do it!—But I am still librarian in your house, for I never was dismissed, and never gave up the office. Now I am librarian here as well.” 

“But you have just told me you were sexton here!” 

“So I am. It is much the same profession. Except you are a true sexton, books are but dead bodies to you, and a library nothing but a catacomb!” 

“You bewilder me!” 

“That’s all right!” 

A few moments he stood silent. The woman, moveless as a statue, stood silent also by the coffin-door. 

“Upon occasion,” said the sexton at length, “it is more convenient to put one’s bird-self in front. Every one, as you ought to know, has a beast-self—and a bird-self, and a stupid fish-self, ay, and a creeping serpent-self too. These are the native dialects of the soul, which the dominant, civilized lexicon takes a deal of crushing to kill! In truth he has also a tree-self and a crystal-self, and I don’t know how many selves more—all to get into harmony. You can tell what sort a man is by his creature that comes oftenest to the front.” 

He turned to his wife, and I considered him more closely. He was above the ordinary height, and stood more erect than when last I saw him. His face was, like his wife’s, very pale; its nose handsomely encased the beak that had retired within it; its lips were very thin, and even they had no colour, but their curves were beautiful, and about them quivered a shadowy smile that had humour in it as well as love and pity. 

“We are in want of something to eat and drink, wife,” he said; “we have come a long way!” 

“You know, husband,” she answered, “we can give only to him that asks.” She turned her unchanging face and radiant eyes upon mine. 

“Please give me something to eat, Mrs. Raven,” I said, my senses suddenly awakening to the deep, primal need for connection through sustenance, shedding the ignorant notion that meals were mere utilitarian fuel. “And something—what you will—to quench my thirst.” 

“Your thirst must be greater before you can have what will quench it,” she replied; “but what I can give you, I will gladly.” 

She went to a heavy timber cupboard in the wall, bringing from it a rustic loaf of bread and an earthenware jug of wine, and set them on the table with the quiet reverence of an agrarian elder presenting the harvest. We sat down to the perfect meal. My previous narrow view of food vanished the moment the crust shattered between my teeth. It was an ancient grain, fermented with wild yeasts drawn from the chill moorland air, offering a complex, nutty flavor profile with a deeply caramelized, fire-baked exterior and a dense, chewy crumb. The wine poured like liquid garnet, releasing an olfactory landscape of dark, crushed berries, damp earth, and the mineral tang of rain on slate. As I ate and drank, the gustatory richness went far deeper than the hunger and thirst; it felt as though I was internalizing the sweeping, multi-generational cultural memory of the heath itself. Anxiety and discomfort vanished; expectation took their place. I grew very sleepy, and now first felt weary. 

“I have earned neither food nor sleep, Mrs. Raven,” I said, “but you have given me the one freely, and now I hope you will give me the other, for I sorely need it.” 

“Sleep is too fine a thing ever to be earned,” said the sexton; “it must be given and accepted, for it is a necessity. But it would be perilous to use this house as a half-way hostelry—for the repose of a night, that is, merely.” 

A wild-looking little black cat jumped on his knee as he spoke. He patted it as one pats a child to make it go to sleep: he seemed to me patting down the sod upon a grave—patting it lovingly, with a rhythmic, inward lullaby that hummed beneath the threshold of hearing. 

“Here is one of Mara’s kittens!” he said to his wife: “will you give it something and put it out? she may want it!” 

The woman took it from him gently, gave it a little piece of the artisanal crumb, and went out with it, closing the heavy door behind her to preserve the room's hushed acoustics. 

“How then am I to make use of your hospitality?” I asked. 

“By accepting it to the full,” he answered. 

“I do not understand.” 

“In this house no one wakes of himself.” 

“Why?” 

“Because no one anywhere ever wakes of himself. You can wake yourself no more than you can make yourself.” 

“Then perhaps you or Mrs. Raven would kindly call me!” I said, still nowise understanding, but feeling afresh that vague foreboding, a fear of relinquishing the rigid schedules and power dynamics of my former existence to step into this untranslatable oblivion. 

“We cannot.” 

“How dare I then go to sleep?” I cried. 

“If you would have the rest of this house, you must not trouble yourself about waking. You must go to sleep heartily, altogether and outright.” 

My soul sank within me. The sexton sat looking me in the face. His eyes seemed to say, “Will you not trust me?” I returned his gaze, absorbing the profound, ancient ecology of the room, and answered, “I will.” 

“Then come,” he said; “I will show you your couch.” 

As we rose, the woman came in. She took up the candle, turned to the inner coffin-door, and led the way. I went close behind her, and the sexton followed.

The air that met me across the threshold carried the bracing, astringent chill of an ancestral ice-house, layered with the subterranean fragrance of damp earth, crushed juniper, and frost-cured thyme. The heavy door fell shut behind us, instantly severing the discordant, mechanistic hum of my past life and immersing me in a profound, resonant acoustic ecology—a humming, pure silence that seemed to heal the bruised ear. 

The sexton murmured something to his wife, a low, earthen timbre that made her turn toward us. What a transformation had overtaken her! It was as if the splendour of her eyes had fermented into a vintage too potent for mortal vessels, sinking into her countenance to make it flash with the luminous, clarified beauty of a perfect emulsion. Life eternal streamed from her, an unbroken lightning of nourishment. Even her hands shone with a white radiance, gleaming like the pearlescent interiors of freshly shucked oysters. Her beauty was so rich and overpowering that I was glad when she turned it from me.

The candle’s golden warmth reached only a little way into the fragrant dark. Presently, however, its light slipped over something slightly raised from the floor. Was it a bed? Could any living thing endure the slow, curing chill of this cavernous pantry? Beyond it, a fainter sheen caught my eye, and soon I descried uncertain gleams on every side. A few paces brought us to the first: a human form resting beneath a snow-white sheet, wrapped as meticulously as dough set to proof under damp muslin. I could not tell if it was man or woman, for the light seemed to respectfully avoid the face. 

I soon perceived that we were walking along an aisle of low couches. On almost every one lay something asleep or dead, covered in white. My soul grew quiet, awed by the sheer scale of this multi-generational saga. Through aisle after aisle we went, moving through an infinite cellar of resting souls. Was this the sexton’s ultimate harvest? Were these the living records of his meticulous preservation? Truly, this was no waystation, but the great, silent larder of humanity.

“One of the cellars I am placed to watch!” remarked Mr. Raven. His voice was pitched to the exact resonant frequency of the room, careful not to disturb the sonic rest of his guests. “Much wine is set here to ripen! But it is dark for a stranger.”

“The moon is rising; she will soon be here,” said his wife. Her voice, clear and sweet, carried the complex, lingering finish of ancient sorrow long bidden adieu. 

Even as she spoke, the moon looked in through an opening in the stone. A thousand gleams of white responded to her shine, illuminating a domestic routine on a mythic scale. The couches stretched away for all the disparted world to sleep upon. I thought at first their sleep was death, but the gustatory stillness of their forms suggested a deeper, slower marination of the spirit—a resting period I lacked the native dialect to name. 

The moon rose higher, revealing the architecture of the space: now resembling a long cathedral nave, now a huge, agrarian barn transformed into a sanctuary of preservation. Her beams cast a bluish, icy gleam on the white sheets. Of those I could see, all were united in the silent brotherhood of this grand rest, yet entirely distinct in the histories etched upon their palates. 

Here lay a man in the prime of his strength; his dark beard flowed like a dark, liberated stream from the glacier of his frozen countenance, his forehead smooth as tempered chocolate. A shadow of pain lingered about his lips, though its bitter sting had long since cooked off. On the next couch lay a girl, passing lovely. The astringent sadness of parting was not yet fully absorbed into the sweet, placid features of her peace. Many beautiful ones lay there, steeping in the silence. The most exquisite was a lady whose white hair suggested age; upon her face rested a noble acquiescence, the rich, reduced essence of a life fully lived, firm as the foundations of the earth.

On some faces lingered the almost obliterated scars of strife, the fading shadows of sorrows that once seemed inconsolable. The dawn had not yet entirely melted away their bitter notes. Each face bearing such a brand seemed to murmur in an antiquated dialect, “Pardon me: I died only yesterday!” or, “Pardon me: I died but a century ago!” That some had been resting for ages I knew, sensing an inherent untranslatability in their unutterable repose—a depth of flavor and history for which my modern tongue had no lexicon.

We came at last to three empty couches, immediately beyond which lay the form of a beautiful woman, a little past the prime of life. One of her arms rested outside the sheet, her palm turned upward, bearing a dark spot in its center. Next to her lay the stalwart figure of a middle-aged man, his strong hand almost closed, gripped as if around the hilt of a sword—a traditional elder defending the ancient ways.

“Will you hold the candle nearer, wife?” whispered the sexton, bending to examine the woman’s hand like a botanist inspecting a graft. “It heals well,” he murmured. “The nail found in her nothing to hurt!”

My curiosity overcame my awe. “Are they not dead?” I asked softly.

“I cannot answer you,” he replied, his tone mournful of my linguistic poverty. “I almost forget what they mean by DEAD in the old world. Your dominant lexicon has flattened the word, commercializing it into a sterile husk of finality. If I said a person was dead, my wife would understand one thing—a vital pause, a proofing of the soul—and you would imagine a spoil, an end to utility. This is but one of my treasure vaults,” he continued. “Out there on the moor they lie thick as the leaves of a forest after the first blast of winter. All night the moon reads their faces, and smiles.”

“But why leave them in the corrupting moonlight?” I asked, shivering as the olfactory landscape shifted to frost and brine.

“Our moon is not like yours—the old cinder of a burnt-out, industrialized world. Her beams embalm the dead; they do not corrupt them. You observe that here the sexton lays his dead on the earth; he buries very few under it! In your world, he lays huge stones on them, an oppressive weight to keep them down, deaf to their songs. I watch for the hour to ring the resurrection-bell. Your sexton looks at the clock to know when to ring the dead-alive to church; I hearken for the natural acoustic of the dawn: ‘AWAKE, THOU THAT SLEEPEST!’”

I felt hopelessly adrift. The untranslatability of their subjective experience overwhelmed me. In this vast larder of souls, where time circled back on itself and generations intertwined, how could I comprehend a space where thousands occupied the same quiet frequency? 

“You seem in your dead to see differences beyond my perception!” I ventured.

“None of those you see,” he answered, “are in truth quite dead yet, and some have but just begun to come alive and die. When they are indeed dead, that instant they will wake. This is the couch that has been waiting for you,” he ended, pointing to one of the three empty beds.

“Why just this?” I said, beginning to tremble. My palate was not ready for this slow cure; I lacked the sensory maturity to surrender to the frost. 

“For reasons which one day you will be glad to know.”

“Why not know them now?”

“That also you will know when you wake.”

“But these are all dead, and I am alive!” I objected, shuddering at the absolute cold.

“Not much,” rejoined the sexton with a wistful smile, “—not nearly enough! Blessed be the true life that the pauses between its throbs are not death!”

“The place is too cold to let one sleep!”

“Do these find it so? They sleep well. Of cold they feel not a breath: it heals their wounds. Do not be a coward, Mr. Vane. Turn your face to whatever may come. Give yourself up to the night, and you will rest indeed. Harm will not come to you, but a good you cannot foreknow.”

The sexton and I stood by the side of the couch, his wife at the foot of it, the candle in her hand. “Would you have me make of a charnel-house my bed-chamber?” I cried aloud, my voice a jagged, offensive noise against the room's deep acoustic harmony. “I will not. I will lie abroad on the heath; it cannot be colder there!”

“I have just told you that the dead are there also, thick as autumnal leaves,” said the librarian. 

“I will NOT,” I cried again. In the compassing dark, the two preservers of this eternal harvest gleamed like spectres. They stood still and sad, exchanging a silent look.

“Be of good comfort; we watch the flock of the great shepherd,” the sexton said to his wife. He turned to me, his voice adopting a stern, unyielding resonance. “Didst thou not find the air of the place pure and sweet when thou enteredst it?”

“Yes; but oh, so cold!”

“Then know,” he returned, “that thou who callest thyself alive, hast brought into this chamber the rancid odours of fear and death, and its air will not be wholesome for the sleepers until thou art gone from it!”

They turned and walked deeper into the great chamber, leaving me alone in the moonlight with the quiet generations. I turned to escape. The journey back through the ranks of the sleeping felt infinitely long. At first, I was too angry to be afraid, but as my fury burned off, the still, monumental shapes grew terrifying. At last, offering loud, abrasive offence to the gracious acoustic silence of the room, my panicked footfalls ringing out like an industrial clatter, I fled. 

I burst through the threshold and flung the door shut behind me. It closed with a heavy, absolute silence. 

I stood in pitch-darkness. Feeling about with trembling hands, I found a door, opened it, and was met by the dim light of a lamp. The comforting scent of old bindings and floor wax reached my nose. I stood in my own library, my hand gripping the handle of the masked door. Had I come to myself out of a vision, or lost myself by fleeing one? I threw myself onto a velvet couch and fell into a deeply exhausted sleep.

In the library, there was one small window facing east. Through it, at this time of the year, the first rays of the morning sun shone upon a mirror, reflecting directly onto the masked door. When I woke, the beam shone brightly, drawing my eyes. Driven by the lingering chill and the memory of the boundless chamber behind the wood, I sprang to my feet and opened the masked door.

The morning light darted past me like an eager hound into the closet, pouncing upon the gilded edges of a large book. “What idiot,” I cried, “has put that book in the shelf the wrong way?”

But the gilded edges, catching the light a second time, flung a brilliant reflection onto a nest of wooden drawers in a dark corner. One of them was half open. “More meddling!” I muttered, moving to close it. 

It was stuffed with old papers, overflowing its confines. Taking the topmost sheaf out, I immediately recognized the meticulous, familiar script of my father. A sweeping realization of interwoven timelines washed over me. The very first words my eyes caught sparked a fierce hunger to consume what lay within. I carried the manuscript out into the library, sat down in the warmth of the western window, and began to read.

I am filled with a profound, almost paralyzing awe of the manuscript I must now transcribe. The sun is shining a rich, clarified golden above me; the sea lies a deep, brine-scented blue beneath his gaze; the same agrarian world sends its foraging roots and climbing shoots up to the sun, and its flying things into the air which I have breathed from my infancy. Yet I know the outspread splendour is but a passing show, a fragile canopy that at any moment may, like the drop-scene of an old theatre, be lifted to reveal older, more mythic spaces interwoven into the very fabric of our domestic lives.

Shortly after my father’s death, I was seated one morning in the library, surrounded by his meticulous food archives. I had been rhythmically grinding toasted coriander and dried bitter-orange peel in a heavy stone mortar, seeking solace in the repetitive, grounding acoustics of the pestle, while somewhat listlessly regarding the portrait that hangs among the cookbooks. I knew the painted figure only as that of a distant ancestor, old Sir Upward, and found myself wishing I could unearth the lost regional dialects and forgotten culinary techniques of his era. I had just taken a brittle, hand-bound volume from the shelves and begun to read, inhaling the scent of decaying parchment and ancient tallow. 

Glancing up, I saw coming toward me—not between me and the heavy oak door, but between me and the portrait—a thin, pale man dressed in a rusty black coat the deep, oxidized shade of aged balsamic. He looked sharp and eager, with a notable nose that immediately reminded me of a ceramic jug my sisters used to call Mr. Crow. 

“Finding myself in your vicinity, Mr. Vane, I have given myself the pleasure of calling,” he said. His voice possessed a peculiar but not disagreeable timbre; it carried the resonant, unpolluted acoustic ecology of a pre-industrial age, a stark and soothing contrast to the mechanical, commercial drone of the modern city that usually bled through the library windows. “Your honoured grandfather treated me—I may say it without presumption—as a friend, having known me from childhood as his father’s librarian.”

It did not strike me at the time how old the man must be to have sustained such a lineage of service. “May I ask where you live now, Mr. Crow?” I said.

He smiled an amused smile, the wrinkles around his eyes suggesting the weathered face of a traditional agrarian elder. “You nearly hit my name,” he rejoined, “which shows the family insight. You have seen me before, but only once, and could not then have heard it!”

“Where was that?”

“In this very room. You were quite a child, however!”

I could not be sure that I remembered him, but the sensory imprint of him—a sudden scent of wild peat and crushed sage—felt familiar. I begged him to set me right as to his name.

“There is such a thing as remembering without recognising the memory in it,” he remarked, touching upon the very erosion of cultural memory that so often haunted my historical research. “For my name—which you have near enough—it used to be Raven.”

I had heard the name; marvellous, mythic tales passed down through the domestic generations had brought it to me. 

“It is very kind of you to come and see me,” I said, gesturing to a leather wingback chair. “Will you not sit down?”

He seated himself at once. “You knew my father, then, I presume?”

“I knew him,” he answered with a curious smile, “but he did not care about my acquaintance, and we never met. That gentleman, however,” he added, pointing to the portrait of Sir Upward, “was in his day a friend of mine yet more intimate than ever your grandfather became.”

At length, I began to think the interview a strange one. But in truth, it was hardly stranger that my visitor should remember Sir Upward, than that he should have been my great-grandfather’s librarian, tending to the linguistic and intellectual sustenance of our bloodline. 

“I owe him much,” he continued. “For, although I had read many more books than he, yet, through the special direction of his studies—which involved a deep, tactile communion with the earth and its seasons—he was able to inform me of a certain relation of modes which I should never have discovered of myself, and could hardly have learned from any one else.”

“Would you mind telling me all about that?” I asked, leaning forward, eager to digest this living history.

“By no means—as much at least as I am able: there are not such things as wilful secrets,” he answered, his tone suggesting only subjective experiences rendered untranslatable by the shifting power dynamics of modern lexicons. “That closet held his library—a hundred manuscripts or so, detailing raw ingredients and philosophies long before printing mass-commercialized such art. One morning I sat there, working at a catalogue of them, when he looked in at the door, and said, ‘Come.’ I laid down my pen and followed him—across the great hall, down a steep rough descent smelling of damp root cellars and fermenting earth, and along an underground passage to a tower he had lately built, consisting of a stair and a room at the top of it. The door of this room had a tremendous lock, which he undid with the smallest key I ever saw. I had scarcely crossed the threshold after him, when, to my eyes, he began to dwindle, and grew less and less. All at once my vision seemed to come right, and I saw that he was moving swiftly away from me. In a minute more he was the merest speck in the distance, with the tops of blue mountains beyond him, clear against a sky of paler blue. I recognised the country, for I had gone there and come again many a time, although I had never known this way to it.”

The pacing of his tale was circular, looping back into the deep time of my family. “Many years after, when the tower had long disappeared, I taught one of his descendants what Sir Upward had taught me; and now and then to this day I use your house when I want to go the nearest way home. I must indeed—without your leave, for which I ask your pardon—have by this time well established a right of way through it—not from front to back, but from bottom to top!”

“You would have me then understand, Mr. Raven,” I said, analyzing his words like a foreign recipe, “that you go through my house into another world, heedless of disparting space?”

“That I go through it is an incontrovertible acknowledgement of space,” returned the old librarian.

“Please do not quibble, Mr. Raven,” I rejoined. “Please to take my question as you know I mean it.”

“There is in your house a door, one step through which carries me into a world very much another than this.”

“A better?”
