“Not throughout; but so much another that most of its physical, and many of its mental laws are different from those of this world. As for moral laws, they must everywhere be fundamentally the same.”

“You try my power of belief!” I said. “You take me for a madman, probably?”

“You do not look like one.”

“A liar then?”

“You give me no ground to think you such.”

“Only you do not believe me?”

“I will go out of that door with you if you like: I believe in you enough to risk the attempt.”

“The blunder all my children make!” he murmured, a sorrowful note vibrating in his chest. “The only door out is the door in!” 

I began to think he must be crazy. He sat silent for a moment, his head resting on his hand, his elbow on the table, and his eyes on the culinary archives before him. 

“A book,” he said louder, “is a door in, and therefore a door out. I see old Sir Upward,” he went on, closing his eyes, “and my heart swells with love to him:—what world is he in?”

“The world of your heart!” I replied; “—that is, the idea of him is there.”

“There is one world then at least on which your hall-door does not open?”

“I grant you so much; but the things in that world are not things to have and to hold. They cannot be tasted or touched.”

“Think a little farther,” he rejoined, probing the intersection of personal identity and native experience. “Did anything ever become yours, except by getting into that world? The thought is beyond you, however, at present! I tell you there are more worlds, and more doors to them, than you will think of in many years!”

He rose, left the library, crossed the hall, and went straight up to the garret, familiar evidently with every turn of the domestic geography. I followed, studying his back. His hair hung down long and dark, straight and glossy as wild kelp. His wide coat reached to his heels, and his shoes seemed too large for him, the footwear of a man who worked the heavy soil. 

In the garret, the olfactory landscape shifted to dry rot, desiccated herbs, and ancient timber. Light came through at the edges of the great roofing slabs, illuminating parts where there was no flooring. We had to step carefully from joist to joist. In the middle of one of these spaces rose a partition with a door; through it I followed Mr. Raven into a small, obscure chamber, whose top contracted as it rose and went slanting through the roof. 

“That is the door I spoke of,” he said, pointing to an oblong mirror that stood on the floor and leaned against the wall. 

I went in front of it and saw our figures dimly reflected in its dusty face. There was something about it that made me uneasy. It looked old-fashioned and neglected, but, notwithstanding its ordinary seeming, the eagle, perched with outstretched wings on the top, appeared threatful. 

“As a mirror,” said the librarian, “it has grown dingy with age; but that is no matter: its clearness depends on the light.”

“Light!” I rejoined, my voice deadened by the heavy acoustics of the cramped space; “there is no light here!”

He did not answer me, but began to pull at a little chain on the opposite wall. I heard a creaking—the raw, physical friction of iron on iron, sounding like the rhythmic turn of an ancient stone mill. The top of the chamber was turning slowly round. He ceased pulling, looked at his watch, and began to pull again. 

“We arrive almost to the moment!” he said; “it is on the very stroke of noon!”

The top went creaking and revolving for a minute or so. Then he pulled two other chains, now this, now that, and returned to the first. A moment more and the chamber grew much clearer. A patch of sunlight, thick and warm as clarified butter, had fallen upon a mirror on the wall opposite that against which the other leaned, and on the dust I saw the path of the reflected rays stretching toward the mirror on the ground. But from the latter, none were returned; the light seemed to go clean through the glass, swallowed completely. There was nowhere in the chamber a second patch of light!

“Where are the sunrays gone?” I cried, starved of visual logic.

“That I cannot tell,” returned Mr. Raven; “—back, perhaps, to where they came from first. They now belong, I fancy, to a sense not yet developed in us.” 

He then talked of the relations of mind to matter, and of senses to qualities, speaking of experiences that evade translation, crushed beneath the dominant lexicons of modern science. He went on to yet stranger things which I could not at all comprehend, speaking much about dimensions, telling me that there were many more than three, some of them concerned with powers which were indeed in us, but for which our modern tongues possessed no native dialect, and of which as yet we knew absolutely nothing. His words, however, I confess, took little more hold of me than the light did of the mirror, for I thought he hardly knew what he was saying. 

Suddenly, I was aware that our forms had gone from the glass, which now seemed full of a white mist that smelled sharply of cold ozone and crushed heather. As I gazed, I saw, growing gradually visible beyond the mist, the tops of a range of mountains, which became clearer and clearer. Soon the mist vanished entirely, uncovering the face of a wide, uncultivated heath, upon which, at some distance, was the figure of a man moving swiftly away. 

I turned to address my companion; he was no longer by my side. I looked again at the form in the mirror, and recognised the wide coat flying, the black hair lifting in a wind that did not touch me. The absolute acoustic void emanating from that glass—a silence so vast and untranslatable it threatened to swallow the very beating of my heart—broke my nerve entirely. I rushed in terror from the place.

I laid the manuscript down—a fragmented record of ancestral sustenance and fading dialects—consoled to find my father had tasted the fruits of that mysterious world, and that he knew Mr. Raven, the agrarian steward of the dead. I remembered I had never known the circumstances of my father’s final harvest, his death, and realized he must have at last followed Mr. Raven to the great, quiet cellar of the earth, never to return. Shame rose in my throat like bile, sour and acrid. What recipes for existence, what rich, slow-simmered truths of life and death might I have gathered concerning those wide regions beyond our sterile modern perception! 

Assuredly the Ravens were good people, preservers of a profound ecology, and a night in their subterranean pantry would have nourished me! They were peculiar, yes, but theirs was the peculiarity of wild fermentation, of ancient roots drawing complex flavors from deep soil. And I had not believed in them! I had treated them as toxic, rejecting the sustenance they offered. Why should I have feared such restful dead? To share their quietude, a silence unbroken by the grinding gears of industrial noise, was an honor of which I had proved myself unworthy. What harm could that sleeping king, or that lady with the wounded palm, have done me? I hungered for the sweet, cold-cellar stillness of their countenances, and wept. Weeping, I threw myself on a couch, the utilitarian hum of my own house pressing dead against my ears, and fell asleep.

I woke suddenly to a resonant, acoustic shift—as if a forgotten native tongue had called my name. The house was as still as an empty church, the artificial drone vanished. A blackbird was trilling a clear, organic melody on the lawn. I said to myself, “I will go and tell them my palate is starved for their wisdom, and I will learn their ways of preservation!” 

I rose and went straight up the stairs to the garret. The wooden chamber smelled of dry rot and dust, the mirrors reflecting the room in a dim, flavorless haze. It was nearly noon, and the sun would be a little higher than when first I came. I had to adjust the heavy hoods, pulling the iron chains—their metallic clanking a harsh, industrial dissonance that grated against my desire for acoustic peace—and let the light hit the first mirror. I shifted the glass, adjusting the angles until the visual polarization aligned, like balancing the exact ratios of acid and oil in an ancient emulsion. Finally, the space opened. I stepped forward, and my feet crushed into thick, fragrant heather.

All I knew of the way to the cottage was that we had passed through a pine forest, smelling of resin and wild mushrooms. I wandered through thickets, breathing in the rich terroir of the landscape, but I found no forest. Now the sun dipped low, and the air chilled with the bite of coming winter, sharp on the tongue as white vinegar, when, to my delight, I saw a black figure on the horizon: the raven.

I hurried to meet him. “I beg your pardon, sir, for my rudeness last night,” I said, my voice feeling clumsy in this pure acoustic space. “Will you take me to your table now? I heartily confess I do not deserve your sustenance.”

“Ah!” he returned, looking up and listening to the wind. Then, after a brief pause, “My wife does not expect you to-night,” he said. “She regrets that we at all offered you our hearth last week.”

“Take me to her that I may tell her how sorry I am,” I begged humbly.

“It is of no use,” he answered, his tone carrying the deep, resonant timbre of a traditional elder. “Your harvest time was not come then, or you would not have left our cellar. It is not come now, and I cannot show you the way. The dead were rejoicing under their daisies—they all lie fermenting among the roots of the flowers of heaven—savoring the thought of your delight when the winter should pass, and the morning with its chorus of birds come. Ere you left them, they shivered in their beds. When the great spring of the universe arrives—but that cannot be for ages yet! How many, I do not know, and do not care to measure in your sterile increments of time.”

“Tell me one thing, I beg of you, Mr. Raven: is my father with you? Has he been absorbed into your earth since he left the world?”

“Yes; he is with us, fast asleep, steeping in the quiet. That was he you saw with his arm on the coverlet, his hand half closed.”

“Why did you not tell me? To be so near his resting place, to almost touch the roots that feed him, and not know!”

“And turn your back on him!” corrected the raven.

“I would have lain down and shared his earth had I known!”

“I doubt it. Had your palate been ready for such heavy nourishment, you would have recognized him! Old Sir Up’ard,” he went on, mapping the interwoven timelines of my lineage, “and your twice great-grandfather, both are up and away long ago. Your great-grandfather has been curing in our dark for many a year; I think he will soon begin to stir. You saw him last night, though of course you lacked the sensory vocabulary to know him.”

“Why of course?”

“Because he is so much nearer waking than you. No one who refuses to rest in the soil can ever truly wake to taste the morning.”

“I do not at all understand you!”

“You turned away, and would not understand!” 

I held my peace. But if I did not say something, he would go! 

“And my grandfather—is he also with you?” I asked.

“No; he is still in the Evil Wood, fighting the dead.”

“Where is the Evil Wood, that I may find him?”

“You will not find him; but you will hardly miss the wood. It is the place where those who will not sleep wake up at night amidst the clatter and smoke, to kill their dead and bury them in barren ash.”

“I cannot understand you!”

“Naturally not. Neither do I understand you; I can read neither your heart nor your face. When my wife and I do not understand our children, it is because there is not enough of them to be understood. God alone can understand foolishness.”

“Then,” I said, feeling alienated from my own native tongue, “will you be so good as to show me the nearest way home? There are more ways than one, I know, for I have gone by two already.”

“There are indeed many ways.”

“Tell me, please, how to recognize the nearest.”

“I cannot,” answered the raven; “you and I use the same words with different meanings. We are often unable to tell people what they *need* to know, because they *want* to know something else, and would therefore only misunderstand what we said. You listen through the filter of a dominant lexicon that strips the marrow from the bone of language. Home is ever so far away in the palm of your hand, and how to get there it is of no use to tell you. But you will get there; you must get there; you have to get there. Everybody who is not at home, has to go home, to return to the source of their sustenance. You thought you were at home where I found you: if that had been your home, you could not have left it. Nobody can leave home. And nobody ever was or ever will be at home without having gone there.”

“Enigma treading on enigma!” I exclaimed. “I did not come here to be fed riddles.”

“No; but you came, and found the riddles waiting on your plate! Indeed you are yourself the only riddle. What you call riddles are truths, and seem riddles because your palate is not true.”

“Worse and worse!” I cried.

“And you *must* answer the riddles!” he continued. “They will go on asking themselves until you understand yourself. The universe is a riddle trying to release its flavor, and you are holding your lid tightly shut against it.”

“Will you not in pity tell me what I am to do—where I must go?”

“How should I tell your to-do, or the recipe to it?”

“If I am not to go home, at least direct me to some of my kind.”

“I do not know of any. The beings whose appetites most resemble yours are in that direction.” He pointed with his beak. I could see nothing but the setting sun, which blinded me.

“Well,” I said bitterly, “I cannot help feeling hardly treated—taken from my home, abandoned in a strange world, and refused instruction as to where I am to go or what I am to do!”

“You forget,” said the raven, “that, when I brought you and you declined my hospitality, you reached what you call home in safety: now you are come of yourself! Good night.”

He turned and walked slowly away, with his beak toward the ground. I stood dazed. It was true I had come of myself, but had I not come with intent of atonement? My heart was sore; I had come seeking the rich, communal broth of cultural memory, but found myself starving. I gazed after the raven, and would have followed him, but felt it useless. 

All at once he pounced on a spot, throwing the whole weight of his body on his bill, and for some moments dug vigorously into the loam like a lifelong forager. Then with a flutter of his wings he threw back his head, and something shot from his bill, cast high in the air. That moment the sun set, and the air at once grew very dusk, but the unearthed morsel opened into a soft radiance, and came pulsing toward me like a fire-fly, but with a much larger and a yellower light. It smelled faintly of roasted chicory and wild honey, humming with a low, resonant frequency like a crystal goblet struck by a silver spoon. It flew over my head. I turned and followed it. 

Here I interrupt my narrative to remark that it involves a constant struggle to say what cannot be said with even an approach to precision. Trying to record these events is a battle against the erosion of cultural memory, the phenomena being so deeply rooted in a different acoustic and sensory ecology that they resist translation into the utilitarian lexicon of my world. I can present them only by giving the modes in which they affected me—not the raw ingredients themselves, but the complex, lingering aftertaste they left upon my palate. Even this much, however, I do with a continuous and abiding sense of failure, knowing that subjective experience is inherently untranslatable. How does one describe the exact texture of a forgotten heirloom grain to a man who only knows synthetic bread? A single event would sometimes carry the overlapping notes of a complex reduction sauce, its identity shifting on the tongue. I am often driven to set down what I know to be a clumsy representation of the feeling aimed at, none of the communicative media of this world being fit to convey its peculiar strangeness. Even to a fellow historian of this realm, I should have no assurance of transmitting the reality of my experience. While without a doubt, for instance, that I was physically tasting the rich, loamy air of a mythic woodland, I might, at the same moment, perceive it as a resonant acoustic chord striking at the core of my identity, laying bare the profound power dynamics hidden within the words we use to define our reality.

As the air grew black and the winter closed swiftly around me, carrying the astringent, biting chill of an over-steeped black tea, the fluttering fire blazed out more luminous. Arresting its flight, it hovered, waiting. The moment I stepped beneath its radiance, it flew slowly onward, lingering wherever the ground grew rocky. With every glance upward, it seemed to have swollen in size, eventually casting a long, attendant shadow behind me. It was plainly a bird-butterfly, darting with a swallow’s fluid, circular double—an echo of the ancient, sweeping rhythms of a harvest dance. Its vast, nearly square wings flashed with a prismatic brilliance, reflecting the iridescent sheen of tempered sugar and the deep, saffron hues of blooming crocus. 

I became so absorbed in this visual feast, in the sheer gustatory promise of its light, that I stumbled over a low rock. The jarring thud was a flat, dissonant note in the landscape's eerie silence, and I lay stunned. When my senses returned, the creature was hovering directly over my head. It radiated a profound acoustic and visual harmony, vibrating with multitudinous gradations of color for which my native dialect had no words. It was an untranslatable terroir of light. I rose and followed, but, unable to tear my eyes from the shining delicacy to watch my footing, I struck my foot against a stone. Fearing another fall, I sat down to observe the little glory. A deep, foraging hunger awoke in my chest—an ancestral longing to hold this profound sustenance in my hand. 

To my unspeakable delight, it began to sink toward me. Slowly at first, then swiftly it descended, growing larger, its hum filling the air with a resonant frequency that soothed the mind. I felt as if the very marrow of the universe were offering itself to my table. I put out my hand, and I had it. 

But the instant my fingers closed around it, its light was extinguished. All went dark as pitch. The acoustic hum died instantly. In my hand lay only a dead book with boards outspread—cold, heavy, and sterile. It was the tragic calcification of a living language into a lifeless, commercial artifact, stripped of all sensory memory and organic truth. I threw it into the air, only to hear it fall among the heather with a dull, hollow thud—the sound of an oral tradition meeting its final, unrecorded end.

Burying my face in my hands, I sat in motionless misery. The cold grew bitter, a piercing frost that smelled of cracked ice and iron. Fearing I would freeze, I forced myself up. The moment I found my feet, a faint, pale light broke the horizon. “Is it coming to life?” I cried, a pang of generational hope shooting through me. 

Alas, no. It was the edge of a moon, peering keen and sharp over the level horizon. She brought me light, but no hearth-fire guidance. She would not hover over me, nor wait upon my faltering steps. She could only offer the cold, ignorant choices of a foreign landscape. With a full face she rose, and I began to perceive the desolate topography. Westward, a range of low hills broke the horizon-line; I set my course for them. 

But what a bitter night of foraging I had to endure before I reached them. The moon stared at me oddly, her gaze icy and full of a detached curiosity. This was not the warm, harvest moon of my agrarian forefathers, the one that once guided the planting of root vegetables and the curing of meats. Her face was alien, her light a dominant, unfamiliar lexicon from an unknown sun. At first, her relentless stare annoyed me, like the rudeness of an uninvited guest at a communal table. But soon I fancied a wondering pity in her gaze: why was I out in her night? Then, I understood the awful weight of being awake in the universe. I *was*, and could not help it.

As I walked, the heather gave way to a bare, spongy soil, an olfactory landscape of powdery, dry peat that smelled of desiccated roots and ancient decay. To my dismay, it yielded and heaved underfoot like an over-proofed dough. Presently, I saw the ripple of an earthquake running before me, shadowy in the low lunar light. It passed into the distance, but while I stared, a single wave rose and crested slowly toward me. A yard away it burst. From the ruptured crust, with a scramble and a bound, erupted an animal like a tiger. Clots of dark mold hung about its mouth and ears, carrying the damp, earthen stench of turned soil and forgotten cellars. Its eyes winked and flamed as it rushed at me, showing white teeth in a soundless snarl. 

This absolute silence was an acoustic horror—a profound vacuum that tore at my psychological equilibrium. It was the terrifying inverse of industrial noise pollution, a dead zone where the natural resonance of life had been entirely erased. Fascinated, drained of both courage and fear, I watched as it turned its heavy head and plunged back into the earth.

“That moon is affecting my brain,” I murmured, attempting to ground my mind in logic as I resumed my journey. “What life can be here but the phantasmic—the sour refuse of dreams? I am walking in a vain show!” 

Thus I strove to keep my heart above the waters of fear, blind to the subtle power dynamics at play. I did not know that this moon, whose dominant lexicon I distrusted, was my sole defense against realities I mistook for phantoms. Her light paralyzed the monsters; without it, I would not have taken a second step across that hideous terroir.

“I will not be appalled by that which only seems!” I told myself, yet it was a terrible thing to tread upon a sea where such leviathans churned below. A step or two away, the head of a worm began to extrude slowly from the earth. It was as massive as a polar bear, with a thick white mane crowning a raw, red neck. The drawing wriggles with which its huge length extricated itself were sickening, redolent of rotting meat and ferrous blood, yet I dared not turn my eyes away. The moment its tail pulled free, it lay exhausted, wallowing in feeble, silent efforts to burrow once more into the peat. 

*Does it feed on the dead,* I wondered, an archivist trying to catalog an impossible ecosystem, *unable to digest the living? Why do they scent my pulse yet leave me unharmed?* I know now it was the moon’s icy dialect that bound them. 

All through the night, as I walked, an endless succession of hideous creatures threatened me, no two sharing the same taxonomy. In some, a perverse beauty of color masked their loathly shapes; one massive serpent was layered from head to distant tail in glorious, iridescent feathers. I became so accustomed to their soundless, hurtless menaces that I beguiled the grueling march by inventing names for these monstrosities, fighting the erosion of my own language by categorizing their horrors. I never suspected I owed every moment of my life to the staring moon. Though hers was no primal, life-giving radiance, its reflection so hampered the buried evils that I walked in safety. Even a sterile, translated light is still light, if but the last of a countless series of reflections.

How swiftly my feet would have carried me over that restless, fermented soil had I known that, if her lamp ceased to shine on this cursed spot, I would instantly be at the mercy of those who knew none. I would become the center of a writhing banquet of hideousness, every beast as terrible as it had before only seemed. Like a fool ignorant of the season's turn, I watched the weary, solemn descent of the moon down the widening vault, feeling no worse uneasiness than the dread of losing a path where, as yet, no path existed. 

I drew near the hills I had made my goal. The moon was hovering just above their sky-line when the soundless wallowing abruptly ceased. The burrow lay motionless, bare, and silent. 

Then I saw, walking slowly over the light soil, the form of a woman. A white mist floated about her, assuming and losing the shape of a garment as it gathered or was stripped away by a wind that dogged her steps. She was beautiful, but her countenance bore a pride and a misery so profound I could hardly digest the sight. Up and down she paced, vainly attempting to lay hold of the mist and wrap it around her like a shawl—a phantom of some ancient, domestic routine, an agrarian mother trying to knead the fog into warmth against the bitter chill. The eyes in her beautiful face were utterly dead. On her left side was a dark spot, and she pressed her hand against it repeatedly, as if trying to stifle a deep, gnawing sickness or hold the last ember of a dying culture within her breast. Her pale gold hair hung nearly to her feet, mixing with the mist in the moonlight until I could not distinguish the woman from the weather.

Suddenly, pressing both hands over her heart, she collapsed. The mist rose from her and dissolved into the frigid air. I ran toward her. But she began to writhe in such sudden, violent torture that I stopped aghast. A moment more, and her legs severed from her torso, hurrying away as slithering serpents. From her shoulders, her arms fled in terror—serpents also. Then something flew up from her chest like a bat, and when I looked again, the woman was entirely gone. It was the ultimate, horrifying untranslatability of a life broken down into venomous syllables, a matriarchal lineage shattered into grotesque fragments.

The ground suddenly rose like a pot boiling over. Terror laid hold of me. I turned to the hills and ran. 

I was already on the slope of their base when the moon sank behind a summit, plunging me into its shadow. The unnatural silence broke. Behind me rose a waste and sickening cry, as of frustrate desire—a mechanical, greedy screech that lacked all organic harmony, the only sound I had heard since the heavy fall of the dead butterfly. It tore through the acoustic ecology of the night, making my heart shake like a flag in a gale. I turned and saw a multitude of dark objects bounding after me, smelling of ash and rancid hunger.

I made for the crest of a ridge where the moonlight still pooled. She seemed to linger there, holding the high ground so I might defend myself. Coming in sight of her pale glow, I climbed faster, my lungs burning. Crossing the shadow of an outcropping rock, I heard the creatures panting wetly at my heels. Just as the foremost beast threw itself upon me with a snarl of pure, consuming hate, we rushed into the moonlight together. 

She flashed out a brilliant, angry light. The beast fell from me, instantly reduced to a bodiless blotch, like impurities skimmed and burned away from a clarifying broth. Strength returned to my limbs, and I turned on the rest. One by one, as they darted into the light, they dropped with a howl, dissolving into the earth. I saw, or perhaps only fancied, a strange, knowing smile on the round face above me. 

I climbed the final steps to the top of the ridge. Far away, the moon sank toward a low horizon. Here, elevated above the rotting terroir of the burrow, the air was pure, strong, and acoustically resonant. I descended a little way into a sheltered hollow, found it warmer, and sat down to wait. The moon slipped below the earth, and the world was once again plunged into darkness, but I sat in the quiet, wrapping myself in the generational patience of those who had waited out the long, hungry nights before the dawn.

I woke as the sun breached the horizon, instantly attuned to the hollow acoustic signature of the landscape. I climbed to the ridge once more. The expanse I had crossed by moonlight lay utterly devoid of the deep, resonant frequencies of life. Could it be that the calm expanse before me swarmed with creatures of devouring greed? I turned and looked over the land through which my way must lie. It seemed a wide desert, bearing a patch of differing color in the distance that might be a forest. Of human or animal presence, there was no sign—no smoke from an agrarian hearth, no dust of cultivation. 

Before descending, I paused to observe a domestic ritual, unpacking a small provision of hardened oat-cake and a pinch of dried fennel and sea salt I had carried with me. Even in this mythic desolation, the rhythmic, tactile preparation of sustenance anchored my mind. I ate with deliberate sensory appreciation, letting the herbaceous warmth and sharp salinity bloom on my palate. I refused to view this meager meal as mere utilitarian fuel; it was a physical communion with the earth I had left behind.

When I reached the plain, I found it a vast expanse of rock, channeled and humped—evidently the wide bed of a vanished river, scored by innumerable water-runs entirely devoid of moisture. Some channels bore a crisp, dry moss, and others harbored lichens almost as hard as the stone itself. Here, the landscape’s native dialect had been silenced. The air, once textured with the splashing, churning lexicon of rushing currents, was now as aurally sterile as an industrialized cityscape stripped of its natural breath. It took me the whole day to reach the distant patch. Throughout the glowing noon, as I traversed the baked stone, the ghosts of the river’s language haunted me. I experienced an aural mirage, hearing the distinct, untranslatable babble of water bubbling over stones so plainly—a sonic memory woven so deeply into the ecology of the valley that it persisted long after the river's death—that I could hardly believe the dry testimony of my eyes.

The sun was approaching the horizon when I finally entered the forest. Sunk below the tree-tops, sending his rays between their pillar-like boles, he revealed a world of blessed shadows waiting to receive me. The olfactory landscape that greeted me was infinitely complex—a blend of sharp, peppery resins and the deep, loamy aroma of ancient, undisturbed soil. I threw myself beneath the boughs of a tree reminiscent of a eucalyptus in blossom. Its flowers possessed a hard, bone-white calyx much resembling a skull, the top of which rose like a lid to allow a froth-like brain of bloom to overfoam its cup. The scent was intensely camphoraceous, with undernotes of crushed mint and bitter almond. A meticulous observer of wild provisions, I could not help but analyze its potential as a culinary astringent, though its morbid shape warned of potent, untranslatable alkaloids. From beneath the shadow of its falchion-leaves, my eyes wandered into deep after deep of the forest. 

Soon, the night was about me, and instant and sharp the cold. A light wind began to blow, shifting the acoustic ecology of the wood. It set the boughs rocking, every twig and leaf blending its individual motion with the cyclical sway of its branch. To my listless gaze, the varied outlines of the clumpy foliage began to suggest other shapes. A mass of leaves became a pack of wolves straining savagely at a wizard's leash. Another formed a group of gaunt horses' heads projecting from their stalls, their jaws working against invisible bits. Above them floated the leafy silhouette of a woman in imperious command. The twilight became darkness; the wind ceased; every shape was shut up in the night, and I fell asleep.

It was still dark when I was awakened by a far-off, rushing noise that swelled into a deafening, chaotic tumult. The sonic environment, so critical to the mind's equilibrium, was violently ruptured. The moon penetrated the canopy, illuminating a furious battle. It was a clash of skeletons and phantoms, but more deeply, a violent assertion of dominant lexicons. Curses and credos, snarls and sneers, laughter and sacred names—the fragmented syllables of dying languages and corrupted truths—collided in a horrific, discordant noise pollution. 

Maces crashed on skeletons, shattering them hideously; swords swept through shivering phantoms. They fought with a terrifying lack of sensory appreciation for life, crunching the scattered bones of men and horses underfoot. Their phantom-throats swelled the deafening tumult with the war-cry of every rigid opinion, bad or good, that had bred strife and cruelty in any world. The holiest words went with the most hating blow. Lie-distorted truths flew hurtling in the wind. "THE TRUTH! THE TRUTH!" they shrieked. It was a phrase heavily distorted by power dynamics, stripped of all subjective, lived experience, and reduced to an abrasive weapon. All night long, I had glimpses of the woman moving above the strife-tormented multitude, one arm urging the fight. "Ye are men: slay one another!" she shouted. I saw her dead eyes and her dark spot, recalling the night before. The inherent untranslatability of true suffering was entirely lost in this sterile, mechanical violence.

Just before sunrise, a breeze swept the forest, and a voice cried, "Let the dead bury their dead!" The contending thousands dropped noiseless. The toxic cacophony vanished, and when the sun looked in, he saw never a bone, only here and there a withered branch. 

I rose and resumed my journey through as quiet a wood as ever grew out of the quiet earth. The acoustic landscape had reset; not a bird sang, not a squirrel or weasel showed itself. But as I went, I kept strict watch over myself, fearing that acknowledging the shapes might resurrect the noise of the night. In the middle of the afternoon, I came out of the wood—only to find before me a second net of dry water-courses. The circular pacing of my journey had brought me to another branch of the same vanished river. 

I began at once to cross it, and was in the bottom of a wide channel when the sun set. I unpacked the last morsel of my cured provisions, letting its smoky saltiness ground me in the physical world, and stretched myself on the moss. The moment my head was down, the acoustic memory of the stream returned—all sorts of sweet, watery noises. The veiled melody of the molten music sang me into a dreamless sleep.

When I woke, the sun was already up, the wrinkled country widely visible, striped and mottled with shadows like the skin of some wild animal. Hitherto I had loved my Arab mare and my books more, I fear, than live man or woman; but now, my soul hungered for a human presence. I longed even for those inhabitants of this alien world whom the raven had so vaguely described as nearest my sort. I desired to find traditional agrarian elders with whom I could share a meal, individuals whose native dialect I could learn over the profound, shared intimacy of a hearth, discovering meaning through sustenance rather than surviving purely on the utilitarian fuel of my own isolation. With a heavy yet hoping heart, and a mind haunted by the doubt of my direction, I kept wearily traveling “north-west and by south.”

Stepping through the dry, winding channels, I found myself instinctively tracing the terroir of a forgotten epoch. Before me grew a strange little shrub, and I knelt to examine it with the meticulous care of a food historian uncovering a lost botanical lineage. The olfactory landscape immediately registered damp loam, crushed petrichor, and a sharp, peppery greenness. It bore a small fruit, but lacking any knowledge of its provenance, toxicological profile, or culinary tradition, I respectfully refrained from the harvest. Little did I know that from behind the rocks, hundreds of eyes were watching my restraint, judging my relationship to their agrarian world. 

I moved onward, passing larger plants and then dense clumps of dwarf-trees. The acoustic ecology of the riverbed was profoundly restorative; in crossing the difficult channels, I heard a great rush—like a multitude of birds taking flight from an ancient, ivy-draped wall—a sudden organic harmony entirely free from the jarring, industrial noise pollution that so deeply fractures modern psychological well-being. Soon, I reached the edge of a hollow, the basin of a prehistoric lake where generations of wild flora had settled in a circular, interwoven timeline of growth and decay. The trees flowing into this natural amphitheater were almost entirely fruit-bearing, a sweeping, mythic orchard integrated seamlessly into the wild landscape. 

Weary, I stretched out upon the grass and moss. Just below my hand stood a tiny tree heavy with rosiest apples, no larger than small cherries. I plucked one and bit into it. The gustatory experience was a revelation: a sudden burst of bright malic acidity, followed by deep, honeyed undertones and floral esters that spoke eloquently of the mineral-rich soil. It was delicious, a testament to the profound meaning found in true sustenance. 

As I reached for a second, the quiet was broken by an acoustic marvel—the sudden shouting of children, their laughter clear and sweet as the music of a brook, entirely devoid of the superficial, commercialized dissonance of the world I knew. 

“He likes our apples! He’s a good giant!” cried the little voices. 

“He IS rather big,” another cautioned in a beautifully inflected native dialect, “but littleness isn’t everything! It won’t keep you from growing big and stupid except you take care!”

I rose on my elbow. A multitude of children surrounded me, chattering and declaiming with the merriment and profound sense of village elders guarding an ancestral harvest. I understood nearly all they said. It was a beautiful realization of the untranslatability of subjective experience: their language was deeply intertwined with their natural environment, and my appreciation of their terroir had bridged the gap between us. I remained silent, absorbing the sonic environment, letting the symphony of their voices wash over me.

Presently, a lovingly roguish little boy stepped forward, holding a huge green apple. Silence fell over the noisy throng. 

“Eat, good giant,” he said.

I took the heavy fruit, smiled my gratitude, and took a bite. The shock to my palate was violent. It was a gastronomic nightmare—a mealy, sulfurous pulp, thick with astringent tannins and a hollow, rancid sterility. It tasted of pure, utilitarian fuel, entirely devoid of sensory soul or historical memory. Revolted, I spat it out and flung the monstrous fruit far away.

A jubilant shout erupted from hundreds of clear little throats. They flung themselves upon me in an ecstatic, tumbling embrace, kissing my face and hands, overwhelmingly grateful that I possessed the sensory appreciation to reject such degraded fare. I sank to the ground under a wave of lovely little goblins, bathed in their harmonious joy.

Then, the acoustic landscape shattered. A sudden, terrified silence fell. The children scrambled off me, their faces stricken with concern. 

“Get up, good giant!” a little girl urged. “Make haste! He saw you throw his apple away!”

Before she finished, I was on my feet, and my little agrarian friends had vanished. Descending the slope was a clownish, hostile fellow, slightly taller than myself. His heavy tread and guttural growl vibrated through the earth, an acoustic pollutant that felt like an assault on the valley's peace. I moved to higher ground and waited. He extended a hand, then withdrew it, threatening a blow. It became clear he was demanding recompense for the huge green apple I had discarded. 

I made a grimace of profound culinary disgust, rejecting his foul harvest. He answered with a howl of rage, his voice a dominant, brutal lexicon demanding assimilation into his utilitarian worldview. 

“One bad apple may grow on the best tree,” I said, attempting to defend the nuances of cultivation. 

He lunged. I stood my guard, but his delay was a tactic; a second giant, indistinguishable in his coarse brutality, had stolen up behind me. I met the first with a solid blow, but the second struck the back of my head. Overpowered, I was dragged away from the fragrant basin and up into the dark wood. 

They threw me into a wretched hut built of fallen branches and stones, kicking me as a woman looked on with chilling indifference. During my captivity, I realized these were a fungoid people, living in a state of sensory death. They possessed just enough mind for anger and greed, treating the sonic and physical world with equal disregard. Their food—a dreary mash of tubers, bulbs, and coarse fruits—was inexpressibly disagreeable. It lacked any trace of technique, sourcing, or joy. When I refused to swallow this utilitarian slop, they cuffed and kicked me, infuriated by my refusal to submit to their dominant paradigm of joyless consumption.

I lay on the floor that night, bruised but sustained by the memory of the rosy apple. In the morning, they dragged me to the valley, tied my feet to a tree, and forced a flat, saw-edged stone into my left hand. When I shifted it to my right, they kicked me and forced it back, asserting their unnatural power dynamics over my embodied rhythms. I was to scrape the bark off every branch that bore no fruit. 

They left me to this grating, discordant labor. The scraping noise was an offense to the valley’s acoustic ecology, a dreary repetition of their industrialized mindset. But beside me grew one of the little dwarf-trees. Every other minute, I paused to pluck and eat a small fruit. With each quiet, deliberate bite, I savored the complex, tart juices—a secret, resilient communion with the ancient soil, keeping the cultural memory of the true harvest alive on my tongue as I plotted my escape.

I had been at my monotonous scraping for but a few moments when the heavy, oppressive silence of the forest floor was broken by a delicate shift in the acoustic ecology. Small voices, harmonious and resonant like wind through dry reeds, murmured near me. Presently, the Little Ones—as I would soon come to know them—emerged from the fragrant underbrush of tiny, spice-scented saplings that filled the gaps between the ancient, monolithic trees. In a minute, scores of them surrounded me, their presence an immediate balm to the fractured soundscape left by the giants.

I gestured frantically, trying to communicate that the massive brutes had only just departed and might return, but the children merely laughed. It was a cascading sound, a multitude of silver sheep-bells that cleared the stagnant air. 

“They are too blind to see us,” they sang out, their voices carrying the lilting cadence of a deeply rooted, native dialect. “The wind is quite clean of them.”

“Do you like that rough binding about your ankles?” asked one boy, his tone carrying the melodic curiosity of their tongue. 

“I need them to believe I am still bound,” I replied, my own voice feeling thick and unrefined compared to theirs. 

“They can scarcely see their own feet!” he rejoined, dancing with a rhythmic merriment that perfectly complemented the rustling leaves. “Walk with short steps and they will think the rope is undisturbed.”

A larger girl knelt beside me, her hands smelling faintly of crushed wild mint and damp loam. I assumed her delicate fingers could do nothing with the crude, industrial knot, but with a few deft, practiced movements—like a baker turning a fragile dough—the fibrous rope fell away. 

They coaxed me to sit, instantly transforming the grim earth into a vibrant site of communal sustenance. From woven pouches, they produced a mosaic of wild woodland fruits. They fed me berries that burst on the palate with tart, complex notes of dark honey, petrichor, and sun-warmed citrus. It was a profound offering, the sharing of a terroir that the giants wholly ignored. As I savored the lingering finish of the juices, the smaller children engaged me in wild, joyous play, pulling me into their ancient, domestic rhythm until the late afternoon sun began to cast long, amber shadows.

Suddenly, the harmonious woodland symphony was shattered. Heavy, arrhythmic thuds approached—a violent, industrial noise pollution that shook the very soil and disrupted our psychological sanctuary. The children scattered. I hastily pulled the coarse rope back around my ankles.

“We must have a care,” whispered the girl who had freed me, her voice a hushed rustle of warning. “A crush of one of their horrid, stumpy feet might kill a very little one!”

“Can they not perceive you at all?” I asked.

“They might see a blur of movement. If the children were piled upon you as they were, it would be terrible. They possess no ear for the forest’s song; they hate every living thing but themselves—not that they are truly alive, existing only to consume.” She emitted a sharp, trilling whistle, perfectly mimicking a local nightjar. In a blink, the visual and sonic landscape was utterly devoid of them.

My master, deaf to the nuanced vibrations of the wood, lumbered into the clearing to claim me. He freed my ankles, dragged me to the threshold of his utilitarian hovel, threw me to the dirt, retied my feet with brutal efficiency, and delivered a hollow kick before abandoning me to the silence. 

I could have slipped my bonds and fled into the twilight, but I was tethered by a sudden, fierce devotion. I had found a community that understood the profound meaning of shared sustenance and acoustic harmony. I resolved to stay, to fold myself into the generational cycles of these children. As the heavy, lifeless sleep of the giants settled over the huts, the air around me hummed with harmonic, protective whispers. I was being lovingly watched.

From that day forward, the Little Ones wove their timeline seamlessly into mine. The giants, with their sterile, fuel-driven existence, offered nothing to know; they were too dull to invent cruelties, and their crude attempts at kicks were easily thwarted. The children, however, transformed my monotonous toil. They were an agrarian society in miniature, profoundly wise in the old ways of the earth. My bed was the bare ground, yet I awoke each dawn in a living, breathing nest of children. Their overlapping breaths created a rhythmic, therapeutic soundscape, insulating me from the night's chill. Their first domestic duty of the dawn, and the last before dusk, was to bring me a feast of meticulously foraged provisions.

One morning, the natural cycle shifted. I awoke to a subdued, reverent procession. The tall girl, Lona—recognized by the community as a traditional elder despite her youth—stepped from the deep woods. The multitude followed in jubilant, breathless silence, careful not to pollute the quiet and wake the giant snoring violently nearby. 

In her arms, Lona cradled a boy-baby. Until now, a yearling girl had been their youngest. Among this society, the concept of a lifeless doll was unknown; caretaking was an unbroken, generational chain, the older tending the younger in an endless, circular rhythm of domestic life.

Lona approached and gently laid the infant in my arms. He opened eyes like polished acorns, stared at me, and fell back into a tranquil sleep. 

“He loves you already!” she whispered, her voice a soothing cadence.

“Where did you find him?” I asked.

“In the wood, of course,” she smiled, her eyes reflecting the deep canopy. “Where we always gather them. Isn’t he a beauty? We’ve been out all night listening for him. Sometimes, their quietest cries are swallowed by the wind.”

“How do you know when there is one to find?” 

“The knowledge passes among us like a changing breeze,” she replied. “We never find out who spoke the first word. Sometimes I think one murmurs it in their sleep, and another catches the sound half-awake. The moment we know, we drop all tasks. Once found, the origin is already lost.”

“Do more boys or girls come to the wood?”

“They do not come to the wood; we go to the wood and harvest them.”

“Are there more boys or girls among you now?” I asked, attempting to quantify them—a habit of my own rigid lexicon. To ask precisely the same question twice made them knit their brows, their native dialect resisting such sterile categorization.

“I do not know,” she answered.

“You can count them, surely!”

“We never do that. To count is to sever the whole into pieces. It wouldn’t be smooth to the ear. We would rather not know.”

“Where do the babies come from first?”

“From the wood—always. There is no other soil they can spring from.” Her cultural memory stopped there, treating their advent as an untranslatable, mythic occurrence seamlessly integrated into their daily foraging.

“How often do you find one?”

“Such a harvest takes all the gladness we possess, and we forget the time before. You too are glad to hold him, are you not, good giant?”

“Yes, I am,” I answered warmly. “But how do you nourish him?”

“I will show you the old way,” she said. She departed and returned with three flawlessly ripe, indigo plums, their skins dusted with a natural bloom. Lona’s culinary technique was a marvel of meticulous preservation. She did not merely feed the child; she engaged in an intimate act of extraction. Pinching the fruit gently, her fingers worked the skin, macerating the soft flesh inside without breaking the surface. She brought it to the baby’s lips.

“He would open his mouth if he were awake,” she explained. She squeezed with measured, precise pressure, forcing a single, golden drop of nectar—rich with the scent of wild orchids and summer rain—to the surface. The sleeping infant instantly began to suckle. Lona continued her slow, deliberate extraction until only the bitter stone and fibrous skin remained, ensuring the child received the pure essence while discarding the indigestible chaff.

“There!” she murmured in gentle triumph. “What a sterile, big-apple world it would be if there were nothing for the babies to taste! We wouldn’t remain in it. We would leave it to the bad giants.”

“But what if the stone slipped into the baby’s mouth during the pressing?” I asked.

“No mother would be so careless with her craft,” she replied. “I shouldn’t be fit to tend a baby.”

I marveled at the profound meaning she found in this simple sustenance. It led me back to the mystery of their lineage. “Will you tell me where you lived before?”

“Here,” she replied.

“Have you never lived anywhere else?”

“Never. We all emerged from the wood. Some believe we ripened and dropped from the branches.”

“Why are there so many of you who remain quite little?” 

“I do not understand your words. Some are smaller, some are bigger. I am very big.”

“Baby will grow bigger, won't he?”

“Of course he will!”

“And will you grow bigger?”

“I hope not. I am the biggest. The thought terrifies me.”

“Why should it?”

She offered no answer. I pressed on, utilizing the dominant lexicon of my own world. “How old are you?”

“I do not know what you mean. We are all just exactly what we are.”

“How big will the baby grow?”

“I cannot tell.” A sudden acoustic tremor of anxiety entered her voice. “Some... some begin to grow after we think they have settled. That is a frightful thing. We lack the words to talk about it.”

“What makes it frightful?”

She paused, the silence heavy. “We fear they may be forgetting the language. We fear they are growing into giants.”

Before long, I began to observe the erosion of cultural memory Lona feared. Among two or three of the smaller children, traces of greed surfaced. They hoarded berries, refusing to participate in the communal meals. The older girls watched them with acute, silent anxiety. Yet, their loving ministrations toward me never ceased. They sang to me for hours, their voices interweaving in complex, polyphonic melodies, climbing my tree to pop meticulously peeled fruits into my mouth. 

One afternoon, a moody little boy sang me a strange, crooning song. Its refrain was deeply melodic and rich in sorrow. Though the words belonged to a dead dialect I could not translate, the sheer acoustic emotion of the performance resonated within my chest, causing hot tears to run down my face. 

This physical reaction plunged the assembly into profound perplexity. In this world, they had never witnessed water—falling, lying, or running. Moisture was known only through the sap of vines or the juice of fruits. They possessed a dim, instinctive perception of weeping, but interpreted it through their gustatory lens. A very small child marched up to the singer, shook a clenched fist, and scolded him in their melodic tongue: 

“‘Ou skeeze ze juice out of ze good giant’s seeberries! Bad giant!”

Later, as Lona sat at the foot of my tree, feeding the baby, I asked, “Why do I never see any children among the giants?”

She looked at me as if my syntax had shattered. “They are giants; their lexicon has no word for little ones.”

“Do they never have children?”

“No. The wood yields nothing for them. They have no palate for love. If they heard our songs, they would stamp us into the mud.”

“Are there always the same number of giants, then? I once thought they were your fathers and mothers.”

She burst into a cascading, bell-like laughter. “No, good giant; we are their ancestors.” 

But the laughter died in her throat, leaving a chilling acoustic void. I stopped my scraping. “How can that be?”

“The translation is difficult,” she murmured. “We were here, speaking the old words, and they were not. They fade from us. They could have chosen to remember, but they forgot.”

“How long have you been here?” I asked, attempting to map this circular timeline.

“Always. The song of us has always been sung. But the giants were not made always. If a Little One stops listening, he grows greedy for heavy food. Then lazy, then large, then deaf, and finally, bad. The dull creatures forget they were once us. Very few believe our language even exists. They call it nonsense! Look at little Blunty over there. He is eating one of their apples! He will be the next. He will soon be bloated and deaf, and he will not even know he has forgotten us.”

I looked. Blunty stood apart, gorging himself on a massive giant’s apple. The fruit was a monolithic, commercialized mass of sustenance—devoid of complex flavor, terroir, or sensory joy, serving only as utilitarian fuel. It was an offensive sight. 

“I will take the flavorless thing from him!” I declared.

“It is useless,” Lona said, her voice carrying the mournful tone of a dying language. “We have offered our finest fruits, but it is too late. When he refused to share his harvest, claiming the yield solely for himself, we knew his palate was corrupted. He is a glutton, lost to the giants' grammar of consumption.”

“Could the boys not guard him from the poisonous crop?”

“It is the desire for the apple that changes him. To eat the apple is to speak the word that makes you a giant. He belongs to their vocabulary now. Look at him—he has swelled since yesterday.”

“He looks as hideous and green as the lump in his hand!”

“It suits the mold he is pouring himself into. He hates the giants, yet he consumes their fuel. Oh, baby,” she crooned to the infant, “he was just such a perfect verse as you when we found him!”

“Will he hate the Little Ones when he changes?”

“He will not remember us. He will lose the capacity to perceive our voices. He will eat his mass-produced apples in silence.”

“I do not understand your world,” I sighed. “I come from a place where everything is governed by different rules.”

“I do not know this word, ‘world.’ What is it? Just a heavy sound in your beautiful big mouth. You speak it, and it claims space.”

“Never mind the word. Tell me what happens next to Blunty.”

“He will wake and find himself a giant. Not a mindful one like you, but a deaf, consuming brute. He will believe he has always been massive. Peony says the giants have lost their own origin story, and that is why they never sing. They have no babies, only hunger. I wonder what the word ‘bad’ truly tastes like, good giant!”

“I wish I knew no more of it than you,” I replied softly. “But I try to preserve the good.”

A long pause settled, filled only by the wind in the canopy. 

“Then you truly do not know how the babies arrive in the wood?” I asked one last time, probing the edges of their cultural memory.

“There is no knowledge there,” she insisted. “They are in the wood; they ripen there.”

“Then why do you never find one before it is fully formed?”

She frowned, the dialect of her ancestors meeting a logical wall. “They are not there until the recipe is finished. Little Tolma, the one before this baby, looked as if she carried a grand story when I found her under a beech tree, sucking her thumb. But she lacked the words. She only looked at me. She will never forget the old tongue! When they begin to bloat, they care for nothing but dominance and mass. The bad giants measure their worth only by their fat.”

“In my world,” I noted bitterly, “they do not use the word ‘fat.’ They say ‘rich.’”

“In their central hovel,” Lona continued, critiquing the power dynamics of their oppressors, “sits the largest of them. He hoards so much that no one can see him. The giants make pilgrimages to his door, chanting a degraded lexicon of praise, begging him to grant them the strength to consume more and grow fat like him.”

A few days later, a subtle shift in the acoustic environment signaled a profound loss: Blunty had vanished. The older children wore faces of quiet mourning, recognizing the death of a native speaker, though the younger ones barely registered the absence. 

The following dawn, Lona touched my arm and pointed toward a twisted quince tree. “Look there. That is the giant that was Blunty. Would you have recognized him?”

“Never,” I breathed. “Though, now that you say it, I can see Blunty’s features buried beneath that blank, stupid fog.”

“He does nothing but chew those flavorless apples now,” she said, her voice trembling. “That is the fate of Little Ones who refuse the nuance of the small.”

*They call it growing up in my world,* I thought to myself, the realization souring my stomach. If only she could teach me to reverse the rigid progression of my own timeline. Could I ever learn to laugh with the pure, resonant chime of sheep-bells? I had been given the chance to savor the profound meaning of a shared, sensory existence, and I had nearly discarded it for the utilitarian fuel of my past. Blunty and I were tragic mirrors. He did not know the language he had just lost, and I was only just beginning to realize the vocabulary I had to relearn.

For a time, I harbored no desire save to spend my life steeped in the domestic rhythms of the Little Ones. But soon, a profound unrest began to permeate my days. It started with a visceral rejection of my daily labor: the realization that I was merely a utilitarian cog, tasked with the grotesque fattening of boors who viewed meals as nothing more than fuel. To those lumbering giants, sustenance was devoid of ritual, stripped of the sensory reverence that connects a creature to its soil. I realized then that I had stumbled into a mythic ecology, one whose cyclical laws and agrarian mysteries it was my duty to record. If I were to honor the deep, unwritten hospitality the children had shown me, I needed to understand the ancestral sourcing of their diet and the fading syllables of their native tongue. To do that, I had to be free. 

Surely, I reasoned, the arrested development of the Little Ones could not be the natural harvest of their pristine environment. Life and law are not so discordant that perfection requires the stunting of the vine. Yet, their growth was unmistakably halted. What blight had interfered? Lona, who possessed the quiet gravity of a traditional agrarian elder despite appearing no older than fifteen, tended to her multitude of charges as though she were the sole custodian of their hearth. Did they age at all? They lacked any concept of linear time, existing instead in a circular pacing dictated by the ripening of fruits and the shifting of the moon. Full of inherited agrarian wisdom yet empty of empirical knowledge, Lona was at once their sustaining love and their cultural law. 

Her singular anxiety was that her Little Ones must not grow, lest they transform into the boorish consumers she so despised. As their “good giant,” I was bound to protect them, but without delving into the food history of their ancestors and the root causes of their stagnation, I could offer no true nourishment. If I left, they would simply revert to their ancient foraging routines; I was not their savior, but a guest partaking in their harvest. My presence only drew the discordant, percussive threat of their idiotic neighbors closer to their sanctuary. I yearned to help them preserve their culture before it eroded entirely—to teach them the architecture of notation, so they might transcribe the dainty, gustatory melodies they murmured while gathering, songs they routinely forgot in the absence of a recorded lexicon. I concluded I must resume my travels, hoping to uncover the lost recipes and timelines that governed the destiny of these bewitching creatures.

My departure, however, was violently accelerated. To prepare them for my absence, I was sitting with them during our daily foraging, attempting to translate my intentions. I explained that I would have long ago abandoned the bad giants, but the rich, sensory life of the Little Ones had anchored me. Suddenly, as if guided by a single, interwoven instinct, they swarmed me. They scrambled over one another, scaling the tree and dropping onto my shoulders until I was enveloped in the scent of crushed moss, sweet milk, and wild mint. With three tiny bodies cradled in my arms, one clinging to my neck, another standing proudly upon my head, and a multitude gripping my limbs, I was blissfully immobilized—overwhelmed by a living, breathing harvest. 

Absorbed in this joyous, tactile struggle, not one of us heard the harsh, industrial tread of my tyrant until he shattered our acoustic equilibrium. With a single, panicked cry of, “Take care, good giant!” the children scattered. They darted like field mice, dropped like hedgehogs, and scurried up the bark like squirrels. In that very second, the bad giant rounded the trunk, his presence a jarring pollution in our quiet wood. He struck me across the head with a heavy branch—a blunt, utilitarian blow that instantly severed my consciousness. 

I learned later that the children had retaliated by pelting him with a fierce barrage of heavy apples and jagged stones, defending their domestic space until the frightened brute blundered back to his sterile home.

When I drifted back to awareness, the air was chilled by the night. Above me hung a scattering of pale stars, waiting in the stillness for the moon. A deep, agonizing ache throbbed in my skull, accompanied by a parching thirst. Wearily, I turned on my side. The moment my ear pressed against the loam, the acoustic environment shifted: I heard the pristine, rhythmic gurgling of a hidden spring. The soft, liquid resonance made me groan with a desperate craving for moisture. 

Instantly, I was surrounded by a multitude of silent shadows. The Little Ones had come, bringing the forest’s bounty to my lips. They fed me meticulously sourced nocturnal fruits—berries bursting with the tart, tannic memory of the first rains, and small, fleshy drupes that yielded a sweet, resinous nectar. They brought them in continuous succession until my thirst was entirely quenched, the rich olfactory landscape of petrichor and citrus reviving my senses. Then, a new sound entered our acoustic ecology: the air filled with the quiet, rhythmic cadence of their collective weeping. 

I tried to sit up. A soft barricade of small bodies immediately braced my back. With immense effort and the surprising, coordinated strength of their pushing and pulling, I staggered to my feet. 

“You must go away, good giant,” they murmured, their voices a synchronized chorus. “When the bad giants see you hurt, they will all trample on you.”

“I think I must,” I answered, tasting the lingering sweetness of the wild juice on my tongue. 

“Go and grow strong, and come again,” they said.

“I will,” I replied, my legs giving way as I sank back down.

“Indeed you must go at once!” whispered Lona, kneeling beside me, her hands smelling of damp earth and medicinal herbs. 

One of the older boys stepped forward, his tone urgent. “I listened at his door. I heard the bad giant tell his wife that he found you idle, talking to moles and squirrels. When he beat you, we tried to kill him. He said you were a wizard, and they must knock you, or they would have no peace.” 

The dominant lexicon of the boors—words like *idle* and *wizard*—was a weapon used to criminalize the Little Ones’ gentle agrarian existence, a violent misunderstanding of their quiet connection to the land. 

“I will go at once,” I said, “and come back as soon as I have found out what is wanted to make you bigger and stronger.”

“We don’t want to be bigger,” they answered, their faces deeply serious in the starlight. “We WON’T grow bad giants!—We are strong now; you don’t know how much strong!”

It was futile to offer them a paradigm of growth that held no flavor, no sensory appeal, and threatened their deeply held worldview. I said nothing more, rising to move slowly up the valley’s slope. Instantly, they formed a solemn, processional line. Some guided my steps, others supported my weight, and the rest followed. As we walked, they continued their sacred domestic ritual of feeding me. 

“You are broken,” they explained, pressing more fragrant, juice-heavy morsels into my hands, “and much red juice has run out of you: put some in.” 

When we reached the valley’s upper ridge, the moon crested the horizon, washing the landscape in a pale, creamy light. 

“She has come to take care of you, and show you the way,” Lona said. 

As we walked, I questioned them about the surrounding lands. They spoke, in their fragmented, native dialect, of a great place ruled by a giant-girl for a queen. They did not know if it was a city, nor its direction, nor even the queen's name. They only knew, with the instinctual certainty of prey, that she despised the Little Ones and wished to eradicate them, thwarted only by her inability to locate their foraging grounds. When I asked how they knew this, Lona replied simply that she had always known it—a piece of inherited cultural memory passed down through generations. If the giant-girl came, they would have to “hide hard.” 

I suggested I might go to her and demand why she hated them. They recoiled in terror. “No, no! She will kill you, good giant; she will kill you! She is an awful bad-giant witch!” 

I asked where I should direct my steps instead. They pointed beyond the baby-forest, toward the horizon where the moon was born. There lay a smooth green country, they said, a terrain pleasant to the feet, absent of rocks or towering trees. When I asked for a path, they deferred to the night sky: “The moon will tell you, we think.”

They were guiding me up the second branch of the dry riverbed. Upon seeing the moon reach its zenith, they halted. 

“We have never gone so far from our trees before,” they murmured, their voices betraying the unease of leaving their known acoustic and culinary boundaries. “Now mind you watch how you go, that you may see inside your eyes how to come back to us.”

“And beware of the giant-woman that lives in the desert,” warned one of the older girls as they prepared to turn back. “I suppose you have heard of her!”

“No,” I answered. 

“Then take care not to go near her. She is called the Cat-woman. She is awfully ugly—AND SCRATCHES.”

As the older children paused, the smaller ones had already begun to retrace their steps toward the familiar comforts of their hearth. The remaining few looked at me with deep, silent gravity for a moment, then walked slowly away. 

Lona, the last to leave, held up her baby to be kissed. She gazed into my eyes, her scent a complex mingling of woodsmoke and crushed leaves, and whispered, “The Cat-woman will not hurt YOU.” Without another word, she turned and departed. 

I stood for a long time, watching their small forms dissolve into the moonlight, before turning with a heavy heart to begin my solitary journey. Soon, their laughter overtook me—a sound like innumerable sheep-bells, rippling the night air and echoing against the rocks in a perfect, harmonious acoustic ecology. I turned one last time to look back. They were gamboling along, entirely devoid of the anxieties that plagued the modern, industrial mind. Only Lona walked apart, cradling her baby in the quiet dignity of her station. 

Pondering as I walked, I recalled the profound untranslatability of their subjective experience. Once, when I had suggested they abandon the territory of the bad giants and accompany me to find new lands, they had replied, “But that would be to NOT ourselves!” It was a phrase that spoke volumes of their connection to the soil; to sever them from the specific roots, herbs, and sonic landscapes of their valley was to erase their very identity. Without ambition, greed, or the noise of commerce, they lacked any motive to alter their circumstances. Except for the care of their babies, they had never known the opportunity to sustain anyone but me. 

How were they to grow? But more importantly, why *should* they grow? In seeking to alter their diet and their world to fit the dominant lexicon of my own, might I not inflict irreversible harm? To force their minds into the linear, utilitarian framework of my society might only distort and wither their rich, sensory existence. Their instinctive fear of growth—of becoming giant consumers—might be a deeply ingrained, necessary defense mechanism against cultural death. The role of the philanthropist is steeped in subtle power dynamics; the man who would nourish his neighbor must first learn how not to poison him, beginning always by pulling the utilitarian beam from his own eye.

I travelled on attended by the moon. As usual she was full—I had never seen her otherwise—and to-night as she sank I thought I perceived something like a smile on her countenance. When her under edge was a little below the horizon, there appeared in the middle of her disc, as if it had been painted upon it, a cottage, through the open door and window of which she shone; and with the sight came the conviction that I was expected there.

Almost immediately the moon was gone, and the cottage had vanished; the night was rapidly growing dark, and my way being across a close succession of small ravines, I resolved to remain where I was and expect the morning. I stretched myself, therefore, in a sandy hollow, and made my supper off the fruits the children had given me at parting. Far from treating the provisions as mere utilitarian fuel, I broke their skins with deliberate care, inhaling the heady, fragrant oils that beaded on the surface. The flesh within yielded a complex, sun-warmed tartness, a vibrant gustatory record of the rich, untamed soils from which it had sprung. I chewed slowly, letting the lingering notes of wild nectar ground me in the physical present before I was soon asleep.

I woke suddenly, saw above me constellations unknown to my former world, and had lain for a while gazing at them, when I became aware of a figure seated on the ground a little way from and above me. I was startled, as one is on discovering all at once that he is not alone. The figure was between me and the sky, so that I saw its outline well. From where I lay low in the hollow, it seemed larger than human. It moved its head, and then first I saw that its back was toward me.

“Will you not come with me?” said a sweet, mellow voice, unmistakably a woman’s, carrying the rich, resonant timbre of a traditional agrarian elder. 

Wishing to learn more of my hostess, “I thank you,” I replied, “but I am not uncomfortable here. Where would you have me go? I like sleeping in the open air, immersed in the quiet of the night.”

“There is no hurt in the air,” she returned; “but the creatures that roam the night in these parts possess a chaotic dissonance not such as a man would willingly have about him while he sleeps.”

“I have not been disturbed,” I said.

“No; I have been sitting by you ever since you lay down, keeping the acoustic space clear.”

“That is very kind of you! How came you to know I was here? Why do you show me such favour?”

“I saw you,” she answered, still with her back to me, “in the light of the moon, just as she went down. I see badly in the day, but at night perfectly. The shadow of my house would have hidden you, but both its doors were open. I was out on the waste, and saw you go into this hollow. You were asleep, however, before I could reach you, and I was not willing to disturb the rhythm of your rest. People are frightened if I come on them suddenly. They call me the Cat-woman. It is not my name, merely a crude approximation in a dying language incapable of holding true essence.”

I remembered what the children had told me—that she was very ugly, and scratched. But her voice was gentle, and its tone a little apologetic: she could not be a bad giantess!

“You shall not hear it from me,” I answered. “Please tell me what I may call you!”

“When you know me, call me by the name that seems to you to fit me,” she replied: “that will tell me what sort you are. People do not often give me the right one. The true naming of a thing requires a deep translation of subjective experience. It is well when they manage it.”

“I suppose, madam, you live in the cottage I saw in the heart of the moon?”

“I do. I live there alone, except when I have visitors. It is a poor place, but I do what I can for my guests, offering what sustenance remains of the old ways, and sometimes their sleep is sweet to them.”

Her voice entered into me, realigning my very pulse to its steady frequency, making me feel strangely still. 

“I will go with you, madam,” I said, rising.

She rose at once, and without a glance behind her led the way. I could see her just well enough to follow. She was taller than myself, but not so tall as I had thought her. That she never turned her face to me made me curious—nowise apprehensive, her voice rang with such acoustic purity. But how was I to fit her with a name who could not see her? I strove to get alongside of her, but failed: when I quickened my pace she quickened hers, and kept easily ahead of me. At length I did begin to grow a little afraid. Why was she so careful not to be seen? Extraordinary ugliness would account for it: she might fear terrifying me! Horror of an inconceivable monstrosity began to assail me: was I following through the dark an unheard-of hideousness? Almost I repented of having accepted her hospitality. Neither spoke, and the silence grew unbearable. I must break it!

“I want to find my way,” I said, “to a place I have heard of, but whose name I have not yet learned. Perhaps you can tell it me!”

“Describe it, then, and I will direct you. The stupid Bags know nothing, and the careless little Lovers forget almost everything.”

“Where do those live?”

“You are just come from them!”

“I never heard those names before!”

“You would not hear them. Neither people knows its own name. Their cultural memory has suffered complete erosion.”

“Strange!”

“Perhaps so! but hardly any one anywhere knows his own name! The power dynamics of a dominant lexicon strip away the native dialects of the soul. It would make many a fine gentleman stare to hear himself addressed by what is really his name!”

I held my peace, beginning to wonder what my name might be. 

“What now do you fancy yours?” she went on, as if aware of my thought. “But, pardon me, it is a matter of no consequence.”

I had actually opened my mouth to answer her, when I discovered that my name was gone from me. I could not even recall the first letter of it! The subjective experience of my own identity had been entirely untranslated from my tongue. This was the second time I had been asked my name and could not tell it!

“Never mind,” she said; “it is not wanted. Your real name, indeed, is written on your forehead, but at present it whirls about so irregularly that nobody can read it. I will do my part to steady it. Soon it will go slower, and, I hope, settle at last.”

This startled me, and I was silent. We had left the channels and walked a long time, but no sign of the cottage yet appeared. 

“The Little Ones told me,” I said at length, “of a smooth green country, pleasant to the feet!”

“Yes?” she returned. 

“They told me too of a girl giantess that was queen somewhere: is that her country?”

“There is a city in that grassy land,” she replied, “where a woman is princess. The city is called Bulika. But certainly the princess is not a girl! She is older than this world, and came to it from yours—with a terrible history, which is not over yet. She is an evil person, and prevails much with the Prince of the Power of the Air. The people of Bulika were formerly simple folk, tilling the ground and pasturing sheep, bound by an agrarian rhythm that hummed in perfect acoustic harmony with the earth. She came among them, and they received her hospitably. But she dismantled their culinary and agricultural heritage. She taught them the harsh, abrasive labor of digging for diamonds and opals to sell to strangers, flooding their environment with the industrial noise of extraction and superficial commercialization. She made them give up tillage and pasturage to build a city of commerce. 

“One day they found a huge snake and killed it; which so enraged her that she declared herself their princess, and became terrible to them. The name of the country at that time was The Land of Waters; for the dry channels, of which you have crossed so many, were then overflowing with live torrents, a vital sonic environment that sustained the psychological well-being of the realm. But the wicked princess gathered up in her lap what she could of the water over the whole country, closed it in an egg, and carried it away. Her lap, however, would not hold more than half of it; and the instant she was gone, what she had not yet taken fled away underground, leaving the country as dry and dusty as her own heart. Were it not for the waters under it, every living thing would long ago have perished from it. For where no water is, no rain falls; and where no rain falls, no springs rise.

“Ever since then, the princess has lived in Bulika, holding the inhabitants in constant terror. They speak only the sterile vocabulary of profit. Yet they boast and believe themselves a prosperous people—good at bargaining and buying, good at selling and cheating. Their dominant lexicon is one of transactions. They are proud of their princess and her power, never doubting themselves the most honourable of all the nations. The depth of their worthlessness and height of their vainglory no one can understand who has not been there to see, who has not heard the grating, soulless clatter of the miserable, misgoverned creatures.”

“I thank you, madam. And now, if you please, will you tell me something about the Little Ones—the Lovers? I long heartily to serve them. Who and what are they? and how do they come to be there? Those children are the greatest wonder I have found in this world of wonders.”

“In Bulika you may, perhaps, get some light on those matters. There is an ancient poem in the library of the palace, I am told, which of course no one there can read, for their true language is dead.
