But in it is plainly written that after the Lovers have gone through great troubles and recovered the native dialect of their own name, they will fill the land, and make the giants their slaves.”

“By that time they will have grown a little, will they not?” I said.

“Yes, they will have grown; yet I think too they will not have grown. It is possible to grow and not to grow, to grow less and to grow bigger, both at once—yes, even to grow by means of not growing! The cycles of generations do not move in straight lines, but in great, interwoven circles.”

“Your words are strange, madam!” I rejoined. “But I have heard it said that some words, because they mean more, appear to mean less!”

“That is true, and such words have to be understood in the body, not just the mind. It were well for the princess of Bulika if she heard what the very silence of the land is shouting in her ears all day long! But she is far too clever to understand anything.”

“Then I suppose, when the little Lovers are grown, their land will have water again?”

“Not exactly so: when they are thirsty enough, they will have water, and when they have water, they will grow. To grow, they must have water. And, beneath, it is flowing still.”

“I have heard that water twice,” I said; “—once when I lay down to wait for the moon—and when I woke the sun was shining! and once when I fell, all but killed by the bad giant. Both times came the acoustic resonance of the water, a deep, subterranean hum that healed me.”

The woman never turned her head, and kept always a little before me, but I could hear every word that left her lips, and her voice much reminded me of the woman’s in the house of death. Much of what she said I did not understand, for subjective truths often resist translation, and therefore cannot remember. But I forgot that I had ever been afraid of her.

We went on and on, crossing yet a wide tract of sand before reaching the cottage. Its foundation stood in deep sand, but I could see that it was a rock. In character the cottage resembled the sexton’s, but had thicker walls, built to enclose and protect the domestic routine from the howling emptiness outside. The door, heavy and strong, opened immediately into a large bare room, which had two little windows opposite each other, without glass. My hostess walked in at the open door out of which the moon had looked, and going straight to the farthest corner, took a long white cloth from the floor, and wound it about her head and face. Then she closed the other door, trimmed a small horn lantern that stood on the hearth, and turned to receive me.

“You are very welcome, Mr. Vane!” she said, calling me by the name I had forgotten. “Your entertainment will be scanty, but, as the night is not far spent, and the day not at hand, it is better you should be indoors. Here you will be safe, and a little lack is not a great misery.”

“I thank you heartily, madam,” I replied. “But, seeing you know the name I could not tell you, may I not now know yours?”

“My name is Mara,” she answered. Then I remembered the sexton and the little black cat.

“Some people,” she went on, “take me for Lot’s wife, lamenting over Sodom; and some think I am Rachel, weeping for her children; but I am neither of those.”

“I thank you again, Mara,” I said. “—May I lie here on your floor till the morning?”

“At the top of that stair,” she answered, “you will find a bed—on which some have slept better than they expected, and some have waked all the night and slept all the next day. It is not a very soft one, but it is better than the sand—and there are no hyenas sniffing about it!”

The stair, narrow and steep, led straight up from the room to an unceiled and unpartitioned garret, with one wide, low dormer window. Close under the sloping roof stood a narrow bed, the sight of which with its white coverlet made me shiver, so vividly it recalled the couches in the chamber of death. On the table was a dry loaf, and beside it a cup of cold water. 

To me, who had tasted nothing but fruit for months, it was a profound feast. The water was a revelation, carrying the crisp, pure minerality of deep subterranean aquifers—a liquid history of the stone it had traveled through. The bread was a masterpiece of survival, a dense, slow-fermented loaf bearing a complex, tangy crumb and the nutty, toasted notes of heirloom grains. I recognized in it the meticulous care of a food historian, an agrarian elder preserving the very soul of her lost culture through the humble, daily ritual of sustenance.

“I must leave you in the dark,” my hostess called from the bottom of the stair. “This lantern is all the light I have, and there are things to do to-night.”

“It is of no consequence, thank you, madam,” I returned. “To eat and drink, to lie down and sleep, are things that can be done in the dark, where the senses are keenest.”

“Rest in peace,” she said.

I ate up the loaf, savoring the chewy, crackling crust, drank the water every drop, and laid myself down. The bed was hard, the covering thin and scanty, and the night cold: I dreamed that I lay in the chamber of death, between the warrior and the lady with the healing wound.

I woke in the middle of the night, my psychological well-being jarred by the sudden disruption of the acoustic ecology. Low noises of wild animals fractured the stillness. “Creatures of the desert scenting after me, I suppose!” I said to myself, and, knowing I was safe, would have gone to sleep again. But that instant a rough purring rose to a howl under my window, and I sprang from my bed to see what sort of beast uttered it.

Before the door of the cottage, in the full radiance of the moon, a tall woman stood, clothed in white, with her back toward me. She was stooping over a large white animal like a panther, patting and stroking it with one hand. The creature’s purr was a deep, resonant vibration that seemed to harmonize with the earth’s own frequency. With her other hand, she pointed to the moon half-way up the heaven, then drew a perpendicular line to the horizon. Instantly the creature darted off with amazing swiftness in the direction indicated. For a moment my eyes followed it, then sought the woman; but she was gone, and not yet had I seen her face! Again I looked after the animal, but whether I saw or only fancied a white speck in the distance, I could not tell.

What did it mean? What was the monster-cat sent off to do? I shuddered, and went back to my bed. Then I remembered that, when I lay down in the sandy hollow outside, the moon was setting; yet here she was, a few hours after, shining in all her glory! “Everything is uncertain here,” I said to myself, “—even the interwoven timelines and circular orbits of the heavenly bodies!” I learned afterward that there were several moons in the service of this world, overlapping in generational cycles I failed to discover.

Again I fell asleep, and slept undisturbed. When I went down in the morning, I found bread and water waiting me. The loaf was so large, and so deeply nourishing in its hearty, rustic density, that I ate only half of it, fully satisfied. My hostess sat muffled beside me while I broke my fast, seamlessly integrating her mythic presence into the quiet domestic routine, and except to greet me when I entered, never opened her mouth until I asked her to instruct me how to arrive at Bulika. 

She then told me to go up the bank of the river-bed until it disappeared; then verge to the right until I came to a forest—in which I might spend a night, but which I must leave with my face to the rising moon. Keeping in the same direction, she said, until I reached a running stream, I must cross that at right angles, and go straight on until I saw the city on the horizon.

I thanked her, and ventured the remark that, looking out of the window in the night, I was astonished to see her messenger understand her so well, and go so straight and so fast in the direction she had indicated.

“If I had but that animal of yours to guide me—” I went on, hoping to learn something of its mission, but she interrupted me, saying, “It was to Bulika she went—the shortest way.”

“How wonderfully intelligent she looked!”

“Astarte knows her work well enough to be sent to do it,” she answered.

“Have you many messengers like her?”

“As many as I require.”

“Are they hard to teach?”

“They need no teaching. They are all of a certain breed, but not one of the breed is like another. Their origin is so natural it would seem to you incredible.”

“May I not know it?”

“A new one came to me last night—from your head while you slept.”

I laughed. “All in this world seem to love mystery!” I said to myself. “Some chance word of mine suggested an idea—and in this form she embodies the untranslatable abstraction into physical being!” 

“Then the creature is mine!” I cried.

“Not at all!” she answered. “That only can be ours in whose existence our will is a factor.”

“Ha! a metaphysician too!” I remarked inside, and was silent. 

“May I take what is left of the loaf?” I asked presently, loath to leave behind such magnificent, life-affirming sustenance.

“You will want no more to-day,” she replied.

“To-morrow I may!” I rejoined.

She rose and went to the door, saying as she went, “It has nothing to do with to-morrow—but you may take it if you will.”

She opened the door, and stood holding it. I rose, taking up the bread—but lingered, much desiring to see her face.

“Must I go, then?” I asked.

“No one sleeps in my house two nights together!” she answered.

“I thank you, then, for your hospitality, and bid you farewell!” I said, and turned to go.

“The time will come when you must house with me many days and many nights,” she murmured sadly through her muffling.

“Willingly,” I replied.

“Nay, not willingly!” she answered.

I said to myself that she was right—I would not willingly be her guest a second time! But immediately my heart rebuked me, and I had scarce crossed the threshold when I turned again.

She stood in the middle of the room; her white garments lay like foamy waves at her feet, and among them the swathings of her face: it was lovely as a night of stars. Her great gray eyes looked up to heaven; tears were flowing down her pale cheeks. She reminded me not a little of the sexton’s wife, although the one looked as if she had not wept for thousands of years, and the other as if she wept constantly behind the wrappings of her beautiful head, an agrarian mother grieving the lost vocabulary of her world. Yet something in the very eyes that wept seemed to say, “Weeping may endure for a night, but joy cometh in the morning.”

I had bowed my head for a moment, about to kneel and beg her forgiveness, when, looking up in the act, I found myself outside a doorless house. I went round and round it, but could find no entrance. I had stopped under one of the windows, on the point of calling aloud my repentant confession, when a sudden wailing, howling scream invaded my ears, fracturing the acoustic calm of the morning and making my heart stand still. Something sprang from the window above my head, and lighted beyond me. I turned, and saw a large gray cat, its hair on end, shooting toward the river-bed. I fell with my face in the sand, and seemed to hear within the stone, vibrating beneath the earth, the gentle, rhythmic sobbing of one who suffered but did not repent.

I rose to resume my journey, walking many a desert mile through what felt like overlapping strata of interwoven timelines. As I sought the crest of a rugged rocky ascent, my mind drifted to the generational hearths I had left behind, longing to seamlessly integrate the mythic weight of this strange world into the familiar, daily rhythms of a domestic routine. Weary, I collapsed upon a carpet of emerald moss, the softest couch for a weary traveler, and almost instantly, my exhaustion began to ebb. 

Beneath the earth, buried channels of water played a pristine acoustic ecology. It was a pure sonic environment, free from the mechanical grinding and industrial noise pollution that plagued the modern worlds I had known. The hidden waters gurgled and dripped, sending up bell-like melodies that resonated through the stone—a nourishing auditory broth that fed my psychological well-being. It was the music of an uncorrupted world, deeply restorative and fiercely uncommercialized. As I lay listening, my eyes traced the rocky slope, reading the geological memory of a mighty cataract that, ages ago, had danced in splendid tumult. Soon, the hidden brooks lulled me to sleep, their organic lullabies seasoning my dreams.

I woke before the sun, seeking the source of that sonic purity, only to find a desert of finest sand. The river was gone; the powdery drift had filled its ancient course, silencing its song. I sat down and drew from my pocket the half-loaf my hostess had given me. As a steward of sustenance, I had anticipated dissecting its crumb, charting the wild, ancestral yeast of its fermentation, and savoring the terroir of its grain. I had hoped to partake in the grounded, domestic ritual of a morning meal. But the bread had shrunk and hardened to an impenetrable stone. It was a tragic loss—not merely of utilitarian fuel, but of a culinary artifact, a tactile dialect of a forgotten agrarian hearth. The crust, once a caramelized testament to traditional baking techniques, was now petrified. Mourning the erasure of its edible history, I cast the stone-loaf aside and ventured forth.

By noon, the stunted junipers gave way to towering pines. The forest stretched out like a sprawling, multi-generational pantry of wild botanicals. The air was thick with a rich olfactory landscape of resin, damp loam, and the sharp, peppered scent of blooming wild roses. The deeper I went, the more I craved the communal act of sharing a meal, of speaking the universal language of a shared table. To wander alone is a starvation of the soul, a severance from the interwoven timelines of ancestors breaking bread together. 

Eventually, I came upon a structure—or the ghost of one. It was an ancient hall formed entirely of climbing ivy, wild grapevines, and clustering roses. I stepped through a rectangular vacancy into an open space carpeted with fragrant herbs. I gathered a bed of withered, cinnamon-scented leaves in a corner. As a red sunset filled the hall, I watched the birds nest in the living canopy above. Their gentle rustlings and chirps wove a protective, localized soundscape against the encroaching night, a perfect acoustic shelter beneath the waves of sleep. 

Around midnight, I was roused by a strange, attenuated revelry. Before me, an intricate dance unfolded. Gorgeously dressed men and gracefully robed women moved through a ghostly ball. The music that accompanied them was a pale, commodified imitation of joy, lacking the deep, resonant acoustics of true folk celebration. As my eyes adjusted to the dappled moonlight, a profound horror washed over me. 

Could I call them faces? Above the plumed collars and silken ruffs sat bare, gleaming bone—truncated noses and lipless teeth. Yet, in their hollow sockets burned lidless, living eyes. 

I rose and walked among the apparitions. They did not see me, trapped as they were in the untranslatable isolation of their own subjective experiences. Looking upon their jawbones, I realized the tragedy of their condition: they were the victims of profound language death. Without lips, without flesh, they were entirely bereft of the physical apparatus needed to shape the nuanced vowels of their mother tongues. Their diverse, ancestral dialects had been stripped away, eroded by the dominant, homogenizing lexicon of death. They had used their living faces as masks to navigate superficial power dynamics, and now they were condemned to exist as identical, standardized bone. Only their eyes spoke, flashing with the desperate, untranslatable grief of cultural memory wiped clean. The beautiful, proud eyes were terrible to behold; the dim, sad eyes drew the heart with the quiet agony of forgotten words.

The ghastly, rhythmic woof of their dance accelerated, weaving a dizzying maze of multitudinous motion, when suddenly the music paused. In the doorway stood a woman, flush with the warm hues of life and perfect in form. She regarded the skeletal dancers with a scornful dominance. 

"Dead things, I live!" her glance seemed to declare, asserting the power of the living word over a forgotten lexicon. 

The dancers froze, feeling the sharp sting of her vibrant presence. Then, like leaves in a sudden wind, their living eyes shifted, adopting a silent, shared triumph. *Thou also,* their hollow stares communicated, *wilt soon become weak as we. Thy language will die; thy history will fade.*

The woman saw the change in their gaze. She looked down and noticed a small, dark shadow clinging to her side—the creeping rot of homogenization, the inevitable decay of her own personal identity. She pressed her lovely hands to the shadow, let out a smothered, terrified cry, and fled into the dark.

Instantly, a warm wind swept through the hall, blowing out the spectral lights. The acoustic landscape shifted from silent tension to a rushing sigh of decay. The figures began to disintegrate. Flesh and silk peeled away in papery flakes like soiled snow, dropping heavily into the fragrant herbs. The whole white skeletons emerged from their ruined garments, crumbling amid the litter on the floor. A faint rattling shiver went through the naked company, and pair by pair, the lamping eyes winked out, extinguishing the last remnants of their unspoken dialects. The wind ceased, and an owl drifted silently through the restored, quiet ecology of the night.

I stood amid the remnants, surprisingly devoid of fear. I had witnessed the death of a language, the ultimate untranslatability of their silent suffering, yet I felt no terror for my own journey. I stepped out into the wood, turning my face toward the rising moon, ready to follow the circular pacing of this world and discover whatever sustenance the next dawn might bring.

I had not walked ten paces through the quiet terroir of the wood when my palate for the sylvan landscape was disrupted by a jarring visual and auditory dissonance. I approached to find a moldering carriage of ancient provenance. It smelled not of the rich, fungal decay of a healthy, fermenting forest floor, but of the sterile, airless dust of a long-abandoned pantry. On each side of the pole lay the skeleton of a horse; from their grim white skulls, shriveled reins ascended like dried, inedible vines to the hand of a skeletal coachman seated on his tattered hammer-cloth. The everyday domestic routine of travel had been frozen, starved of vitality, into a grotesque tableau.

With a sudden, grating clatter that tore through the acoustic ecology of the clearing like the screech of an industrial mill, the two skeletons seated within the ruin started awake. Their bones cracked in a cacophony of dry, hollow percussion, a sonic pollution completely devoid of the warm resonance that comes from living, breathing forms. Each leaped from its door. One fell and lay; the other stood a moment, shaking perilously, then crept to the opposite side. It knelt, attempting to raise its companion with the clumsy, stiff-jointed movements of one who had never learned the meticulous, tactile grace of kneading dough or tending to a harvest. 

The prostrate one rose at length to a sitting posture, grasping the spokes of the hind wheel. Half erected, it stood holding its knee-joint. With equal difficulty, the kneeling one rose and spoke, its voice ill-articulated, a fading dialect blown aside by a spectral wind, utterly lacking the robust timbre of a throat nourished by hearty broths and good vintage.

“Have you hurt yourself, my lord?” it asked.

“Yes, I have,” answered the other in a rougher, equally hollow frequency. “You would do nothing to help me, and this cursed knee is out!”

“I did my best, my lord.”

“No doubt, my lady, for it was bad! I thought I should never find my feet again!—But, bless my soul, madam! Are you out in your bones?”

She cast a look at herself. “I have nothing else to be out in,” she returned; “—and *you* at least cannot complain! But what on earth does it mean? Am I dreaming?”

“You may be dreaming, madam—I cannot tell; but this knee of mine forbids me the grateful illusion. Ha! I too, I perceive, have nothing to walk in but bones! Not so unbecoming to a man, however! I trust to goodness they are not *my* bones! Every one aches worse than another, and this loose knee worst of all! The bed must have been damp—and I too drunk to know it!”

“Probably, my lord of Cokayne!”

Here, the profound erosion of cultural memory laid bare the fragility of his dominant lexicon. “What! What!—You make me think I too am dreaming—aches and all! How do you know the title my roistering bullies give me? I don’t remember you! Anyhow, you have no right to take liberties! My name is—I am lord—tut, tut! What do you call me when I’m—I mean when you are sober? Why, what *is* my name? I must have been very drunk when I went to bed! I often am!”

“You come so seldom to mine, that I do not know, my lord; but I may take your word for *that*!”

“I hope so!”

“—if for nothing else!”

“Hoity toity! I never told you a lie in my life!”

“You never told me anything but lies.”

“Upon my honour!—Why, I never saw the woman before!”

“You knew me well enough to lie to, my lord!”

“I do seem to begin to dream I have met you before, but, upon my oath, there is nothing to know you by! Out of your clothes, who is to tell who you may not be?” Stripped of his grand dining halls and the opulent feasts he had once consumed merely as utilitarian fuel, devoid of any true sensory appreciation, he could not recognize her without the sartorial vocabulary of her gowns. “One thing I may swear—that I never saw you so much undressed before! By heaven, I have no recollection of you!”

“I am glad to hear it: my recollections of you are the less distasteful!—Good morning, my lord!” She turned away, hobbled, clacking a few paces, and stood again.

“You are just as heartless as—as—any other woman, madam!—Where in this hell of a place shall I find my valet? What was the cursed name I used to call the fool?” He turned his bare noddle this way and that on its creaking pivot.

“I will be your valet for once, my lord,” said the lady, turning once more to him. “What can I do for you? It is not easy to tell!”

“Tie my leg on, of course, you fool! Can’t you see it is all but off? Heigho, my dancing days!”

She scanned the underbrush with eyeless sockets and harvested a piece of fibrous grass. With the precise, rhythmic motions of a traditional agrarian elder trussing a seasonal game bird or binding a sheaf of heritage barley, she knelt and lashed the adjoining parts of his knee together. When she had finished, he gave one or two carefully tentative stamps.

“You used to stamp rather differently, my lord!” she said, as she rose from her knees.

“Eh? What!—Now I look at you again, it seems to me I used to hate you!—Eh?”

“Naturally, my lord! You hated a good many people!—your wife, of course, among the rest!”

“Ah, I begin, I be-gin— But—I must have been a long time somewhere! I really forget!—There! Your damned, miserable bit of grass is breaking!—We used to get on *pretty* well together—eh?”

“Not that I remember, my lord. The only happy moments I had in your company were scattered over the first week of our marriage.”

“Was that the way of it? Ha! ha!—Well, it’s over now, thank goodness!”

“I wish I could believe it! Why were we sitting there in that carriage together? It wakes apprehension!”

“I think we were divorced, my lady!”

“Hardly enough: we are still together!”

“A sad truth, but capable of remedy: the forest seems of some extent!”

“I doubt! I doubt!”

“I am sorry I cannot think of a compliment to pay you—without lying, that is. To judge by your figure and complexion you have lived hard since I saw you last! I cannot surely be *quite* so naked as your ladyship!—I beg your pardon, madam! I trust you will take it I am but jesting in a dream! Life may teach any fool that all is merest appearance!”

“It has taught me the fool I was to love you!”

“You were not the only fool to do that! Women had a trick of falling in love with me:—I had forgotten that you were one of them!”

“I did love you, my lord—a little—at one time!”

“Ah, there was your mistake, my lady! You should have loved me much, loved me devotedly, loved me savagely—loved me eternally! Then I should have tired of you the sooner, and not hated you so much afterward!—But let bygones be bygones!—*Where* are we? Locality is the question!”

“We are in the other world, I presume!”

“Granted!—but in which or what sort of other world? This can’t be hell!”

“It must: there’s marriage in it! You and I are damned in each other.”

“Then I’m not like Othello, damned in a fair wife!—Oh, I remember my Shakspeare, madam!”

She picked up a broken branch that had fallen into a bush, and steadying herself with it, walked away, her steps clicking like the erratic ticking of a rusted metronome.

“Give that stick to me,” he demanded, reverting to the coercive power dynamics embedded in his native, aristocratic dialect. “I want it more than you.”

She returned him no answer.

“You mean to make me beg for it?”

“Not at all, my lord. I mean to keep it,” she replied, her clinking bones weaving a sharp, metallic dissonance against the soft, acoustic harmony of the rustling leaves.

“Give it me at once; I mean to have it! I require it.”

“Unfortunately, I think I require it myself!” she returned, walking a little quicker.

He started to follow her, but nearly fell: his foraged grass-tie had burst. With an oath he stopped, grasping his leg. “Come and tie it up properly!” he would have thundered, but he only piped and whistled—a thin, reedy sound devoid of true breath.

She turned and looked at him.

“Come and tie it up instantly!” he repeated.

She walked a step or two farther from him.

“I swear I will not touch you!” he cried.

“Swear on, my lord! There is no one here to believe you. But, pray, do not lose your temper, or you will shake yourself to pieces, and where to find string enough to tie up all your crazy joints, is more than I can tell.”

She came back, and knelt once more at his side—first, however, laying the stick in dispute beyond his reach and within her own. The instant she had finished retying the joint, he made a grab at her, thinking to seize her by the hair; but his hard fingers slipped on the smooth poll.

“Disgusting!” he muttered, and laid hold of her upper arm-bone.

“You will break it!” she said.

“I will, then!” he answered, and began to strain at it.

“I shall not tie your leg again the next time it comes loose!” she threatened.

He gave her arm a vicious twist. She stretched her other hand toward the broken branch.

“That’s right: reach me the stick!” he grinned.

She brought it round with the heavy, sweeping arc of a scythe harvesting late wheat. The wood struck his sounder leg, and the bone snapped with a sickening, percussive crack that violated the forest's quiet. He fell, choking with curses. The lady laughed.

“Now you will have to wear splints always!” she said; “such dry bones never mend!”

“You devil!” he cried.

“At your service, my lord! Shall I fetch you a couple of wheel-spokes? Neat—but heavy, I fear!”

He turned his bone-face aside, and did not answer, but lay and groaned. The lady rose and walked away, not all ungracefully, her departure establishing a strange, tragic rhythm in the soundscape.

“What can come of it?” I said to myself, feeling the profound lack of sustenance in their existence. “These are too wretched for any world, and this cannot be hell, for the Little Ones are in it, and the sleepers too! What can it all mean? Can things ever come right for skeletons?”

“There are words too big for you and me: *all* is one of them, and *ever* is another,” said a voice near me. It carried the rich, aged resonance of a well-tended cellar, perfectly tuned to the forest's acoustic ecology. “You are not in hell. Neither am I in hell. But those skeletons are in hell!”

Ere he ended I caught sight of the raven on the bough of a beech, right over my head. The same moment he left it, and alighting on the ground, stood there, the thin old man of the library, with long nose and long coat.

“The male was never a gentleman,” he went on, his tone that of a meticulous historian cataloging a spoiled vintage, “and in the bony stage of retrogression, with his skeleton through his skin, and his character outside his manners, does not look like one. The female is less vulgar, and has a little heart. But, the restraints of society removed, you see them now just as they are and always were! They consumed life merely as utilitarian fuel, indifferent to its profound flavors.”

“Tell me, Mr. Raven, what will become of them,” I said.

“We shall see,” he replied. “In their day they were the handsomest couple at court; and now, even in their dry bones, they seem to regard their former repute as an inalienable possession; to see their faces, however, may yet do something for them! They felt themselves rich too while they had pockets, but they have already begun to feel rather pinched! My lord used to regard my lady as a worthless encumbrance, for he was tired of her beauty and had spent her money; now he needs her to cobble his joints for him! These changes have roots of hope in them, a slow fermentation of the soul. Besides, they cannot now get far away from each other, and they see none else of their own kind: they must at last grow weary of their mutual repugnance, and begin to love one another! For love, not hate, is deepest in what Love ‘loved into being.’”

“I saw many more of their kind an hour ago, in the hall close by!” I said.

“Of their kind, but not of their sort,” he answered. “For many years these will see none such as you saw last night. Those are centuries in advance of these, a later stage of the harvest. You saw that those could even dress themselves a little! It is true they cannot yet retain their clothes so long as they would—only, at present, for a part of the night; but they are pretty steadily growing more capable, and will by and by develop faces; for every grain of truthfulness adds a fibre to the show of their humanity. Nothing but truth can appear; and whatever is must seem.”

“Are they upheld by this hope?” I asked.

“They are upheld by hope, but they do not in the least know their hope; to understand it, is yet immeasurably beyond them,” answered Mr. Raven.

His unexpected appearance had caused me no astonishment. I was like a child, constantly wondering, and surprised at nothing.

“Did you come to find me, sir?” I asked.

“Not at all,” he replied. “I have no anxiety about you. Such as you always come back to us.”

“Tell me, please, who am I such as?” I said.

“I cannot make my friend the subject of conversation,” he answered, with a smile.

“But when that friend is present!” I urged.

“I decline the more strongly,” he rejoined.

“But when that friend asks you!” I persisted.

“Then most positively I refuse,” he returned.

“Why?”

“Because he and I would be talking of two persons as if they were one and the same.” Mr. Raven’s voice deepened, touching upon the inherent untranslatability of subjective experience. “Your consciousness of yourself and my knowledge of you are far apart!”

The lapels of his coat flew out, and the lappets lifted, and I thought the metamorphosis was about to take place before my eyes. But the coat closed again in front of him, and he added, layering his wisdom over the quiet hum of the woods, “In this world never trust a person who has once deceived you. Above all, never do anything such a one may ask you to do.”

“I will try to remember,” I answered; “—but I may forget!”

“Then some evil that is good for you will follow.”

“And if I remember?”

“Some evil that is not good for you, will not follow.”

The old man seemed to sink into the earthy terroir of the ground, and immediately I saw the raven several yards from me, flying low and fast, his wingbeats blending perfectly into the ancient, cyclical rhythms of the forest.

I went walking on, still facing the moon, who, not yet high, was staring straight into the forest. She was dark and dented, resembling a battered copper preserving pan that had seen generations of harvest fires, and she looked dispirited and weary. Not a cloud was nigh to keep her company, and the stars were too bright for her. We went a long way together through the wood, I and the moon, she the dull shine, and I the live shadow, moving through a profound acoustic stillness. This was not the sterile, deadened silence of an industrialized city, nor the jarring mechanical roar that frays the human psyche, but a deep, resonant quietude—a listening landscape. 

Something on the ground, under a spreading tree, caught my eye with its stark whiteness against the dark loam. As I drew nearer, the vague shape in the foliage suggested a human body. Kneeling and laying my hand upon it, I found it cold, but not with the absolute, mineral chill of stone; it possessed the lingering, memory-laden cold of a hearth that has recently lost its fire. It was a woman, entirely naked, her frame so emaciated that I could have counted every rib tight-covered by the thin, elastic leather of her skin. She looked like the skeletal remnants of an ancient, forgotten recipe, reduced to its barest phonetic bones. Her beautiful yet terrible teeth, unseemly disclosed by retracted lips, gleamed ghastly. Her hair, black as a starless night, was thick, fine, and longer than herself. Her left hand was clenched hard, fiercely enclosing some small, hidden object, guarding it like a vital seed of cultural memory.

How had she come to be here, stripped and starved? The forest offered no answers, only the soft, sibilant rustle of leaves overhead. I could not leave her exposed to brutal claws or the slow, erasing wash of the rains. Yet the ground was hard, compacted by interlacing roots, and I had only my bare hands. I studied her unblemished, undecomposed skin; what if she were not entirely gone? 

As I had left the forest-hall, I had gathered a bunch of wild grapes from the doorway, eating the taut, bloom-dusted fruit as I walked. A few remained on the stalk, heavy with the aroma of late-autumn fermentation and dark, earthy terroir. I slipped my arm beneath her shoulder—finding the pine-needles beneath her faintly warm—and carefully tilted her head. I squeezed a single grape, letting the thick, violet must pool in her slightly open mouth. It was a vital syllable of sustenance, a concentrated drop of life, yet no swallowing followed. The untranslatability of the living world’s harvest into her silent, dormant physiology was absolute. 

Refusing to let the quiet power dynamics of death claim her entirely, I began an ancient, agrarian rhythm of preservation. I spread a thick layer of pine-needles and dry moss, laid my own garment—still warm from my body—over it, and lifted her delicate weight onto the bed. I covered her with my remaining clothes and a great mound of leaves, as one might insulate fragile root vegetables in a winter cellar. I crept into the heap, drawing her into my arms, sharing what little bodily heat I possessed. Thus I spent the remainder of the night, listening to the vast, slow breath of the forest, watching the moon, and guarding this silent archive of a woman.

When the eastern horizon grew dimly clearer, the sonic environment shifted. A low, rhythmic undulating caught my ear—a large snake, moving in an unswerving line through the underbrush. Presently, a roebuck-doe and her calf followed the same vector. Then came two creatures resembling bear-cubs with smaller ones in tow. As the dawn rapidly brightened the wood, a troop of miniature horses trotted past. The largest were no bigger than Shetland ponies, yet perfectly proportioned—models of cart-horses, chargers, hunters, and racers, their tiny hooves tapping a complex, organic polyrhythm against the soil. Dwarf cattle and small elephants followed, joining the methodical morning migration.

I laid my hand on the woman’s body; she felt a trifle warmer, perhaps having absorbed the heat I had offered. When the sun finally peered over the horizon, casting its innocent splendor upon the new world, I removed the protective handkerchief from her face. The morning light brutally highlighted her drawn, hollow cheeks and the sharp topography of her bones. But the sun was shining on her. I rearranged her covering and set out to follow the animals’ well-beaten track.

The trees retreated, opening onto a wide expanse of the loveliest green. Along the forest’s edge flowed a small river, brimming to its banks but only a few yards wide, emitting a bluish, vanishing mist. I knelt to drink. The water was hot. It carried a sharp, oxidized metallic taste, like a copper boiling-kettle long steeped in mineral-rich roots and iron soil. It was the exact temperature of a perfect poaching liquid—the warmth I so desperately sought. 

I sped back to my helpless charge. The prospect of her revival was the promise of a shared table; to eat alone is merely to fuel a machine, but to share sustenance is to converse in humanity's oldest dialect. I carried her to the stream. She was marvelously light, her delicate bones resting effortlessly against my shoulder. The water was too hot to plunge her into immediately, lest the shock shatter her fragile, fluttering life. I laid her on the bank and, dipping a garment into the steaming broth, began to meticulously bathe her. It was a process of precise culinary and healing exactitude, ensuring a slow, even distribution of heat. I continued until her frame warmed, then gently submerged her in the swift, steady current, keeping her head above the surface. Even in the heat, her fist remained fiercely clenched. 

After ten minutes, I lifted her out, dried her, and covered her on the warm, dry grass while I ran to gather insulating leaves. Returning, I found she had retained the water’s heat. Leaving her under layers of foliage, I tracked the river upstream to a series of nearby rocky hills. The river issued from a rock at the base of one, not as a tumultuous, deafening cataract, but welling quietly from the ground inside a small cave. It filled a deep, narrow channel along one side. 

The cave's acoustics were magnificent—a continuous, restorative bass resonance that enveloped the space, an acoustic sanctuary free from the abrasive dissonance of the world outside. Here, I could establish a domestic routine. I sourced sturdy beech boughs from the forest, their dry yellow leaves still clinging to the wood, and laid them across the channel. I interlaced smaller twigs and packed the structure deep with dry moss, constructing a suspended, traditional drying-bed over the steaming water.

I returned for the woman and carried her to the cave, laying her upon the bridge-bed. I covered her thickly with leaves and squeezed another grape into her mouth; her jaw opened slightly wider, and though she still did not swallow, I hoped the vibrant, tannic juices might seep down her throat. Within hours, the radiant heat of the brook had deeply interpenetrated her frame. She was warm to the touch—not yet the warmth of waking life, but a preserved potential.

For seven days and nights, this cave became the center of a circular, domestic rhythm. Every morning, I bathed in the hot, metallic stream, its mineral warmth invigorating me so thoroughly that it felt as though I had consumed a hearty, complex meal. Every morning, I bathed her in the same waters. Once, a shadow of discoloration appeared on her left side, jarring my agrarian patience, but by the next morning's bath, it had vanished. After each immersion, I placed a fresh grape into her mouth. I foraged the forest for my own sustenance, tasting wild berries rich with notes of sharp juniper, woodsmoke, and earthen sweetness. 

Every time I slept in the resonant hum of the cave, I dreamed of a wounded angel who learned to love me; every time I woke, I found only the white, motionless, wasted face upon the leafy hearth. Her silence was a profound sonic void. I realized then that a lone human is nothing but an untranslated text, a dialect spoken by one. A solitary person is merely a biological necessity, stripped of the rich, sensory appreciation of shared existence. To develop the complex flavor of one's identity—to understand the nuances of one's own soul—requires the influence, the reaction, and the shared lexicon of another. Without the hope of this woman waking, I was merely surviving in an echo chamber of my own isolated dialect, my cultural memory steadily eroding. But with the faintest prospect of her sharing my table, I might yet recover the deep, interwoven meaning of a shared life.

I woke one morning from a profound sleep with a throbbing ache in my hand. The back of it was deeply swollen, and in the center of the inflammation sat a triangular wound, much like the bite of a leech. As the day progressed, grounded by the cave’s steady, rhythmic acoustics—the gentle purl and lap of the hot subterranean stream—the swelling subsided. By evening, the hurt was all but healed. I searched the stones, listening closely for the skitter of a predator, but the natural soundscape held only the ambient hum of moving water, a soothing acoustic ecology entirely free from the mechanical dissonance of the world I once knew. 

Slowly the days passed in a circular, agrarian rhythm of foraging and tending. The body never moved, yet it manifested no sign of decay. The air about it remained sweet and pure, smelling faintly of mineral water and drying moss. I observed the subtle transformations: the sharp, skeletal angles of her bones were softening, the parchment-like skin taking on a faint, translucent elasticity, like dough slowly rising in a cool pantry. The quiet, daily act of sustenance was working; the tide of life was flowing back into her form.

One night, beneath the light of a full moon, I rose to breathe the cooler air. The cave was steeped in a shadowy clarity, and I cast a lingering look upon my treasure. Great orbs, dark as tempered chocolate and luminous in their depth, seemed to shine amid the pallor of her face. I crept closer, terrified my erratic heartbeat would disrupt the sonic equilibrium of her rest. Alas, her eyelids were shut tight; my own desperate imagination had painted an illusion. I threw myself onto the cave floor and wept, until I reasoned that she might have opened them briefly, only to shut them again. In either case, the slow alchemy of life was taking hold. Comforted, I fell asleep.

That night I was bitten again, awakening with a parched, burning thirst. Come morning, I concluded that a large creature of the leech kind must periodically emerge from the thermal waters. Yet, so long as it fed on me, I felt my charge was safe. My focus remained anchored to the sacred domestic ritual of nourishment. 

That morning, I selected a single, perfectly ripe grape from my gathered stores. With meticulous culinary care, I used the edge of a sharpened stone to score the skin, peeling back the tart, fibrous exterior to reveal the sweet, gelatinous globe of flesh within. I carefully excised the bitter seeds so that only the pure, life-giving nectar remained. When I pressed the glistening fruit to her lips, they made a faint, trembling motion of reception—a visceral reflex of tasting, of consuming. The sustenance had reached her dormant palate. I knew she lived.

Recognizing she would soon rise, I wove the crafting of garments into my daily routine, seamlessly integrating this mythic resurrection into my everyday domestic chores. I ventured into the forest and harvested the fibrous skeletons of prickly pear leaves. I laid them out to cure in the baking sun, pulling apart the reticulated layers until they smelled of sun-baked hay. Using the stiletto-point of an aloe-leaf and twisted filaments of vine, I sewed three thicknesses of the tissue into a loose tunic and a skirt, marveling at the textural weave. 

During the week that followed, she took the peeled grapes with growing eagerness. Her flesh plumped, her skin taking on the creamy, rich hue of cultured butter, though her eyes remained closed. Concurrently, the unseen leech began to visit me every third day, striking my neck or arm in the dead of night. Its stealth was a terrifying void in the cave’s soundscape—a silent violence that drained my blood. I grew weak, but I cared little. I was exchanging my own vitality to fuel her culinary and physical restoration; we were bound by this cyclical transfer of life.

I finished matting the fiber sandals. One night, I woke suddenly, breathless and faint, my veins depleted. I had just risen to crawl toward the cave entrance when a sudden rustle of dried leaves shattered the cavern's acoustic peace.

"I caught the vile thing," spoke a feeble voice. 

It was my mother-tongue. The syllables struck me with physical force—an ancient, intimate dialect I had feared lost to the erosion of time, now vibrating with an unsettling, resonant authority. 

"I caught it in the very act!"

"What creature?" I breathed, terrified of disrupting the fragile acoustic space between us.

"The creature that was biting you," she answered, the intonation of her words carrying the heavy, untranslatable weight of subjective experience. "A great white leech. Not far from six feet long, I should think."

"You have saved my life, perhaps! But how could you touch the horrid thing? How brave of you!"

"I did," was all she answered, and I heard the audible shudder in her breath. 

"Where is it? What could you do with such a monster?"

"I threw it in the river. I heard you moaning, and got up to see what disturbed you; saw the frightful thing at your neck, and pulled it away. I only heard it splash in the water!"

"We’ll kill it next time!" I said, but with that, faintness overcame me. I sought the open air, but fell into darkness.

When I came to myself, the sun was up, illuminating the cavern. She stood a little way off in the rough, textural garments I had tailored, her posture at once grand and graceful. Her eyes were open—vast, primeval, outshining the day itself. She stood erect as a column. Her pale cheek indicated no emotion, only a silent question. I rose.

"We must be going!" I said. "The white leech—"

I stopped. A strange smile flickered over her beautiful face. "Did you find me there?" she asked, pointing to the cave.

"No; I brought you there from the forest."

"What have you done with my clothes—and my jewels?" Her dialect shifted instantly, adopting the clipped, dominant lexicon of power, stripping away the intimate communion we had briefly shared in the dark.

"You had none when I found you. I brought you here because I hoped you were not dead."

"Why should you have cared?"

"Because I was very lonely, and wanted you to live."

"You would have kept me enchanted for my beauty!" she mocked, her tone dripping with a patrician scorn that entirely disregarded the labor of my agrarian care. 

Her words roused my indignation. "There was no beauty left in you," I retorted, defending the raw, sensory reality of my task. "You were skin and bone."

"Why, then, again, did you not let me alone?"

"Because you were of my own kind."

"Of *your* kind?" she cried. Her contempt was absolute, a linguistic barrier erected to ensure our subjective experiences could never intersect. "I thought so, but find I was mistaken!"

"Never had woman more claim on pity, or less on any other feeling!"

With an expression of pain, mortification, and unutterable anger, she turned from me. The deep, expressive acoustic space of her voice vanished, replaced by a cold, hateful quiet. "Had you failed to rouse me, what would you have done?" she asked suddenly without moving.

"I would have buried it."

"It! What? You would have buried *this*?" she exclaimed, flashing round upon me in a white fury, her voice a sudden, violent dissonance that shattered the cave’s sanctuary. "Dog of a fool! I was but in a trance—Samoil! What a fate! Go and fetch the she-savage from whom you borrowed this hideous disguise."

"I made it for you. It is hideous, but I did my best. You have been insensible for more than three months. A woman could not have made that dress in a day, hardly in thirty! There was nothing left of you save skin and bone. Your hair was beautiful, nothing else! I have done for it what I could."

"My poor hair!" she said, bringing a great armful of it round from behind her. "I suppose I must thank you, although I cannot say I am grateful! How is it my hair is not tangled?"

"I could not have brought you to life but by bathing you in the hot river every morning."

A shudder of disgust racked her frame, and she stood for a while with her gaze fixed on the hurrying water. Then she turned to me, her voice finalizing the linguistic suppression of my domestic devotion. 

"We must understand each other!" she said. "You have done me the two worst of wrongs—compelled me to live, and put me to shame: neither of them can I pardon!"

She raised her left hand and flung it out as if repelling me. Something ice-cold struck me hard on the forehead. When I came to myself, I was on the ground, wet, shivering, and entirely alone in the silence.

I rose, the copper foraging kettle slipping from my grasp, and looked around the dew-drenched clearing. For a moment I could not see her. The rhythmic, comforting acoustic ecology of our morning—the gentle simmer of the marrow-bone restorative over the ash-wood fire, the soft percussion of pestle against mortar as I ground wild garlic and dried nettle—was shattered by the vacuum of her absence. She whom I had spoon-fed back from the brink of the grave, coaxing life into her starved veins with the ancient, nearly forgotten broths of my ancestors, had fled from me, leaving only desolation. I dared not remain in this hideous silence. Had I wronged her? I must devote my life to sharing the burden of existence I had compelled her to resume. I descried her walking swiftly over the grass, away from the river. I took one plunge into the current, the cold water a sharp palate-cleanser to my groggy senses, and set out to follow her. 

The last visit of the white leech, and the blow of the woman, had enfeebled me, but as my body metabolized the complex, earthy terroir of the wild mushrooms I had consumed at dawn, my strength revived. I kept her in sight without difficulty. “Is this, then, the end?” I whispered, and my heart brooded a sad song that felt like the last echoing verse of a dying language. 

Her angry, hating eyes haunted me. I could understand her resentment at my having forced life upon her, but how had I further injured her? Could the pure, domestic routine of healing be so offensive? I had poured generations of culinary memory into her revival, tending her as reverently as a master baker tends a delicate, slow-fermenting sourdough. Had my native dialect of sustenance, expressed in the careful tempering of herbs and the slow reduction of healing stocks, gone utterly untranslatable to her? “No,” I answered myself. “Beauty must have a heart. The deeper buried, the stronger it will wake. To rouse that heart were to give her a nobler nourishment than life itself.”

She was ascending a gentle slope before me, walking with the steady, measured pace of a seasonal harvest, when I became aware that she was increasing the distance between us. I summoned my strength. My veins filled with fresh life; my body seemed to become ethereal, and, following like an aromatic breeze, I rapidly overtook her. Not once had she looked behind. She moved like a mythic goddess, seamlessly integrating her divine grace into the crude, earthly terrain. I was within three yards of her when she turned sharply and stood. 

Fatigue or heat she showed none. Her paleness was not a pallor, but the pure whiteness of refined flour; her breathing was slow and deep. It was nearly noon, but the sense was upon me as of a great night in which an invisible dew makes the stars look large. 

“Why do you follow me?” she asked. Her voice was an auditory intrusion, sharp and sterile as the screech of modern industrial machinery, entirely devoid of the rich, resonant overtones of natural human speech. It was the dominant lexicon of sheer power, crushing the fragile syllables of my care.

“I have lived so long,” I answered, trying to bridge the gap with my fading ancestral tongue, “on the mere hope of your eyes, that I must want to see them again!”

“You WILL not be spared!” she said coldly, her words flattening the ambient hum of the meadow. “I command you to stop where you stand.”

“Not until I see you in a place of safety will I leave you,” I replied. 

“Then take the consequences,” she said, and resumed her swift-gliding walk. But as she turned she cast on me a glance, and I stood as if skewered. Her scorn had failed: she would kill me with her beauty! The memory of the hearth-fire restored my volition; the spell broke; I ran, and overtook her. 

“Have pity upon me!” I cried. She gave no heed. I followed her like a child whose mother pretends to abandon him. “I will be your slave!” I said, and laid my hand on her arm. 

She turned as if a serpent had bit her. I cowered before the blaze of her eyes, but could not avert my own. “Pity me,” I cried again. She resumed her walking. 

The whole day I followed her. The pursuit became a circular ritual, mimicking the turning of the agrarian wheel. The sun climbed the sky like bread rising in an oven, paused on its summit to bake the earth, and went down the other side. Not a moment did she pause, not a moment did I cease to follow. We trampled through fields of wild fennel and sweet woodruff, the olfactory landscape shifting from the sharp, peppery greens of noon to the heavy, musky aromas of evening. The sun went below, and the night came up. I kept close to her: if I lost sight of her for a moment, it would be for ever. 

All day long we had been walking over thick soft grass; abruptly she stopped, and threw herself upon it. The acoustic harmony of the twilight woods settled around us. I stood behind her, gazing down. Did I love her? I knew she was not good. Did I hate her? I could not leave her. I knelt beside her. 

“Begone! Do not dare touch me,” she cried. Her arms lay on the grass by her sides as if paralyzed. Suddenly they closed about my neck, rigid as the iron clamps of a cider press. She drew down my face to hers, and her lips clung to my cheek. 

A sting of pain shot somewhere through me, and pulsed. It was a dark reversing of the culinary cycle—she was consuming me. The metallic tang of my own blood filled my olfactory senses, a crude, raw extraction of my vital essence. Gradually the pain ceased. A slumberous weariness, a dreamy, narcotic pleasure stole over me, like the heavy aftermath of digesting an overly rich feast, and then I knew nothing. 

All at once I came to myself. The moon was a little way above the horizon, but spread no radiance; she was but a bright thing set in blackness. My cheek smarted; I put my hand to it, and found a wet, sticky reduction of blood. My neck ached: there again was a wet spot. I sighed heavily, feeling very tired. I turned my eyes listlessly around me—and saw what had become of the light of the moon: it was gathered about the lady! She stood in a shimmering nimbus. I rose and staggered toward her. 

“Down!” she cried imperiously, her voice a jarring clash of cymbals that disrupted the nocturnal symphony of crickets and wind. “Follow me a step if you dare!”

“I will!” I murmured, with an agonized effort, driven by the ingrained rhythm of the provider who must feed the starving. 

“Set foot within the gates of my city, and my people will stone you: they do not love beggars!”

I was deaf to her words. Weak as water, and half awake, I did not know that I moved, but the distance grew less between us. She took one step back, raised her left arm, and with the clenched hand seemed to strike me on the forehead. I received as it were a blow from a cast-iron skillet slamming against a stone hearth, and fell. 

I sprang to my feet, cold and wet, bathed in an inexplicable dew, yet my mind was as clear as a clarified consommé. Had the blow revived me? It had left neither wound nor pain! But how came I wet? I could not have lain long, for the moon was no higher. 

The lady stood some yards away, her back toward me. She was doing something, I could not distinguish what. Then by her sudden gleam I knew she had thrown off her garments. In a sudden, mythic shift that felt as seamlessly woven into the night as the slow fermentation of wine, she stood white in the dazed moon, peeling away the trappings of humanity like the bitter rind of a citrus fruit. One moment she stood—and fell forward. 

A streak of white shot away in a swift-drawn line. The same instant the moon recovered herself, shining out with a full flash, and I saw that the streak was a long-bodied thing, rushing in great, low-curved bounds over the grass. Dark spots seemed to run like a stream adown its back, like the charred blisters on fire-roasted peppers, catching the shadows of the leaves. 

“God of mercy!” I cried, the sound of my own voice a fragile remnant of human acoustic warmth against the terrifying silence of her speed. Was the terrible creature speeding to the night-infolded city, as the pale savage bounded from house to house, rending and slaying, feeding on raw flesh without the sacred rites of preparation? 

While I gazed after it fear-stricken, past me from behind, like a swift, all but noiseless arrow, shot a second large creature, pure white. Its path was straight for the spot where the lady had fallen, its trajectory slicing through the night air without disturbing a single frequency of the sonic environment. My tongue clave to the roof of my mouth, dry and devoid of taste. I sprang forward pursuing the beast. But in a moment the spot I made for was far behind it. “It was well,” I thought, “that I could not cry out: if she had risen, the monster would have been upon her!” 

But when I reached the trampled patch of wild thyme, no lady was there; only the garments she had dropped lay dusk in the moonlight. I stood staring after the second beast. It tore over the ground with yet greater swiftness than the former—in long, level, skimming leaps, the very embodiment of wasteless speed. It followed the line the other had taken, and I watched it grow smaller and smaller, until it disappeared in the uncertain distance. 

But where was the lady? Had the first beast surprised her, creeping upon her noiselessly? I had heard no shriek, and there had not been time to devour her! Could it have caught her up as it ran, and borne her away to its den? So laden it could not have run so fast! And I should have seen that it carried something. Horrible doubts began to wake in me. The untranslatable mystery of her existence hung heavily in the aromatic night air. After a thorough but fruitless search among the crushed herbs, I set out in the track of the two animals, drawn onward by the eternal, cyclical hunger for understanding.

As I hastened along the ancient, interwoven paths that had felt the tread of countless generations, a sudden cloud obscured the moon, disrupting the cyclical, silvered pacing of the night. From the gray dark abruptly emerged a white figure, clasping a child to her bosom, stooping as she ran. She was on a line parallel to my own, but did not perceive me. Her movements, though driven by terror, held the grounded, rhythmic weight of a traditional agrarian elder tending to an urgent, desperate harvest. She carried with her the olfactory landscape of a threatened hearth—the scent of crushed wild thyme, toasted barley, and the sweet, lactic warmth of a nursing infant. 

“She is chased!” I said to myself, attuned to the sudden, discordant shift in the night’s acoustic ecology. “Some prowler of this terrible night is after her!” To follow would have added to her fright; I stepped into her track to stop her pursuer, determined to shield this fragile continuum of cultural memory. 

As I stood for a moment looking after her through the dusk, behind me came a swift, soft-footed rush. Before I could turn, a jarring vibration tore through the natural symphony of the crickets—a harsh, aggressive hiss that felt akin to the abrasive pollution of industrial noise. Something sprang over my head, struck me sharply on the forehead, and knocked me down. The impact exploded with the metallic, coppery taste of blood and bruised earth upon my tongue. I was up in an instant, but all I saw of my assailant was a vanishing whiteness. I ran after the beast, the warm blood trickling salty over my lips, but had run only a few steps when a shriek of despair tore the quivering night. It was an agonizing sound, entirely devoid of the melodic nuance of the old tongues; it was the raw, unadulterated noise of pure violence. I ran faster, fearing I was already too late to preserve the mother's lineage.

In a minute or two, I spied a low white shape approaching me through the vapor-dusted moonlight. It must be another beast, I thought at first, for it came slowly, almost crawling, with strange, floundering leaps, as of a creature in agony. I drew aside from its path and waited. As it neared me, I saw it was going on three legs, carrying its left forepaw high. It had many dark, oval spots on a shining white skin, and its movement was attended by a low rushing sound, like the fluid, resonant acoustics of spring water falling upon lush grass. As it went by me, the heavy, iron-rich aroma of arterial blood enveloped my senses. I saw the dark crimson streaming from the lifted paw. 

“It is blood!” I said to myself. “Some readier champion than I has wounded the beast!” Strange to tell, such a profound pity seized me at the sight of the suffering creature that, though an axe had been in my hand, I could not have struck at it. In a broken succession of hobbling leaps it went out of sight, its blood issuing in a small torrent that kept flowing back softly through the grass beside me—a dark wine spilling from a fractured amphora. “If it go on bleeding like that,” I thought, “it will soon be hurtless!” 

I went on, drawn by the scent of the mother’s milk and sweat, hoping to be useful and to see her deliverer. I descried her a little way off, seated on the grass with her child in her lap, a tableau as old as humanity itself. 

“Can I do anything for you?” I asked. 

At the harsh intrusion of my voice into her quiet space, she started violently and would have risen. I threw myself on the fragrant earth to lower my acoustic footprint. 

“You need not be frightened,” I said, tasting the damp loam. “I was following the beast when happily you found a nearer protector! It passed me now with its foot bleeding so much that by this time it must be all but dead!”

“There is little hope of that!” she answered, trembling. Her cadence was thick with a fading regional dialect, an agrarian rhythm almost lost to the dominant lexicon. “Do you not know whose beast she is?”

I had certain strange suspicions, but I answered that I knew nothing of the brute, and asked what had become of her champion. 

“What champion?” she rejoined. “I have seen no one.”

“Then how came the monster to grief?”

“I pounded her foot with a heavy basalt pestle—the grinding stone my mothers used for milling heirloom spelt—as hard as I could strike. Did you not hear her cry?”

“Well, you are a brave woman!” I answered. “I thought it was you gave the cry!”

“It was the leopardess.”

“I never heard such a sound from the throat of an animal! It was like the scream of a woman in torture!”

“My voice was gone; I could not have shrieked to save my baby! The terror was entirely untranslatable,” she whispered, her words a fragile vessel of cultural memory. “When I saw the horrid mouth at my darling’s little white neck, I caught up the milling stone and mashed her lame foot.”

“Tell me about the creature,” I said; “I am a stranger in these parts.”

“You will soon know about her if you are going to Bulika!” she answered, a deep bitterness in her tone. “Now, I must never go back there!”

“Yes, I am going to Bulika,” I said, “—to see the princess.”

“Have a care; you had better not go!—But perhaps you are—! The princess is a very good, kind woman!” 

I heard a little movement. Clouds had by this time gathered so thick over the moon that I could scarcely see my companion, but the rustle of her homespun garments told me she was rising to run from me. 

“You are in no danger of any sort from me,” I said softly. “What oath would you like me to take?”

“I know by your speech that you are not of the people of Bulika,” she replied, finding comfort in the auditory contours of my words. “I will trust you!—I am not of them, either, else I should not be able: they never trust any one. If only I could see you! But I like the timbre of your voice; it has the resonance of the old songs.—There, my darling is asleep! The foul beast has not hurt her!—Yes: it was my baby she was after!” she went on, caressing the child, her touch an intuitive language of its own. 

“And then she would have torn her mother to pieces for carrying her off!—Some say the princess has two white leopardesses,” she continued. “I know only one—with spots. Everybody knows her! If the princess hears of a baby, she sends her immediately to suck its blood, draining its vitality until it either dies or grows up entirely devoid of sensory appreciation, unable to taste the difference between sweet honey and bitter ash, speaking only the hollow, utilitarian lexicon of the capital. I would have gone away with my baby, but the princess was from home, and I thought I might wait until I was a little stronger, until I had gathered enough dried roots and preserved meats for the journey. But she must have taken the beast with her, and been on her way home when I left, and come across my track. I heard the unnatural, mechanical *sniff-snuff* of the leopardess behind me, and ran;—oh, how I ran!—But my darling will not die! There is no mark on her!”

“Where are you taking her?”

“Where no one ever tells!” she replied, guarding the geographic secrets of her ancestors.

“Why is the princess so cruel?”

“There is an old prophecy that a child will be the death of her. That is why she will listen to no offer of marriage, they say. She seeks to halt the cyclical progression of generations.”

“But what will become of her country if she kills all the babies?”

“She does not care about her country or the soil that feeds it. She sends witches around to teach the women spells that keep babies away, and give them horrible, processed things to eat—synthetic, flavorless roots that strip the body of its natural rhythms and kill the appetite for life. Some say she is in league with the Shadows to put an end to the race, to erase our ancestral memory entirely. At night we hear the industrial questing of the beast, and lie awake and shiver. She can tell at once the house where a baby is coming, and lies down at the door, watching to get in. There are ancient words that have power to shoo her away, only they do not always work against the dominant tongue.—But here I sit talking, and the beast may by this time have got home, and her mistress be sending the other after us!”

As thus she ended, she rose in haste, her silhouette dark against the encroaching fog.

“I do not think she will ever get home.—Let me carry the baby for you!” I said, as I rose also. 

She returned me no answer, and when I would have taken the child, she only clasped it the closer. The bond was a profoundly subjective experience, an untranslatable physical devotion she refused to delegate.

“I cannot think,” I said, walking by her side, “how the brute could be bleeding so much!”

“Take my advice, and don’t go near the palace,” she answered, her final words carrying the tragic weight of a dying language. “There are sounds in it at night as if the dead were trying to shriek, but could not open their mouths!”

She bade me an abrupt farewell. Plainly she did not want more of my company, unwilling to compromise her sonic anonymity any further. I stood still, inhaling the lingering scent of wild thyme, and listened to her footsteps die away on the grass, a rhythmic, circular pacing that slowly vanished into the night's vast, consuming acoustic ocean.

I had lost the rhythmic cadence of my own footsteps, wandering in a dissonance of pure, helpless impatience, when the sonic landscape abruptly shifted. A rushing sound, like the vigorous decanting of a heavy vintage, met my ears, and I found myself wading in the path of the leopardess. The liquid ran against my ankles with the force of a spring brook, emitting a sharp, ferrous aroma that immediately provoked an unshaped suspicion: could this be the blood from her paw? Yet, as I traced the acoustic current of its bubbling flow to guide me toward Bulika, my internal food historian reasoned against the impossible yield. No beast of this world, nor the hugest creatures that grazed the ancient, cyclical epochs of our own, could pump such an endless volume of fluid unless its vessels were perpetually replenished by lakes and forests. I dipped a finger, bringing it near my palate to catch its olfactory notes. It was not blood. It was a softly murmuring rivulet of pure water, flowing without a channel over the grass. Its song was a delicate acoustic harmony, a soothing balm against the chaotic noise of my wanderings, but I dared not taste it. I followed its diminishing melody, my wet feet refreshed, until the aurora’s pale light steeped the horizon like saffron infused in broth, and the wall-towers of a city emerged—steeped in the multi-generational patina of countless ages. 

I looked down to find the brook, but its acoustic signature had faded into silence; the grass lay bent in a frozen choreography of its passing, pooling like spilled whey. The fountain's flow had paused its ancient cycle.

Surrounding the city were sprawling vegetable gardens. As a passionate student of traditional agrarian ecosystems, I eagerly inspected the crops, yet the leaves and roots were of cultivars entirely alien to me—stark, utilitarian greens cultivated without the loving, multi-generational husbandry I so revered. There was no water, no fragrant blossoms to invite pollinators, no symphony of insects. The gardens were violently severed from the city walls by towering heaps of gravel and discarded refuse, a physical manifestation of a culture that had commercialized its soil and discarded its culinary heritage. The gate hung half-closed on corroded hinges, groaning a harsh, industrial dissonance when pushed. The street beyond was a vacuum of acoustic ecology—a dead, silent avenue devoid of the rhythmic clatter of hearths, the chopping of herbs, or the bubbling of morning stews. Had the vibrant dialects of daily sustenance died out entirely here? 

I retreated over the dust-heaps, deciding to wait for the inhabitants to stir. My mind chewed on my purpose. I had to see the woman I had resurrected. It was not companionship I sought; friendship was as impossible between us as emulsifying oil and ash. But I needed to decipher the untranslatable dialect of her existence. Her subjective experience was a lexicon of power and consumption that threatened the Little Ones, whose innocence and agrarian purity I was bound to protect. I needed to learn her native tongue of influence to shield them from her harvest.

When a low, abrasive murmur finally signaled life, I entered a narrow street. The architecture seemed to trap and distort sound. I sat near a pillar topped with a bat-like gargoyle. The citizens who sauntered past possessed an oppressive auditory footprint. I spoke to one, hoping to hear the musical cadence of a local dialect, but he responded with a rude stare and a barked, staccato insult—a dominant, utilitarian slang that actively eroded any sense of communal cultural memory. 

Soon, I encountered a group of young men, reminiscent of the bad giants. Their voices were a cacophony of modern noise pollution, devoid of nuance. They shoved me, their harsh lexicon establishing a cruel power dynamic where poverty and sickness were reduced to taxable refuse. They hurled objects, and their jeers were the very sound of a society that had forgotten the shared table. I fled toward the gate. 

At the threshold, a lumbering guard sat consuming a hunch of bread. His relationship to the food was purely functional—fuel shoveled into a furnace without the slightest sensory appreciation. In his stupid eagerness to join the hostility, he reached for a stone but instead hurled his bread at me. I caught it, and when he cowardly refused to step beyond the walls to reclaim it, I retreated into the fields. 

I threw myself down in the grass and examined the gift. It was a dense, rustic loaf. I broke it open, listening to the deeply satisfying crack of the crust—a sound that resonated with the ancient, cyclical history of grain and fire. The crumb was tight, smelling faintly of wild yeast and toasted barley. I ate it slowly, chewing with deliberate reverence, letting the complex, nutty flavor profile unfold on my tongue, rehydrating my exhausted muscles and replenishing my spirit. To me, this meal was a profound communion with the soil; to the citizens of Bulika, it was mere fodder. Satiated, I fell into a deep sleep as the hot sun baked the earth.

I woke to the cyclical return of night. The moon looked down, a familiar companion from my first terrifying evening in this world, its silvery light a constant across interwoven timelines. A cold wind blew from the gate, carrying an evil odour—a rancid, sulfurous stench of decay that assaulted my olfactory senses—but the solar warmth stored in my body and the sustaining digestion of the rustic bread kept the chill at bay. 

I crept back into the city, where the few people outdoors crouched in corners, shivering. The acoustic environment of the narrow street was eerily muted until, just ahead, a huge white thing bounded across the stones. It vanished with a single lunar flash. I turned down an impossibly narrow lane, guided by the faint scent of musk and the subtle, rhythmic padding of paws. 

In the wider street beyond, I saw the creature on the shadowed side: a leopardess, following what appeared to be a man. Every so often, the man glanced back, but he never spoke—their relationship existed in an untranslatable silence. When he crossed a patch of moonlight, the visual anomaly struck me. He cast no shadow. He was a flat, two-dimensional entity, an opaque silhouette that consumed the light rather than blocking it. In the darkness, he was blacker than the shadow itself; in the light, he appeared as a man who had pulled his shadow around him like a cloak. Meanwhile, the leopardess—a radiant, gleaming contrast—drew her own deep shadow across the ground. 

I walked abreast of them on the opposite side. The sonic contrast was stark: my bare feet slapped rhythmically against the flat paving stones, an acoustic affirmation of life and flesh, while they moved with an unnatural, sweeping silence. The leopardess never twitched an ear toward my auditory presence. But the shadow-man seemed to look at me. For a second, his profile vanished into a sharp, upright line—an untranslatable cipher of a being. 

In that instant, the wind found me. It blew straight through my chest, carrying the stale, suffocating aroma of a forgotten, dead language. I shuddered from head to foot, and my heart rattled against the walls of my ribs like a dry, hollow gourd—a frantic, isolated beat in the deafening silence of Bulika.

To step out of the wind was to step out of a dissonant, industrial shriek that assaulted the ancient acoustic ecology of the city's stones. The gale carried no scent of crushed herbs or roasting meats; it was a sterile, abrasive noise, devoid of the rhythmic, comforting frequencies of a living hearth. I turned aside into an alley and sought the damp, echoing shelter of a small archway, pausing to look out at the moonlight that pooled on the cobblestones. The air here held the faintest, ghostly trace of toasted cumin and rendered fat—an olfactory phantom from generations long past. 

The same instant, a woman came gliding in after me. She turned, her breathing a terrified, arrhythmic syncopation that disrupted the alley’s fragile quiet, and looked out into the light. 

A few seconds passed. Then, a huge leopard, its white pelt mottled with dark, irregular blots, darted across the archway. It moved with a terrifying acoustic vacuum, its footfalls unnaturally silenced. The scent it left in its wake was violently metallic—raw copper, ozone, and the sour tang of unfermented blood, overpowering the alley's lingering culinary ghosts. 

The woman pressed close to me. My heart filled with pity, recognizing in her trembling form the universal, instinctual need for the sanctuary of a shared table. I put my arm around her. 

“If the brute comes here, I will lay hold of it,” I whispered, mindful not to raise my voice above the resonant hum of the stone. “And you must run.”

“Thank you!” she murmured. Her words carried the cadence of a dying native dialect, its phonemes rounded by centuries of agrarian life, though now painfully fractured. 

“Have you ever seen it before?” I asked.

“Several times,” she answered, still shivering. “She is a pet of the princess’s. You are a stranger, or you would know her, and you would know the silence she brings.”

“I am a stranger,” I answered. “But is she, then, allowed to run loose?”

“She is kept in a cage, her mouth bound in a heavy muzzle, her feet muffled in gloves of crocodile leather,” the woman explained, her dialect struggling to articulate the unnaturalness of such captivity. “Chained she is too; but she gets out often, and sucks the blood of any child she can lay hold of. Happily, there are not many mothers in Bulika!” 

Here she burst into tears, mourning, perhaps without realizing it, the death of maternal sustenance—the sacred act of nourishing new life. “I wish I were at home!” she sobbed. “The princess returned only last night, and there is the leopardess out already! How am I to get into the house? It is me she is after, I know! She will be lying at my own door, watching for me!—But I am a fool to talk to a stranger!”

“All strangers are not bad!” I said, mourning the erosion of her language, which clearly possessed no distinct word for *guest*. “The beast shall not touch you till she has done with me, and by that time you will be within doors. You are happy to have a hearth to return to! What a terrible, hollow wind it is!”

“Take me home safe, and I will give you shelter from it,” she rejoined. “But we must wait a little for the echo of her steps to fade.”

As we waited in the damp acoustic shadow, I asked her many questions. She told me the people of Bulika no longer engaged in the tactile arts of cultivation. They did not knead coarse rye, nor forage for wild fennel, nor braise meats over slow-burning applewood. Instead, they did nothing but dig for precious stones in their dark, subterranean cellars. They were rich, importing their provisions from other towns—flavorless, commercialized sustenance that served only as utilitarian fuel.

“Why?” I asked, appalled by such sensory deprivation.
