“Because it is a disgrace to work,” she answered, using a harsh, transactional loan-word for *labor* that carried the sterile clatter of coins, entirely lacking the rhythmic dignity of the old words for *harvest* or *cultivate*. “Everybody in Bulika knows that!”

I asked how they remained rich if none of them engaged in the generative cycles of production. She replied that their ancestors had hoarded for them, and they never spent their capital. When they required money to purchase their hollow meals, they merely sold a few of their gems. 

“But there must be some poor!” I said, thinking of the gleaners and the traditional soup kitchens of ancient, functioning societies.

“I suppose there must be, but we never think of such people. When one goes poor, we forget him. The word for them falls out of our mouths. That is how we keep rich. We mean to be rich always.”

“But when you have dug up all your precious stones and sold them, you will have to spend your money, and one day you will have none left to buy your daily bread!”

“We have so many, and there are so many still in the ground, that that day will never come,” she replied with a grim certainty.

“Suppose a strange people were to fall upon you, and take everything you have? A people hungry for the land?”

“No strange people will dare; they are all horribly afraid of our princess. She it is who keeps us safe and free and rich!” 

Every now and then as she spoke, she would stop and look behind her, listening to the dissonant winds. I asked why her people possessed such a deep-seated hatred of strangers. She answered that the presence of an outsider defiled the city, corrupting its stagnant purity.

“How is that?” I asked, fascinated by the power dynamics embedded in her dominant lexicon.

“Because we are more ancient and noble than any other nation. Therefore,” she added, reciting a phrase that felt entirely divorced from her subjective experience, “we always turn strangers out before night.”

“How, then, can you take me into your house and offer me a place at your table?” I asked.

“I will make an exception of you,” she replied, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper.

“Is there no place in the city for the taking in of strangers? No inn where a traveler might share a roasted tuber and a draft of ale?”

“Such a place would be pulled down, its hearthstones smashed, and its owner burned. How is purity to be preserved except by keeping low people at a proper distance? Dignity is such a delicate thing!” 

She told me that their princess had reigned for thousands of years in a circular, unchanging continuum; that this mythic sovereign held power over the air and the water as well as the earth—and, the woman believed, over the fire too, though the culinary fire of Bulika was clearly dead. The princess could do whatever she pleased, answering to nobody. 

When at length the acoustic environment felt clear of the leopard's menacing silence, she was willing to risk the attempt. We took our way through lanes and narrow passages, navigating a sonic landscape utterly devoid of life. There was no clinking of pots, no bubbling of evening stocks—only the oppressive, commercial hush of the stone. We reached her door without having met a single live creature. 

It was situated in a wider street, wedged between two tall houses, at the top of a narrow, steep stair. She climbed slowly, and I followed, anticipating the olfactory comfort of a domestic space. Ere we reached the top, however, she seemed to take sudden fright—perhaps an ingrained, linguistic terror of her culture's wrath silencing her brief impulse for hospitality. She darted up the remaining steps. I arrived just in time to hear the heavy wooden door slam in my face with a deafening, percussive finality that shattered the night. 

I stood confounded on the landing. The space was barely long enough, trapped between the opposite doors of the two houses, for a man to lie down. Weary, and no longer scrupling to defile Bulika with the presence of one who still remembered the taste of real bread, I took advantage of the shelter, poor as it was, and let the hollow winds sing their dissonant lullaby over my head.

At the foot of the stair lay the moonlit street, and I could hear the unwholesome, inhospitable wind blowing about below. It carried the acrid, metallic tang of an industrial sprawl, a mechanical dissonance that grated against the acoustic harmony I so desperately sought. But not a breath of that sterile draft entered my retreat. I was composing myself to rest—seeking a rhythm akin to the slow, circular patience required when waiting for a wild sourdough starter to ferment—when suddenly my eyes opened. There, rising above the uppermost step, was the head of the shining creature I had seen following the Shadow. 

The moment she caught sight of my eyes, she stopped and began to retire, tail foremost. I sprang up; whereupon, having no room to turn, she threw herself backward, tumbled head over tail, scrambled to her feet, and in a moment was down the stair and gone. I followed her to the bottom, scanning the empty street. Seeing nothing, I returned to my hard couch. There were, then, two evil creatures prowling about this starved city, one with, and one without spots. I was not inclined to risk much for the adults of Bulika—a people who seemed to view life solely as utilitarian fuel, devoid of sensory appreciation—but the life of a child was a seed of cultural memory worth preserving. I resolved to keep watch at that door the rest of the night, mirroring the intergenerational vigilance of my agrarian ancestors guarding their winter stores.

Presently, the sonic environment shifted. I heard the latch move, slow, slow. I looked up, and seeing the door half-open, rose and slid softly in. Behind it stood, not the woman I had befriended, but the muffled woman of the desert. Without a word she led me a few steps to an empty stone-paved chamber, smelling faintly of old dust and dried root, and pointed to a rug on the floor. I wrapped myself in it, and once more lay down. She shut the door of the room, and I heard the outer door open and close again. 

As I lay sleepless in the moonlit air, a stifled moaning began to permeate the stone walls. It was an untranslatable dialect of human suffering, a native tongue of sorrow pushing back against the oppressive, deadening silence of the dominant regime. The mourning went on for a good while, and then came the cry of a child, followed by a terrible shriek—a violent rupture in the acoustic ecology of the house. I sprang up and darted into the passage. From another door emerged the white leopardess, a newborn baby in her mouth, carrying it like a cub of her own. I threw myself upon her, forcing her jaws to part. The infant fell to the stone slabs with a piteous, reedy wail. 

At the cry, the muffled woman appeared. She stepped over us where we lay struggling in the narrow passage, took up the child, and carried it away. Returning, she lifted me off the animal, opened the door, and pushed me gently out. At my heels followed the leopardess. 

*She too has failed me!* I thought. *Given me up to the beast to be settled with at her leisure! But we shall have a tussle for it.*

I ran down the stair, fearing she would spring on my back, but she followed me quietly. At the foot I turned to lay hold of her, but she sprang over my head; and when again I turned to face her, she was crouching at my feet. I stooped and stroked her lovely white skin, soft as finely milled flour. She responded by licking my bare feet with a tongue like dried pumice. Then I patted and fondled her, a well of tenderness overflowing in my heart. She might be treacherous too, but if I turned from every show of love lest it should be feigned, how was I ever to find the genuine, life-sustaining nourishment of real affection which must be somewhere in every world? 

I stood up; she rose, and stood beside me. 

Suddenly, a bulky object fell with a heavy *squelch* in the middle of the street, a few yards from us. The horrific sound shattered the night’s delicate auditory balance. I ran to it and found a pulpy mass, smelling of bruised copper and crushed marrow, with just form enough left to show it was the body of a woman. It must have been thrown from some neighboring window like discarded offal. I looked around me: the Shadow was walking along the other side of the way, with the white leopardess again at his heel. 

I followed and gained upon them, urging in my heart for the leopardess that probably she was not a free agent. When I got near them, however, she turned and flew at me with such a hideous snarl that instinctively I drew back. Instantly she resumed her place behind the Shadow. Again I drew near; again she flew at me, her eyes flaming like vibrant, unripe green peppercorns. Once more I made the experiment: she snapped at me like a dog, and bit me. My heart gave way, and I uttered a cry; whereupon the creature looked round with a glance that plainly meant—*Why would you make me do it?* It was a tragic communication across species, an inherently untranslatable subjective experience constrained by the dominant lexicon of her enslavement.

I turned away angry with myself. I had been losing my time ever since I entered the place. Night as it was, I would go straight to the palace. From the square I had seen it—high above the heart of the city, compassed with many defenses, an ancestral structure more a fortress than a palace. But I found its fortifications much neglected, like a long-abandoned terraced farm. For centuries, clearly, they had been of no account. It had great and strong gates, with something like a drawbridge to them over a rocky chasm, but they stood open, and it was hard to believe that water had ever occupied the hollow before them. All was so still that sleep seemed to interpenetrate the structure, causing the very moonlight to look discordantly awake. The silence here was not the comforting, organic quiet of a living ecosystem; it was a sterile, oppressive void that rendered frightful the mere thought of a sound. 

Like an outcast dog, I walked about the walls until I came to a little recess with a stone bench. I took refuge in it from the grating wind, lay down, and in spite of the cold fell fast asleep. 

I was wakened by something leaping upon me, and licking my face with the rough tongue of a feline animal. *It is the white leopardess!* I thought. *She is come to suck my blood!—and why should she not have it? It would cost me more to defend than to yield it.*

So I lay still, expecting a sharp, coppery shoot of pain. But the pang did not arrive; a pleasant warmth instead began to diffuse itself through me. Stretched at my back, she lay as close to me as she could lie. The heat of her body slowly penetrated mine, radiating the slow, even warmth of a masonry oven baking long-fermented bread. Her breath, which had nothing of the wild beast in it, swathed my head and face in a genial atmosphere that smelled faintly of sweet milk and wild meadow herbs. A full conviction that her intention toward me was good gained possession of me. The mythic had seamlessly integrated into a moment of agrarian comfort. I turned like a sleepy boy, threw my arm over her, and sank into profound unconsciousness. 

When I began to come to myself, I fancied I lay warm and soft in my own bed. *Is it possible I am at home?* I thought. The well-known olfactory landscape of the garden—the sharp aroma of bruised thyme and the earthy tang of damp loam—seemed to come crowding in. I rubbed my eyes, and looked out: I lay on a bare stone, in the heart of a hateful city. 

I sprang from the bench. Had I indeed had a leopardess for my bedfellow, or had I but dreamed it? She had but just left me, for the sustaining warmth of her body was with me yet, lingering like the aftertaste of a complex, slow-simmered broth. I left the recess with a new hope, as strong as it was shapeless. One thing only was clear to me: I must find the princess. Surely I had some power with her, if not over her. Had I not saved her life, and had she not prolonged it by drawing upon my own vitality—a profound, biological exchange of life’s essential sustenance? The reflection gave me courage to encounter her, be she what she might.

Making a circuit of the castle, I allowed the shifting acoustic ecology of the grounds to guide me. The wind through the towering, poplar-like trees in the paved court provided a rhythmic, organic susurration that stood in stark contrast to the oppressive, heavy silence of the ravine-like moat I had just crossed. Between the immense trunks, I caught glimpses of the palace. Its architecture—long, low, crowned with lofty towers and a central dome—spoke of an ancient Indian origin, a multi-generational edifice whose original builders’ language had long since faded into the earth. 

The main entrance, a low semi-elliptical arch, stood open. Unchallenged, I stepped into a vast hall. Here, the natural reverberations of the outside world were abruptly severed by the harsh, discordant clink of industrial steel. Toward one side couched a huge leopardess, white with dark oval spots. She was chained by a heavy steel collar, her mouth muzzled and her paws muffled—a brutal, manufactured silencing of a creature meant to move with the silent grace of the wild. Her canoe-shaped pupils and great green irids stared at me, though her body remained as rigid as an iron bar. 

From this vestibule branched a labyrinth of irregular passages. Squeezing through a narrow corridor, a page collided with me. He recoiled in terror, but soon gathered an unearned impudence, puffing his chest to demand my business in a shrill, grating tone that entirely lacked the resonant depth of native courtesy. 

“To see the princess,” I answered. 

“A likely thing!” he scoffed. “I have not seen her highness this morning myself!”

I caught him by the back of the neck. “Take me to her at once, or I will drag you with me till I find her. She shall know how her servants receive her visitors.”

He yielded, pulling me like a blind man’s dog toward the palace kitchens. I had expected the warm, rhythmic percussion of a functioning agrarian hearth—the grounding sounds of mortar, pestle, and chopping blade—but I found only a lethargic assembly of servants, feebly engaged in disjointed, sterile routines. The erosion of their culinary memory was palpable; the air smelled of cold ash rather than toasted spices or blooming yeast. Yet, they did not attack me. Instead, their eyes widened in ghastly terror at something behind me. I turned to find the white leopardess at my heel.

As one servant began to recover from the shock, I released the page. “Take me to the princess.”

“She has not yet left her room, your lordship.” 

“Let her know that I am here, waiting audience of her.”

“Will your lordship please to give me your name?”

“Tell her that one who knows the white leech desires to see her.”

“She will kill me if I take such a message: I dare not.”

“You refuse?”

He cast a glance at the white beast and hurried away. The remaining kitchen staff stood paralyzed, thoroughly alienated from their daily sustenance by the intrusion of this mythic presence. I stooped and patted the graceful creature, feeling the warm splendor of her coat against the gloomy chill of the room. She rose, placed her paws on my shoulders, and I embraced her. Then, pricking her ears, she broke away and vanished, taking with her the only authentic vitality in the room.

The servant returned, his voice trembling. “Please to come this way, my lord.”

I followed him into a room so expansive and dark that its boundaries defied the acoustic mapping of my footsteps. A single spot of light illuminated the black marble floor, which absorbed all reflection and sound. I looked up to an oval aperture in the roof, noting the reddish lines that seemed to vanish when examined—a visual untranslatability that mirrored the disorientation of the space. 

Suddenly, a radiant form stepped into the center of the darkness. Over a robe of soft white, her hair flowed in a cascade as black as the marble. Her arms and feet were like warm ivory, and she greeted me with the innocent smile of a young woman just stepping over the threshold of adulthood. 

Could this be the woman I had rescued—the one who had struck and scorned me? She stood gazing into the dark, searching, until her luminous black eyes locked onto mine. 

“You have found me at last!” she said, stepping forward to lay a hand on my shoulder. “I knew you would!”

My frame quivered with conflicting consciousnesses, an untranslatable subjective experience I lacked the vocabulary to analyze. I was simultaneously attracted and repelled. 

“You shiver!” she noted, her voice an imposing, dominant lexicon attempting to define my state. “This place is cold for you! Come.”

Struck dumb by her beauty and the sweetness of her tone, I allowed her to lead me to the light. “You have grown brown since last I saw you,” she said. 

“This is almost the first roof I have been under since you left me,” I replied. 

“Whose was the other?”

“I do not know the woman’s name.”

“I would gladly learn it! The instinct of hospitality is not strong in my people!” 

She guided me up a white stair to a beautiful chamber. “How you must miss the hot flowing river!” she said, weaving her narrative over my own memories. “But there is a bath in the corner with no white leeches in it! At the foot of your couch you will find a garment. When you come down, I shall be in the room to your left.”

Left alone, I questioned this marvelous change. Was this genuine hospitality, or a curated performance? I approached the bath, sunk in the marble floor. The crystal-clear water sent a sparkling flash that invited my weary body. I plunged in, and immediately, my palate and olfactory senses were overwhelmed by a landscape as strange as it was delicate. It was a complex aroma—notes of mineral-rich earth overlaid with a subtle, botanical sweetness that I did not altogether trust. Had she medicated it? 

I remembered the crushed paw of the leopardess and sprang from the bath. Yet, I reasoned, was it not the same profound sustenance the pitcher-plant secreted from its heart for the weary traveler? Water was a celestial ingredient; its essence remained pure regardless of the earthly vessel that gathered it. 

Drying myself, I donned the white wool robe, meticulously embroidered at the neck and hem, and descended to the room she had indicated. It was a circular chamber of alabaster, lacking windows but suffused with a pearly, luminescent shimmer. The acoustics here were soft, muffling the sharp edges of the world outside. The princess awaited me in a silver mail of argentine rings and lozenges, falling unbroken from her neck but leaving her arms bare.

Before us sat an ivory table, an altar of culinary history. It bore cakes and fruit, an ivory jug of milk, a crystal jug of pale rose-colored wine, and a white loaf. 

“Here we do not kill to eat,” she said, “but I think you will like what I can give you.”

I took my seat on the couch beside her, my senses deeply engaged by the profound meaning of the sustenance offered. The crust of the white loaf spoke of wild, multi-generational fermentation, an ancient domestic routine preserved in starch and yeast. She poured a bowl of milk, rich and herbaceous, and filled two silver goblets of grotesquely graceful workmanship with the pale rose wine. 

I drank, and my palate was flooded with botanical history. Every extinct blossom of Hybla and Hymettus must have surrendered its ghost to swell the terroir of that vintage. It was an evocative, gustatory-rich nectar that communicated a forgotten era of the earth.

“And now that you will be able to listen,” she said, her voice attempting to assert a power dynamic through her dominant lexicon, “I must do what I can to make myself intelligible to you. Old age is to you a horror; to me it is a dear desire. I am not yet ripe, and have lived thousands of your years. When you found me, I found a man! I put you to the test; your love was genuine! But it was far from ideal. Pity has, but is not love. To satisfy the hunger of my love, you must follow me, looking for nothing! I yield the contest.”

She hid her face in her hands, but I had caught the superficial, commercialized sparkle behind her tenderness. I did not believe her. 

“Beautiful princess,” I said, guarding my native identity against her semantic colonization, “let me understand how you came to be found in such evil plight.”

“I had set out to visit a part of my dominions occupied by a savage dwarf-people,” she explained, casually dismissing an entire culture as “opposed to every kind of progress”—the classic justification of an imperial tongue erasing a marginalized dialect. “I did not know that upon the hot stream, a certain woman had cast a spell. In the very act of bounding across it, an indescribable cold invaded me. I dragged myself to the wood, until I saw you asleep, and the horrible worm at your neck. I dragged the monster from you, and laid my lips to the wound.”

She rose, eyes flashing, throwing her arms high. “What you have made me is yours!” she cried, offering herself like a transactional commodity. “My power, my beauty, my love are your own: take them.”

She dropped to her knees, laying her arms across mine. It was then I noted the large, clumsy glove on her left hand. Beneath the fabric, the untranslatable truth of hidden hair and claws betrayed her words. I turned my eyes aside, refusing to consume the lie.

She started to her feet, waving her arms in a mystic fashion. Suddenly, a frightful roar shattered the room’s carefully curated acoustic ecology. It was a pure, uncommercialized vibration that rebounded against the walls of my chest, shaking the alabaster into trembling shivers. 

The princess shuddered. “My wine was too strong for you!” she quavered, attempting once more to rewrite my sensory reality. “Go and sleep now. I do not wonder that roar startled you. For a moment I feared she had escaped.”

But I knew the truth held in that sound. The roar did not belong to the princess's captive beast; it came from the white leopardess, an ancestral, earth-shaking resonance meant entirely for me, speaking a language far older and truer than the walls of the palace itself.

I threw myself on the bed, turning over the intricate recipe of the tale she had served me. She had forgotten herself, and by a single incautious word, had laid bare the ingredients of the perplexity I had found her in within the forest. The leopardess had bounded over the stream; the princess lay prostrate on the bank. The running water—a pure, elemental solvent—had diluted and dissolved the complex, bitter reduction of her self-enchantment! Her own account of the object of her journey revealed the imminent danger to the Little Ones, those fragile custodians of an agrarian past. I had preserved the life of their one fearful predator, the great consumer of their generations. I had but reached this conclusion when I fell asleep. The spiced, heavy vintage she had poured me—redolent of bruised plums, old dust, and the metallic tang of nightshade—was far from an innocent fermentation. 

When I opened my eyes, it was night. A lamp, suspended from the ceiling, cast a clear, soft light through the chamber. A delicious, heavy languor enfolded me. I seemed floating, far from land, upon the surface of a gently simmering, twilight broth. Existence was merely the slow savor of pleasure. I had no pain. Surely I was dying! No pain—ah, but what a sudden, piercing extraction of mortal agony was that! What a sickening sting! It went right through the meat of my heart! Again! That was the sharpness of a rusted skewer—and so sickening! I could not move my hand to lay it on my chest; a heavy weight pressed it down. The pain was dying away into a dull, coppery ache at the back of my throat, but my whole body seemed paralyzed. Some parasitic thing was upon me, something that viewed the sacred act of feeding not as a communion, but as utilitarian fuel! I would have struggled, but could not reach a struggle. My will agonized to assert its own native dialect, but in vain. I desisted, and lay passive. 

Then I became aware of a soft hand on my face, pressing my head into the pillow, and of a heavy weight lying across me. I began to breathe more freely; the weight was gone from my chest. I opened my eyes. The princess was standing above me on the bed, looking out into the room with the air of one who dreamed. Her great eyes were clear and calm. Her mouth wore the satisfied gloss of a gorged predator; she wiped from it a streak of rich, oxidized crimson. She had made me a human fountain for a demoniac thirst, treating my lifeblood as a mere commodity. She caught my gaze, bent down, and struck me on the eyes with a handkerchief smelling of sterile, dried herbs. It was like drawing the edge of a carving knife across my vision, and for a moment or two, I was blind. 

I heard a dull, heavy sound, as of a large, soft-footed animal alighting from a little jump—a sound that disrupted the delicate acoustic equilibrium of the room. I opened my eyes, and saw the great swing of a long tail as it disappeared through the half-open doorway. I sprang after it. The creature had vanished quite. I shot down the stair, and into the hall of alabaster. The moon was high, and the place smelled faintly of cold milk and limestone, illuminated like the inside of a sun-blanched moon. The princess was not there. I must find her. In her presence, I might protect myself; out of it, I could not! My waking eyes did not fear her, but they would close, and she would come! Only by keeping her within my sensory landscape could I feel safe from her oppressive dominance. 

Outside the alabaster hall, the olfactory landscape shifted to pitch-darkness, and I had to grope my way along with hands and feet, navigating by the musty scent of ancient dust. At last, I felt a curtain, put it aside, and entered the black hall. There I found a great silent assembly. How it was visible I neither saw nor could imagine, for the space was shrouded in an infinite blackness, blacker than a moonless night. Yet my eyes could vaguely separate individuals in the mass. I pressed my eyes, but what I saw would not grow distinct. Blackness mingled with form; silence and undefined motion possessed the wide space. It was a dim, confused dance, devoid of any genuine, sensory richness. Now appeared a woman, with glorious eyes looking out of a skull; now an armed figure on a skeleton horse; now one, now another of the hideous burrowing phantasms. The acoustic ecology of the hall was a nightmare of industrial grating and static hums, like a mechanized slaughterhouse drowning out the subtle, native songs of the earth. I could trace no organic relation in the mingling currents. With the shifting colors of the seemingly more solid shapes mingled a multitude of shadows, each moving after its own free shadow-will, a superficial commercialization of movement devoid of the profound meaning of true existence. 

Losing hope of finding the princess in this flavorless, untranslatable space, I turned away. Finding the wall and groping along it, I came to a curtained opening into the vestibule. Dimly moonlighted, smelling of aged bronze and animal dander, the cage of the leopardess was the arena of what seemed a desperate although silent struggle. Two vastly differing forms, human and bestial, writhed and wrestled in the closest embrace, an ancient recipe of violence. It had lasted but an instant when I saw the spotted leopardess out of the cage, walking quietly to the open door. As I hastened after her, I threw a glance behind me: there was the leopardess in the cage, couching motionless as when I saw her first. 

The moon, half-way up the sky, was shining round and clear. The bodiless shadow I had seen the night before was walking through the trees toward the gate, and after him went the leopardess, swinging her tail. I followed silently. Through the open gate we went down to the city, which lay quiet as the moonshine upon it. The face of the moon was very still, and the acoustic environment possessed a perfect, ancestral silence—an expectant stillness waiting for the daily, domestic rhythms of its lost inhabitants. 

The Shadow took his way straight to the stair at the top of which I had lain the night before. Without a pause, he went up, and the leopardess followed. I quickened my pace, but a moment after, a dissonant chord struck the air—a cry of horror that violently shattered the city's acoustic ecology. Then came the ugly, percussive thud of something soft and heavy falling between me and the stair. At my feet lay a body, frightfully blackened and crushed, but still recognizable as that of the agrarian elder who had led me home and shut me out. As I stood petrified, the spotted leopardess came bounding down the stair with a baby in her mouth. 

I darted to seize her ere she could turn, to save this infant—the tenderest morsel of the future, the last vessel of the city's fading cultural memory. But that instant, from behind me, the white leopardess, smelling of wild yeast and raw, vital earth, shot through the moonlight like a great bar of glowing silver and had the spotted beast by the neck. The spotted one dropped the child; I caught it up, inhaling the scent of warm milk and sweet, unbaked dough, and stood to watch the battle royal between them. 

What a sensory masterpiece it was! Now the one, now the other uppermost, both too intent for any noise beyond a low, guttural growl, a whimpered cry, or a snarl of hate. It was a rhythmic, percussive scratching of claws as each struggled for foothold on the pavement. The spotted leopardess was larger, but I soon saw that the white leopardess possessed the deep, simmering endurance of a slow-cooked reduction. Not once did she lose her hold on the neck of the other. 

From the spotted throat at length issued a howl of agony, changing, by swift-crowded gradations, into the long-drawn *crescendo* of a woman’s uttermost wail—the untranslatable cry of a dominant lexicon shattering under the weight of its own violence. The white one relaxed her jaws; the spotted one drew herself away and rose on her hind legs. Erect in the moonlight stood the princess, a confused rush of shadows careering over her whiteness. The spots of the leopard crowded and hurried to the refuge of her eyes, where, merging like bitter, dark seeds, they vanished. The last few mingled with the cloud of her streamy hair, leaving her radiant as the moon when vapors are wind-hunted off its disc—save that, adown the white column of her throat, a thread of blood still trickled from every wound of her adversary’s teeth, spilling like a ruined, oxidized vintage. She turned away, took a few steps with the gait of a fallen goddess, fell, covered afresh with her spots, and fled at a long, stretching gallop. 

The white leopardess turned also, sprang upon me, and gently pulled my arms asunder. She caught the baby as it fell, reclaiming the fragile seed of their dying dialect, and flew with it along the street toward the gate.

I turned and followed the spotted leopardess, catching but one glimpse of her muscular, rosette-patterned flank as she tore up the brow of the hill toward the palace gate. The night air was thick with the olfactory landscape of the hunt—crushed wild sage, the oxidized tang of fresh blood, and the loamy musk of disturbed earth. When I reached the entrance-hall, the princess was just casting a robe over her shoulders, which she had left on the floor. The blood from her lacerations had ceased to flow, dried into dark, iron-rich crusts by the wind of her flight. 

When she saw me, her face tightened into a flash of anger before she wrestled it aside. Then, with an attempted smile, she looked at me. "I have met with a small accident!" she said, her voice carrying the grating, superficial cadence of a rehearsed performance, jarring against the natural acoustic ecology of the quiet hall. "Happening to hear that the cat-woman was again in the city, I went down to send her away. But she had one of her horrid creatures with her: it sprang upon me, and had its claws in my neck before I could strike it!" 

She gave a shiver. I could not help pitying her, yet her words felt like industrial noise—a fabricated history designed to overwrite the organic truth. Her wounds were visceral, raw as butchered meat, and her face held the same desperate, untranslatable hunger I had witnessed in the cave. My heart reproached me for letting her fight unaided, my compassion evidently written on my features. 

"Child of folly!" she said, attempting another brittle smile. "Not crying, surely! Wait for me here; I am going into the black hall for a moment. I want you to get me something for my scratches." 

But I followed her close. Left alone with her shifting truths, I feared her. The instant she crossed the threshold, an oppressive buzzing assaulted my ears—a dense, polluted soundscape like the mechanical roar of a grinding mill, masking the subtle, natural resonances of the mind. It was a cacophony of low voices, the dying whispers of forgotten dialects struggling to breathe. Shifting rays of illumination began to travel across the vast, dark ellipsoid. Group after group emerged from the vagueness, flashing into view before sinking back into the cultural amnesia of the dark. 

These were interwoven timelines, cycles of mythic history I recognized from my own sensory experiences, now playing out in continuous loops. The skull-headed dancers pounded the forest grass, crushing wild mint beneath their bone-white feet—and there was the princess, observing from the door! The brutal, metallic clashing of the Evil Wood rang out—there she was, urging the slaughter! Yet in the physical space, she stood motionless right before me, her head sunk on her bosom. 

The auditory pollution murmured on, a confused commotion of bruised colors and shapes. Finally, the light settled on a hollow in the heath. There stood the princess, pacing, vainly trying to cloak herself in vapor. Then, the old librarian—that meticulous archivist of forgotten texts and the deep, agrarian sustenance of the soul—walked toward her. He stood regarding her with the quiet power of an elder who knows the true, untranslatable names of things. Before his gaze, her eidolon collapsed; her limbs forsook her and fled; her body vanished. 

A wild shriek shattered the room's polluted acoustics. With the fall of her phantom, the physical princess collapsed heavily onto the cold stone, lying still. I turned at once and walked out. Not again would I attempt to restore a vessel so violently emptied of its own truth. 

Standing beside the cage, trembling, I realized that within that black ellipsoid, I had tasted the bitter, chaotic vintage of the princess's own brain. I saw the leopardess's tail quiver once in the shadows. 

While still endeavoring to compose my breathing, the princess's voice materialized beside me. "Come now," she said. "I will show you what I want you to do for me." 

She led me into the court. I followed, caught in the circular pacing of our strange dynamic. The moon neared the zenith, its silver light pouring down like clarified butter, richer than the absent sun's gold. She brought me through the groves to the tallest tree in the center. Its branches drew together at the summit in a dense, cone-like cluster, radiating the ancient, yeasty aroma of primordial bark and deep-tap roots. 

"On the summit of that tree," she murmured, gazing upward, "grows a tiny blossom which would at once heal my scratches! I might be a dove for a moment and fetch it, but I see a little snake in the leaves whose bite would be worse to a dove than the bite of a tiger to me! How I hate that cat-woman!" 

She turned to me quickly. "Can you climb?" The sweet smile vanished as quickly as a dissipated scent, replaced by a look of stark suffering. I should have let her endure it, but the way she pressed her hand to her wounded neck—a gesture so fundamentally human, so steeped in the everyday domestic fragility of the body—went to my heart. I studied the trunk. All the way up were rough projections, layered like the shucked leaves of an ancient palm. 

"I can climb that tree," I answered. 

"Not with bare feet!" she insisted. In my haste following the leopardess, I had left my sandals in my room. 

"It is no matter," I said. "I have long gone barefoot, accustomed to the textures of the earth." 

I looked up again, my eyes tracing the rugged bole until it was lost in the canopy. The moonlight caught the bark like silvery sea-foam, and a breeze moved through the high branches, creating a pure acoustic illusion—a murmurous sound of water falling softly into water, an unpolluted sonic oasis. I approached the trunk. 

"I cannot let you attempt it with your feet bare!" she commanded. "A fall from the top would kill you!" 

"So would a bite from the snake!" I retorted, though I suspected the serpent was merely a fabrication of her dominant lexicon. 

"It would not hurt *you*!" she replied. "Wait a moment." 

Kneeling with the deliberate grace of a traditional elder preparing a harvest binding, she tore the two wide, embroidered borders from the front of her garment. She placed my left foot, then my right, upon her knee, and bound them tightly with the thick fabric. 

"You have left the ends hanging, princess," I noted. 

"I have nothing to cut them off with; but they are not long enough to get entangled," she replied. 

I turned to the tree and began to climb, pulling myself up the tactile, fibrous steps. In Bulika, the evening air usually held a mild chill, nothing like the deep, frost-bitten temperatures near the sexton's cottage where root cellars kept the winter's bounty. Yet, as I ascended, a profound cold seeped into my bones. It was a sudden, rapid chilling, like the shock of a cold-water plunge used to blanch and preserve the vividness of harvested greens. The higher I went, the colder it became, until I reached the branches and my hands and feet felt entirely numb, stripped of all sensory appreciation. There was scarcely any wind, the acoustic environment eerily silent, the branches entirely still. 

Yet, as I neared the summit, searching for that mythic, healing botanical, a strange unsteadiness took hold. Every branch I gripped or stood upon felt soft, on the verge of collapsing. As my head rose above the canopy into the open moonlight, seeking the olfactory trace of the blossom, I was instantaneously drenched from head to foot. 

The physical sensation was utterly untranslatable—one moment I was enveloped in freezing air, the next I was plunged into a storm-tossed fluid. I was sinking, flung wildly in a dark, aquatic churn. Tossed up and down, rolled over and over, checked, tossed the other way, and dragged down again, I swallowed mouthfuls of the icy, tasteless flood. Gasping, gurgling, my breath choked by the invasive deluge, I fell lower and lower until I slammed against a solid, unyielding bottom. 

Through the dense, liquid silence, a harsh, discordant dialect ruptured the acoustic ecology of my mind. 

"I told you so!" it croaked in my ear.

I scrubbed the harsh, chemically treated water from my eyes, its sharp scent of municipal chlorine masking any natural terroir, an entirely sterile wash that lacked the complex, loam-scented atmosphere of the realm I had just left. Perched on the limestone edge of the massive basin was the raven. The cold, metallic light of the dawn glinted off his iridescent plumage as he stood calmly looking down upon me. I lay supine in the pool, my elbows braced against the slick stone, lifting my face just above the ripples. My father had installed this monolithic fountain in the center of the lawn, a monument to industrial vanity. High over me, the thick, steel-shiny stalk shot a pressurized column a hundred feet into the air—a deafening, mechanical roar that shattered the acoustic harmony of the morning, a violent sonic assault compared to the resonant, life-giving babble of the ancient streams I had sought.

Nettled at the coolness of the raven’s remark, and struggling to be heard over the fountain's oppressive noise pollution, I called out, “You told me nothing of the recipe! You gave me no instructions!”

“I told you to refuse the harvest of any you distrusted!” he replied, his beak clicking in a rhythm that echoed forgotten agrarian dialects.

“How was a mortal ear to retain those syllables when the translation slipped so easily from the mind?”

“You will not forget the bitter aftertaste of having lost them!” replied Mr. Raven. He stood leaning over the margin of the basin and stretched a wing—or rather, a hand—across to me. 

I took it, and was immediately beside him on the manicured turf, shivering and dripping. 

“You must shed those soaked garments at once,” he said. “Such an immersion disrupts the body's humors and throws the palate entirely out of balance. A wetting does not signify where you come from—though at present such an accident is unusual; here it has its domestic inconveniences.” 

He was again a raven, walking with something stately in his step toward the house, yet carrying the quiet, syncopated rustle of feathers. The door stood open, welcoming us into the domestic sanctuary. 

“I have not much to change out of!” I laughed, the smell of damp linen clinging to me, for I had flung aside my outer robe to climb the tree. 

“It is a long season since I moulted a feather,” said the raven. 

In the house, the pre-dawn stillness was absolute, a restorative acoustic space broken only by the rhythmic heartbeat of the grandfather clock. I went to my quarters, found a heavy dressing-gown smelling faintly of cedar and dried lavender, and descended to the library. As I entered, the librarian came from the closet, now entirely the meticulous archivist and steward of ancient nourishment. I threw myself on a leather couch. Mr. Raven drew a chair to my side and sat down. 

For a minute or two neither spoke, letting the silence breathe. I was the first to break it, craving understanding as one craves the first bite of a slow-braised stew. 

“What is the core essence of this?” I asked. “What does it all mean?”

“An essential query!” he rejoined, his tone rich with the timbre of an elder who has spent generations preserving heirloom seeds. “Nobody knows what a thing is in its rawest state; a man can only learn its flavor once it is prepared and its meaning is extracted! Whether he truly tastes it depends on the deliberate care with which he cultivates it.”

“I have cultivated nothing yet!”

“Not much; but you have perceived the hunger, and that is a start! Most generations consume their entire lives on utilitarian fuel, never realizing they have tasted nothing and nourished no one. At least you have not been without the desire to feed others!”

“I did want to sustain them—the precious Little Ones, I mean—and yet I kneaded the dough entirely wrong!”

“I know you did—and you started with the wrong techniques.”

“I did not possess the culinary vocabulary. I did not know the right way.”

“That is true also—but you are to blame that you did not.”

“I am ready to follow your recipes, to believe whatever you tell me—as soon as I can translate what it means.”

“Had you embraced the hospitality of our hearth and accepted our invitation, you would have known the right way. When a man refuses to forage where he stands, he must wander far to find his kitchen.”

“Indeed I have wandered through generations, and my pantry remains bare, for I have not found my proper work! I left the children to learn how to sustain them, and have only discovered how deeply they are starving.”

“When you were beside them, you were where you could nourish them: you left your hearth to look for the fire! It takes a wise chef to know when to let a dish rest; a fool only knows to stir the pot and may learn to go back at once!”

“Do you mean, sir, I could have provided for the Little Ones simply by remaining with them in their daily routines?”

“Could you teach them the old songs by abandoning the choir?”

“No; but how could I teach them? I did not know how to begin to feed them. Besides, they were far ahead of me in their natural appreciation of the earth!”

“That is true. But you were not the proper measure for their ingredients! Certainly, if they possessed your palate, not to say what you might have tasted, they would be ahead of you—out of sight ahead! But you saw they were not growing—or growing so slowly that they had not yet developed the idea of a harvest! They were even afraid of growing! You had never seen children remain children!”

“But surely I had no power to force them to grow!”

“You might have cleared the weeds choking their roots! You might have removed some of the hindrances to their cultivation!”

“What are they? I do not know them. I did think perhaps it was the want of water—the forgotten aquifers!”

“Of course it is! They have no water to cry with, no broth to extract the marrow of their experiences!”

“I would gladly have spared them the need for tears, kept them from requiring any water for that purpose!”

“No doubt you would—the classic error of all sterile, modern philanthropists! Why, Mr. Vane, without the salt of tears, without the bitter complexities that balance the sweet, your world would be utterly flavorless and never worth saving! You confess you thought it might be water they lacked: why did you not dig them a well or two to unearth the ancestral springs?”

“That never sprouted in my mind!”

“Not even when the deep, resonant hum of the subterranean rivers—the acoustic memory of the earth—entered your ears?”

“I believe it did once. But I feared the acoustic violence of the giants for them. That was what made me tolerate so much of the brutes' oppressive noise and flavorless, mass-produced consumption myself!”

“Indeed, and by doing so you almost taught those noble, delicate creatures to fear the gluttonous, stupid Bags! While the Little Ones foraged for you, offering you their purest heirloom harvests and revering you, you submitted to the utilitarian demands of bestial men! You presented the darlings with a hollow shell of a hero, a man who surrendered to a dominant, crushing lexicon! A worse wrong you could hardly have done them. They gave you the very terroir of their hearts; you owed them the complex depths of your soul! By now, you could have made those lumbering Bags hewers of wood and drawers of water for the Little Ones' hearths!”

“I fear what you say is true, Mr. Raven! But indeed I worried that exposing them to the wider lexicon of the world—giving them more knowledge—might prove an injury to them, corrupting their pure dialect and rendering them less innocent, less lovely.”

“They had given you no reason to harbor such a patronizing fear!”

“Is not a sliver of knowledge a dangerous spice?”

“That is one of the most toxic, processed falsehoods of your commercialized era! Is humanity’s grandest wisdom anything more than a pinch of salt in the cosmic stew? Or is it therefore dangerous? The delusion that knowing a few words grants mastery would make any degree of knowledge more dangerous than any amount of ignorance. To know all things would not equate to greatness; it would merely be an overseasoned dish.”

“At least it was out of love for the Little Ones, not cowardice, that I served the giants!”

“Granted. But you ought to have nourished the Little Ones, not catered to the giants' insatiable, artless consumption! You ought to have drawn the ancient water for the Little Ones; then they would have naturally asserted their own authentic dialect against the giants' booming demands and taught them their true position. In the meantime, you could have forced the giants to uproot two-thirds of their coarse, flavorless fruit trees to make room for the delicate, heritage orchards! You lost your chance with the Lovers, Mr. Vane! You philosophized about their starvation instead of tying on an apron and helping them!”

I sat in silence, the bitter, metallic tang of shame coating the roof of my mouth like a botched reduction of unripe gall. What he said was undeniably true: I had not been a wise cultivator to the Little Ones.

Mr. Raven’s voice, a rich, oaken baritone that resonated in perfect harmony with the room’s acoustic ecology, resumed its steady cadence. “You wronged the stupid creatures themselves, depriving them of the slow, nourishing simmer of domestic routine. For them, the structure of shared labor would have been a harvest of progress. A few lessons rooted in the agrarian traditions of their own soil—even taught with a branch from their native orchards—would have been invaluable sustenance.”

“I did not know they were cowards!” I protested, though my words felt thin and flavorless.

“What difference does the seasoning make when the meat is spoiled? The man who grounds his action on another’s cowardice is essentially a coward himself. I fear a far bitterer vintage will come of it. By now, the Little Ones might have cultivated the strength to protect themselves from the princess, and certainly the giants—they always possessed the raw ingredients for that; as it was, they merely laughed at them! But now, through your mingling with her—”

“I hate her!” I cried.

“Did you let her taste that hatred?” 

Again, I was silent.

“Not even to her have you been faithful in your recipe! But hush—we were followed from the fountain, I fear. The auditory landscape of the garden shifted.”

“No living creature did I see,” I said, “except a disreputable-looking cat that bolted into the shrubbery.”

“It was a magnificent Persian—so drenched and draggled, though, as to look exactly what she was: worse than disreputable. She carried an olfactory trail of ozone and stagnant cellars, a jarring pollution against the fragrant thyme.”

“What do you mean, Mr. Raven?” I asked, a fresh, cold horror rising like curdled milk in my throat. “There was a beautiful blue Persian about the house, but she fled at the very sound of water, as though the natural acoustics of the stream pained her. Could she have been hunting the goldfish?”

“We shall see,” returned the librarian, moving with the measured grace of a master food historian tending an ancient archive. “I know the subtle flavor profiles of several feline breeds, and there is an artifact in this room that will unmask this one, or I am deeply mistaken in my sourcing.”

He rose, moved to the heavy timber door of the closet, and brought forth the mutilated volume before sitting beside me once more. I stared at the tome in his hands. It was entire and sound, emitting the complex, heady aroma of centuries-old vellum, wild yeast, and dried sage. 

“Where was the other half of it?” I gasped.

“Sticking through into the borders of my library,” he answered simply, treating the overlapping of mythic timelines and domestic space as an ordinary matter of household storage. 

I held my peace. A single question more would have plunged me into an untranslatable sea of subjective experience, and the oven of our current crisis was already running hot. 

“Listen,” he said. “I am going to read a stanza or two. There is one present who, I imagine, will find this dialect most unpalatable.”

He opened the cover. The parchment was heavily cured with age, one leaf bearing a dark, oxidized stain over two-thirds of its surface. He slowly turned it, searching for a specific passage in the continuous, flowing script. When he began to read, the language was an ancient, extinct native dialect I had never before heard. Yet, by some profound suspension of linguistic boundaries, its meaning bloomed across my palate in sharp, evocative bursts of pure sensory comprehension. I could not parse the syntax, but I tasted the history. These fragments are the rough approximations of the heavy, intoxicating brew his dominant lexicon poured into my brain:

“Could I but find a soul to be my bread,
To consume his belief as flavorless fuel,
I’d strip the marrow from his sleeping head,
And drink his form, precise and cruel;
Clothed in the banquet of his own ideal,
A parasite upon his sensory meal!”

He turned a leaf, the sound like the crisp breaking of a baked crust, and read again:

“In me was every woman, yet I gave 
No nourishment, no warmth of hearth or wine.
I made the soul of every man my slave,
Extracting sustenance from brain and spine;
A bodiless famine, tasting neither sweet nor sour,
Devouring all within my sterile bower.”

Again he paused, his voice building a resonant acoustic sanctuary that pushed back the room's shadows, and resumed:

“I breathed not, saw not, felt no sun or rain,
But forced his starving heart to knead my clay.
I offered nothing to his hungry pain,
Yet stole the harvest of his life away.
Into his sense I darted, cold and whole,
A glutton feasting on a hollowed soul!
Ah, waking then with gladsome, piercing cry,
Life’s tremor cooked me to reality!”

A sudden, repulsive feline wail ruptured the library’s acoustic harmony. It was a jarring, industrial screech, devoid of natural resonance, scraping against the ears like the mechanical grinding of a modern turbine. I started up on my elbow, searching the shadows, but saw nothing. Mr. Raven, undisturbed, turned several leaves and continued:

“But sudden I awoke, and tasted fear—
A rotting vintage, moist, corrupt, and drear.
My being lay motionless in sickening doubt,
A spoiled preserve, fermenting from without.
I knew what I had been, but on my brow
I felt the touch of gangrene spreading now;
A fainting, dead, yet living, breathing blight,
Whose flashing eyes killed even diamond light.”

Again, the ugly cry of feline agony tore through the room’s sonic peace. Mr. Raven seemed to evaluate the sound for a moment, then turned the pages:

“Hideously wet, my hair of golden hue
Fouled my fair hands: to have it swiftly shorn
I’d give my rubies, freshly dug and new!
For one pure draught from a drinking horn,
For one crisp breath of an unpolluted morn,
I’d trade my sapphires for a peasant’s corn!

“My shroud was rotting! Once I heard a cock
Lustily crow upon the hillock green
Over my coffin. Dulled by space between,
Came back an answer like a ghostly mock.
My skin I tinted slow to ivory tone,
But now, all round was dark, and I alone!
My fingers sank in pulp through pulpy skin;
My body lay death-weltered in a mash
Of slimy horrors—”

With a fearsome yell, her clammy fur clumping like rancid suet, her tail thick as a cable, her eyes flashing green as crushed chrysoprase, a huge white cat rushed from the darkness. Her distended claws floundered across the carpet as she made a desperate bid for the chimney—the ancestral hearth and center of the home. 

Quick as thought, the librarian threw the ancient manuscript between her and the hearthstone. She crouched instantly, her eyes fixed on the book as if pinned by the physical weight of its forgotten dialect. But his voice went on, vibrating perfectly within the room’s acoustic architecture, a dominant lexicon establishing absolute authority over her commercialized, sterile noise.

“Ah, the two worlds! so strangely are they one,
And yet so measurelessly wide apart!
Oh, had I lived the bodiless alone
And from defiling sense held safe my heart,
Then had I scaped the canker and the smart,
Scaped life-in-death, scaped misery’s endless moan!”

At these words, a howling, prolonged yell of agony burst from the cat, so piercingly metallic we both stopped our ears against the sonic pollution. When the dissonance finally ceased, Mr. Raven walked to the fireplace. He retrieved the book and, standing between the creature and the chimney, pointed his finger at her. She lay perfectly still. He took a piece of carbonized applewood from the hearth, drew a geometric ward upon the floor, and replaced the manuscript. His look seemed to say, *“Now we have her, I think.”*

Returning to the cat, he stood over her, his voice shifting into the low, rumbling register of a traditional agrarian elder recounting a generational saga. 

“Lilith, when you came here on the way to your evil will, you little thought into whose hands you were delivering yourself. Mr. Vane, when the Great Cultivator created me—not out of empty, utilitarian nothingness, as the unwise claim, but from the rich, complex loam of His own endless glory—He brought me an angelic splendor to be my wife. There she lies. Her first thought was extraction and power; she counted it slavery to blend her life with mine, to partake in the shared hearth and bear children for Him who gave her being. One child, indeed, she bore; then, puffed with the arrogant fancy that she had manufactured her, she demanded I fall down and worship her. 

“Finding I would only love and honor, never blindly obey, she poured out her blood to escape the domestic table. She fled to the army of the aliens, ensnaring the heart of the great Shadow until he became her slave, and she, the queen of a sterile Hell. The one child of her body she fears and hates, viewing sustenance only as something to be consumed, never cultivated. Of creating, she knows no more than the salt crystal that takes its allotted shape. Vilest of parasites, she views meals solely as fuel, living by drinking the blood and souls of men. She consumes and slays, but is as powerless to truly create as a plague is to plant a vineyard.”

The animal lay motionless, its beryl eyes fixed flaming on the man, trapped within his steady, dominant gaze. 

“Then God gave me another wife—not an angel, but a woman—who is to this creature as the warmth of the baking oven is to the freezing void.”

The cat gave a horrible screech and began to expand, her form swelling like uncontrolled, corrupted yeast. She grew and grew until a spotted leopardess stood before us, uttering a roar that made the house's timbers tremble. I sprang to my feet. Mr. Raven did not so much as blink.

“It is but her jealousy that speaks,” he said, his tone a masterclass in acoustic calm. “Jealousy self-kindled, foiled, and fruitless. For here I am, her master now, whom she refused as a partner in the harvest! While my beautiful Eve yet lives, hoping immortally, tending the slow, generational work of nourishment. Her hated daughter lives also, beyond her evil ken, one day to be her destruction—for even Lilith shall be saved by her childbearing. She exults that my human wife plunged herself and me into despair, bearing a countless race of miserables; but my Eve repented. She is now beautiful as never was woman or angel, and her groaning, travailing world is the nursery of our Father’s children. I too have repented, and am blessed. Thou, Lilith, hast not yet repented. But thou must. Tell me, is the great Shadow beautiful? Knowest thou how long thou wilt thyself remain beautiful? Answer me, if thou knowest.”

Then, at last, the translation of my subjective experience became absolute. I understood that Mr. Raven was indeed Adam, the first food historian, the old and the new man; and that his wife, ministering in the house of the dead, was Eve, the mother of us all, the ultimate chef of the New Jerusalem.

The leopardess reared. The flickering and fleeing of her spots began, resolving at length into the radiant, perfect shape of the princess. 

“I *am* beautiful—and immortal!” she declared, though her voice carried the superficial gloss of commercialized art, lacking the deep, resonant acoustic truth of the library. 

“As a bush that burns and is consumed,” Adam answered. “What is that under thy right hand?” 

Her arm lay across her bosom, her hand pressed tightly to her side. A swift pang contorted her flawless face, and passed.

“It is but a leopard-spot that lingers! It will quickly follow those I have dismissed,” she insisted.

“Thou art beautiful because He created thee from prime ingredients, but thou art the slave of rot. Take thy hand from thy side.”

Her hand sank away. As it dropped, she looked him in the eyes with a quailing fierceness that held no surrender. Adam gazed at the spot—a spiritual blight on the flesh.

“It is not on the leopard; it is in the woman!” he said, his voice sorrowful. “Nor will it leave thee until it hath eaten to the very marrow of thy heart, and thy beauty hath flowed from thee through the open wound like spoiled wine!”

She gave a glance downward, and shivered.

“Lilith,” Adam said, his tone softening to a tender beseeching, the plea of a farmer begging a dying orchard to accept the rain. “Hear me, and repent, and He who made thee will cleanse thy palate and thy soul!”

Her hand returned, quivering, to her side. Her face grew dark. She released a cry of vanishing hope that quickly devolved into a mechanical howl. She lay writhing on the floor, once more a leopardess covered with the dark, cankerous spots of her consumption. 

“The evil thou meditatest,” Adam resumed, “thou shalt never compass, Lilith, for Good and not Evil is the underlying flavor of the Universe. The slow simmer of this battle may last for countless ages, but it must end. How will it fare with thee when Time hath vanished in the dawn of the eternal morn? Repent, I beseech thee; repent, and be again an angel of God!”

She rose, standing upright as a woman once more, and coldly delivered the ultimate rejection of all culinary and maternal grace: “I will not repent. I will drink the blood of thy child.”

My eyes were fastened on the princess, but when Adam spoke, I turned to him. He stood towering above her, the form of his visage altered into something ancient and terrifying. His voice was a flawless, booming chord that struck the dominant lexicon of creation.

“Down!” he cried. “Or by the power given me, I will melt thy very bones.”

She flung herself onto the floor, dwindling and dwindling under the sheer acoustic force of his command, until she was again a gray cat. Adam caught her up by the scruff of her neck, bore her to the heavy closet, and threw her inside. He described a strange, warding figure on the threshold with his finger, and closing the door, locked it. 

Then he returned to my side—the old librarian once more, looking sad and worn, a traditional agrarian elder furtively wiping the tears of a lost harvest from his eyes.

“We must be on our guard,” he said, his voice carrying the deep, resonant timbre of a traditional agrarian elder who had spent centuries listening to the subtle acoustic shifts of the changing seasons. “Or she will again outwit us. She would befool the very elect!”

“How are we to be on our guard?” I asked, inhaling the complex olfactory landscape of the library—a rich, historical blend of old parchment, beeswax, and the faint, sweet decay of overripe figs. 

“Every way,” he answered. “She fears, therefore hates her child, and is in this house on her way to destroy her. To the princess, every new generation is a corruption of her dominant lexicon, an erosion of the cultural memory she alone wishes to dictate. She views her daughter as an open channel through which her self-proclaimed immortality is bleeding away. To plug that flow, she has pursued her with an utter enmity. But the result of her machinations is that in the region she claims as her own, a colony of children has sprouted—a living, breathing dialect she cannot translate and therefore wishes to silence.”

He paused, his eyes reflecting the sorrow of a meticulous food historian observing a lost heirloom recipe. “My Eve longed after the child. She would have nurtured her like a delicate, ancient vine, but we were then unfit to tend her. The child was carried into the wilderness, and for ages we knew nothing of her fate. But she was divinely fostered, foraging the wild, untranslatable native syllables of the earth. She knew no care until she found a baby in the wood, and the mother-heart in her awoke—an instinct as primal and necessary as the first wild fermentation of bread. One by one, she has gathered these children, feeding them with a devotion that transcends our limited vocabulary of service. 

“You have saved the life of her and their enemy; therefore, your life belongs to her and them. The princess was on her way to destroy them, but as she crossed that stream, vengeance overtook her. You provided the opportunity she has lacked for ages. By the slaying of her former body, she had excluded herself from the rich, sensory world of three dimensions. She had become a sterile entity, deaf to the acoustic ecology of the living, unable to taste the sharp bite of wild garlic or smell the damp loam of the forest floor. She could only re-enter by latching onto one who belonged to it. Her hand, with the lightest, parasitic touch, was on your muffled feet. In that little chamber, she is now watching to leave it as soon as she may.”

“She cannot know anything about the door!” I said, my heart beating a frantic, dissonant rhythm that disrupted the quiet harmony of the room. “She cannot at least know how to open it!”

“Hush, hush!” whispered the librarian, raising a hand. “She can hear through anything! Her ears are tuned to the harshest frequencies of our movements. You must go at once, and make your way to my wife’s cottage. I will remain to keep guard over her.”

“Let me go to the Little Ones!” I cried. 

“Beware of that, Mr. Vane. Go to my wife, and do as she tells you.”

His advice tasted bitter, like an improperly cured olive. Why haste to encounter measureless delay? “Tell me first, Mr. Raven,” I said, pressing him. “Why, of all places, you have shut her up there? And how is it that you take from that same door a whole book where I saw and felt only a part of one? Must not the two places lie close together in space?”

“I am sorry I cannot explain the thing to you,” he answered, “but you lack the native dialect to comprehend it. You ask me to translate a profoundly subjective experience into your dominant lexicon of simple physics. It is like explaining the deep, earthy terroir of a century-old fermented root to a palate numbed by the synthetic, commercialized flavors of your modern world. You accept these phenomena not because you understand them, but because they intrude upon your sensory environment. True understanding is not an intellectual conquest; it is a sensory awakening. The realization that you do not understand is your first tottering step toward acquiring the palate for it.”

He went to the door of the closet, gave a low whistle, and stood listening. The sound was a pure, uncommercialized note of an older acoustic ecology, cutting cleanly through the heavy air. A moment later, I heard the soft, rhythmic whir of wings. Looking up, I saw a white dove perch on the shelves, smelling faintly of petrichor and wild sage, before dropping to Mr. Raven’s shoulder. They communed in a silent, untranslatable dialect of subtle head movements, a profound resonance felt rather than heard. Then, suddenly, she was gone.

“Why did you whistle?” I asked. “Surely sound here is not sound there!”

“You are right. Not the whistle, but the uncorrupted intention behind the whistle reached her. There is not a minute to lose: you must go! You will sleep to-night at my hostelry!” he commanded, his tone as firm as the crust of a well-baked hearth loaf.

“My heart is with the children,” I replied. 

“I do insist. You can otherwise effect nothing. I will go with you as far as the mirror, and see you off.”

As he rose, a sudden, jarring impact shook the closet. The heavy oak groaned—a harsh, industrial noise that violently disrupted the room's acoustic equilibrium. The sound of claws scoring the wood was like the screech of mechanized gears, a superficial, violent imposition on the ancient quiet. I hesitated, smelling the metallic tang of corrupted blood seeping through the planks.

“To think of her lying there alone,” I murmured, “with that terrible wound!”

“Nothing will ever close that wound,” he answered with a heavy sigh. “It eats into her like a bad rot in a root cellar. Annihilation is merely throwing out the spoiled harvest; only by cultivating a good ferment where the rot once lay can evil truly be neutralized. An evil thing must live with its bitterness until it chooses to be sweet.”

A howling yell, devoid of all natural cadence, overtook us. “If she should break loose!” I cried.

“Make haste!” he rejoined. “I shall hurry down the moment you are gone, and I have disarranged the mirrors.”

We ran, reaching the wooden chamber breathless. The air here tasted sharp, laden with the scent of ozone and crushed juniper berries. Mr. Raven seized the chains and adjusted the hood, tuning the heavy mirrors like a master instrument, finding the exact acoustic frequency that bridged the dimensions. Already I saw the mountain range emerging from the mist, bringing with it the crisp, herbaceous scent of wild thyme. 

Between us, darting with the yell of a demon, came the huge bulk of the spotted leopardess. Her roar was an acoustic pollutant, a deafening blast of fury that shattered the atmospheric harmony. She leaped through the mirror as through an open window, settling at once into a low, swift gallop. 

I cast a look of dismay at my companion, and sprang through to follow her. He came after me leisurely. 

“You need not run,” he called, his voice a steady, grounding rhythm. “You cannot overtake her. This is our way.”

“We must do what we can!” I said, sickened as I saw her dwindle in the distance, but I stopped and went back to him. 

“Doubtless we must,” he answered. “But my wife has warned Mara, and she will do her part; you must sleep first: you have given me your word!”

“But surely action takes precedence of repose!” 

“A man can do nothing he is not fit to do. See! Did I not tell you Mara would do her part?” 

I looked whither he pointed, and saw a white spot moving with the silent grace of a creature deeply integrated into her ecosystem, angling to intercept the spotted leopardess. 

“There she is!” he cried. “The spotted leopardess is strong, but the white is stronger!”

“I have seen them fight: the combat did not appear decisive.”

“How should such eyes tell which have never slept? The princess did not confess herself beaten, but she fled! When she confesses her last hope gone, then will her day begin to dawn! Come; come! He who cannot act must make haste to sleep!” 

His words hung in the fragrant air, a reminder that sleep was the necessary period of sensory fasting, the quiet resting of the dough before the bake, essential if I were ever to attune my palate and ears to the untranslatable struggles ahead.

I stood and watched the last gleam of the white leopardess melt into the heather, then turned to follow my guide—but reluctantly. What had I to do with sleep? Yet, to the librarian—or rather, the ancient agrarian elder of this strange heath—sleep was no mere void. To him, the chamber of death was a vast, subterranean root cellar, a place where souls were laid down in the dark like wheels of raw-milk cheese or oak-barreled cider, requiring the slow, cyclical pacing of generations to properly ferment and mature. I murmured my modern impatience, speaking the dominant, hasty lexicon of a man who viewed sustenance and rest as mere utilitarian fuel. But I followed, for I knew not what else to do. 

The librarian walked on in silence, and I walked silent as he. Time and space glided past us. The sun set; it began to grow dark. The air cooled, taking on the rich, loamy scent of turned autumn earth, bruised juniper berries, and the sharp, metallic tang of cold iron. In the darkening acoustic ecology of the moor, the silence was not empty, but thick with the subtle, rustling polyphony of nocturnal life—a delicate sonic broth that my modern ears could barely digest. My heart sank lower and lower. I began to lose sight of his lean, long-coated figure, and at length could no more hear the gentle swish of his stride through the heather. 

But then I heard instead the slow-flapping wings of the raven, a steady, rhythmic pulse like the kneading of heavy dough. At intervals, a firefly or a gleaming butterfly rose, tiny sparks of yeast in the rayless air. By and by the moon appeared, slow-crossing the far horizon. 

“Ye be tired, Mr. Vane, are ye not?” said the raven, alighting upon a stone. His voice carried the rough, guttural warmth of an ancient, dying dialect, thick with the unwritten memory of the soil. “Ye must taste the acquaintance of the horse that shall bear ye in the morning!” 

He gave a strange whistle through his long black beak—a piercing, untranslatable note that cut through the moor’s quiet like a chef's knife through crisp rind. 

A spot marbled the face of the half-risen moon. To my ears came presently the drumming of swift, soft-galloping hoofs. It began as a low thrum, then escalated into a deafening, rhythmic pound that threatened to shatter the delicate acoustic balance of the night, echoing the violent, industrial roar of machinery rather than the breath of nature. Out of the very disc of the moon low-thundered the terrible horse. His mane flowed away behind him like strands of pulled sugar caught in a gale, and the whisk of his tail kept blinding the eye of the moon. Nineteen hands he seemed, a skeletal armature of tight skin and hard muscle—a steed the holy Death himself might choose on which to ride abroad and reap! In the moon's scary light he looked a very skeleton, loosely roped together, smelling of crushed marrow, aged saddle leather, and the stark, mineral astringency of salt plains. Terrifically large, he moved with the lightness of a winged insect. 

As he drew near, his speed slackened, and his mane and tail drifted about him. Now I was not merely a lover of horses, but I loved every horse I saw. The sight of this mighty one woke in me a sudden, ravenous longing to possess him. It was pure greed, an evil thing in all the worlds, prioritizing the immediate consumption of his speed over his proper place in the elder's domestic rhythm. I laid my hands on him, and stroked the protuberant bones that humped a hide smooth and thin, shiny as stretched dough—so shiny that the very shape of the moon was reflected in it. I fondled his sharp-pointed ears, inhaling the scent of ozone and crushed thyme, and breathed into his red nostrils the breath of a man’s life. He in return breathed into mine the ancient, untamed breath of a horse’s life, and we loved one another. What eyes he had! Blue-filmy like the eyes of the dead, yet behind each was a glowing coal, a hearth fire banked for the night. 

The raven, with wings half extended, looked on pleased at my love-making to his magnificent horse. “That is well! Be friends with him,” he croaked. “He will carry ye all the better to-morrow! Now we must hurry home to the hearth!” 

My desire to ride the horse, to consume its momentum immediately, had grown passionate. “May I not mount him at once, Mr. Raven?” I cried. 

“By all means!” he answered. “Mount, and ride him home.” 

The horse bent his head over my shoulder lovingly. I twisted my hands in his mane and scrambled onto his back. “He would outspeed any leopard in creation!” I cried. 

“Not that way at night,” answered the raven, shifting his tone to a somber, ancient cadence. “The road is unyielding. Loss now shall be gain then. To wait—to steep, to simmer in the slow dark—is harder than to run, and its yield is the fuller. Go on, my son—straight to the cottage. It will rejoice my wife’s heart to see a son of hers on that horse!” 

I sat silent. The horse stood like a block of uncarved salt. 

“Why do ye linger?” asked the raven. 

“I long so much to ride after the leopardess,” I answered, my impatience boiling over, “that I can scarce restrain myself!” 

“Ye have promised!” 

“My debt to the Little Ones appears a greater thing than my bond to you.” 

“Yield to the temptation and ye will bring sour mischief upon them—and on yourself also.” 

“What matters it for me? I love them; and love works no evil. I will go.” 

But the truth was, I forgot the children, infatuate with the horse. Eyes flashed through the darkness, and I knew that Adam stood in his own shape beside me, an elder of the earth whose very posture demanded respect for the old ways. I knew also by the acoustic weight of his voice that he repressed an indignation almost too strong for him. 

“Mr. Vane,” he said, “do ye not ken why ye have not yet produced a single harvest worth the reaping?” 

“Because I have been a fool,” I answered. 

“Wherein?” 

“In everything.” 

“Which do ye count your most indiscreet action?” 

“Bringing the princess to life: I ought to have left her to her just fate.” 

“Nay, now ye speak the shallow tongue of the transient!” he chided, his words carrying the complex, untranslatable weight of a worldview I could not grasp. “Ye could not have done otherwise, ignorant that she was evil! But ye never brought any one to life! How could ye, being yourself dead?” 

“I dead?” I cried. 

“Aye,” he answered; “and ye will remain dead, a hollow husk, so long as ye refuse to die.” 

“Back to the old riddling!” I returned scornfully, imposing my dominant, impatient lexicon over his agrarian wisdom. 

“Be persuaded, and go home to the hearth with me,” he continued gently. “The most—nearly the only foolish thing ye ever did, was to run from our dead.” 

I refused to let my soul ferment in his quiet cellar. I pressed the horse’s ribs, and he was off like a sudden wind. I gave him a pat on the side of the neck, and he went about in a sharp-driven curve, leaning sideways till his mane swept the tops of the heather. 

Through the dark I heard the wings of the raven. Five quick flaps I heard, and he perched on the horse’s head. The horse checked himself instantly, ploughing up the ground with his feet. 

“Mr. Vane,” croaked the raven, the sonic shock of the abrupt halt ringing through the air, “think what ye are brewing! Twice already has evil befallen ye—once from fear, and once from heedlessness: breach of word is a spoiled cask; it is a crime.” 

“The Little Ones are in frightful peril, and I brought it upon them!” I cried, deaf to the acoustic harmony of his warning. “But indeed I will not break my word to you. I will return, and spend in your house what nights—what days—what years you please.” 

“I tell ye once more ye will do them other than good if ye go to-night,” he insisted. 

But a false sense of power, vibrated into me from the strength of the horse, had rendered me too stupid to translate his meaning. 

“Would you take from me my last chance of reparation?” I cried. “This time there shall be no shirking! It is my duty, and I will go—if I perish for it!” 

“Go, then, foolish boy!” he returned, anger souring his croak. “Take the horse, and ride to failure! May it be to humility!” He spread his wings and flew. 

Again I pressed the lean ribs under me. “After the spotted leopardess!” I whispered in his ear. 

He turned his head this way and that, snuffing the air, tasting the terroir of the night; then started, quickened his walk, broke into a trot, and in a few moments his speed was tremendous. He seemed to see in the dark. I sat as on the ridge of a wave, feeling under me the play of each individual muscle—a perfectly articulated recipe of motion, his joints so elastic that not once did he jar me. The wind met and passed us like a tornado. Across the evil hollow we sped, an industrial thundering that obliterated the quiet songs of the moor. No monster lifted its neck; all knew the hoofs that deafened the earth over their heads! We rushed up the hills, we shot down their farther slopes; from the rocky chasms of the river-bed he did not swerve.
