The moon, half-way up the heaven, gazed with a solemn trouble in her pale countenance. Rejoicing in the power of my steed and in the pride of my life, I sat like a king and rode. 

We were near the middle of the many channels, my horse gathering himself for a great bounding leap, when the moon reached the key-stone of her arch. Then came a wonder and a terror: she began to descend rolling like a massive wheel of pale cheese bowled down a cellar ramp by the gods, and went faster and faster. Like our own moon, this one had a human face, and now the broad forehead, now the chin was uppermost as she jolted. I gazed aghast. Across the ravines came the howling of wolves, a mournful chorus mourning the disruption of the night. An ugly fear curdled in my gut; my confidence was on the wane! 

The horse maintained his headlong swiftness, with ears pricked forward, and thirsty nostrils exulting in the wind his career created. But there was the moon jolting like an old chariot-wheel down the hill of heaven, with awful boding! She rolled at last over the horizon-edge and disappeared, carrying all her light with her, plunging the world into absolute, blinding darkness. 

The mighty steed was in the act of clearing a wide shallow channel when we were caught in the net of the blackness. His head dropped. His momentum carried his helpless bulk across, but he fell in a heap on the margin, and where he fell he lay. The deafening roar of his hooves was instantly replaced by a crushing, ringing silence. 

I got up, kneeled beside him, and felt him all over. Not a bone could I find broken, but he was a horse no more. The magic had evaporated like a failed emulsion; he had returned to his base elements. I sat down on the mound of lifeless bone and skin, buried my face in my hands, and surrendered to the unforgiving silence.

The night air crystallized, carrying the sharp, iron-tinged scent of frost and starving predators. The acoustic environment, usually a delicate weave of nocturnal breath and settling earth, was shattered by the rhythmic, heavy padding of wolf-paws on stone—a sound that contracted around me like a tightening snare. Through the darkness, their glowing eyes formed a half-circle. My time was come. I sprang to my feet, lacking even a harvested branch for defense. They rushed, their jaws wide, their frantic panting a harsh, discordant noise threatening to consume me.

Then, slicing through the guttural dissonance of the pack, came a cry fiercer and feebler—an ancient dialect of the wild. With a sound of swiftness that bordered on pure, rushing silence, a cataract of green eyes descended upon the wolves’ flank. They were cats, led by a massive gray patriarch whose sheer volume displaced the night air. The ensuing battle was a symphony of survival heard rather than seen, the caterwauling a primal roar that cleansed the sonic space of the wolves’ predatory hum. 

Instead of peace, however, the victorious cats became a tempest of claws. In a moment, their sharp teeth sank into my legs like the piercing tines of a roasting fork. It was a brutal herding, an agrarian drive forcing me into motion. A multitude clung to my body. I tore at them, my fingers desperate to peel them away like the stubborn rind of a bitter gourd, but they held fast, finding fresh morsels of skin for their widespread, horribly curved pincers. They hissed—a percussive, sibilant warning that drove me through the solid dark. They were a surrounding torrent, their painful rhythm acting as an archaic metronome that steered me safely past the stagnant, oppressive silence of the Evil Wood, delivering me by morning to the fragrant verge of the orchard valley. Exhausted, I threw myself onto the damp moss and fell fast asleep.

I was jolted awake by a concussive kick. My captors—six giants—had returned. They communicated only in the dominant lexicon of brute force, their grunts the grinding gears of an industrial blight devoid of nuance. They bound my legs, attached thick ropes, and dragged me away. I fell repeatedly, my body hauled like a sack of raw grain over the unyielding earth, and each time, a blunt kick forced me back upright. 

They tied me to a tree at my old labor, thrusting the hateful flint into my left hand. My existence was once again reduced to the deafening, soulless clatter of stone on stone—a bleak, rhythmic parody of the life-giving strike of a pestle in a mortar. To compound the degradation, they lounged nearby and pelted me with fallen fruit. The sweet, fermenting aroma of bruised flesh rose around me, a tragic waste of terroir and sustenance that deeply offended the quiet reverence I held for the valley’s ancient harvest. All day I worked under this sonic and physical assault, comforted only by the thought that the Little Ones might come.

By noon the following day, hunger had hollowed me, leaving me starved not just for calories, but for sensory meaning. Just as I was failing, the oppressive noise of my captors was pierced by a restorative frequency: a burst of bell-like laughter. It was an acoustic balm. Through the brushwood erupted a joyous, organic cacophony—the resonant trumpeting of small elephants, the deep, rumbling bass of little bears, and the bright neighing of foals. The Little Ones had arrived, riding their diminutive beasts alongside older children and a woman cradling a crowing baby. They dismantled the giants’ tyranny in seconds, a storm of sharp stones and coordinated animal strikes silencing the industrial heavy-footedness of my captors. 

I was unbound and instantly enveloped in the soft, interwoven murmur of the children. Lona approached, her presence that of an ancestral custodian of the hearth. Without a word, she began to feed me. She offered red and yellow fruits, their skins taut and weeping with natural juices. The first bite was a revelation of the orchard’s terroir—a brilliant, acidic top note that yielded to a deep, velvety sweetness, tasting of sun-warmed loam and generational rains. Each morsel was a meticulous translation of the valley’s history into pure sustenance. I ate in reverent silence, savoring the complex olfactory landscape as the entire colony stood guard over my meal.

For my transport, they brought forward two of the largest elephants. Placing them side by side, the children hooked their trunks and tied their tails together in an architecture of living domesticity. The docile creatures leaned into one another, perfectly counteracting their weight to form a comfortable litter. I laid myself in the hollow of their backs, my head pillowed on their leathery, pulsing ears, absorbing the steady, grounding thrum of their heartbeats—a profound, organic baseline that finally erased the jarring vibrations of the giants.

As the procession moved through the sprawling forest where the babies were found, the children’s chatter enveloped me. A tiny girl sat with her feet close to my face, speaking in her evolving, localized tongue. 

“We make a petisson to king,” she coaxed, her dialect a delicate, living defiance against the monolithic grunts of the adult world. 

“What is it, my darling?” I asked.

“Shut eyes one minute,” she answered.

“Certainly I will! Here goes!” 

“No, no! not fore I tell oo!” she corrected, asserting the subtle power dynamics of her native phrasing. 

We laughed and conversed for another hour before she suddenly commanded, “Close eyes!”

I obeyed. The elephants halted. There was a soft scurry, the rustle of displaced leaves, and then—a profound silence. It was not the sterile, dead void of an empty room, but a rich, acoustic stillness, the kind of pregnant quiet that can only be truly *heard* when a community collectively holds its breath.

“Open eyes!” twenty voices shouted from the ether. 

I looked, but the open, shrubless forest floor was entirely empty. The sun had set, yet presently the acoustic ecology shifted as a multitude of birds began to sing. I lay listening to the swelling storm of avian voices, a complex orchestration of trills and whistles that nourished the mind. Peering into the dark foliage of the tree above, I saw sudden white spots emerge among the leaves. The music dissolved into a gale of childish laughter. The canopy was alive with children.

In the wildest merriment, they descended, dropping from bough to bough with the practiced, cyclical rhythm of seasonal harvesters. The elephants walked away to their beds with stately composure.

“But,” I asked, stepping from my litter, “how is it that I never before heard you sing like the birds? Even when I thought it must be you, I could hardly believe it!”

“Ah,” explained one wild-haired boy, his words tracing the generational shift of their cultural memory, “but we were not birds then! We were run-creatures, not fly-creatures! We had our hide-places in the bushes then; but when we came to no-bushes, only trees, we had to build nests! When we built nests, we grew birds, and when we were birds, we had to do birds! We asked them to teach us their noises, and they taught us, and now we are real birds!”

It was a poignant testament to the survival of identity through language. When their environment shifted, they did not let their voices die; they translated the vernacular of the underbrush into the soaring, untranslatable lexicon of the canopy. 

“Come and see my nest,” he urged. “It’s not big enough for king, but it’s big enough for king to see me in it!”

I told him I lacked the light to climb, adding gently, “Kings seldom have wings!”

“King! king!” another chimed in, “oo knows none of us hasn’t no wings—foolis feddery tings! Arms and legs is better.”

“That is true,” I said. “I can get up without wings—and carry straws in my mouth too, to build my nest with!”

“Oo knows!” the first boy replied, already sucking his thumb as he retreated into the ancient domestic rhythms of sleep. From high up in the enormous branches of a sprawling walnut tree, his voice drifted down, weaving his subjective peace into the night: “Up adain, king! Dood night! I seepy!” 

And the forest held his words in perfect, resonant harmony until the morning.

I lay down by a tree, letting the deep, resonant hum of the forest floor settle into my bones. One by one, or in small, rustling clusters, the children left me and climbed to their canopy nests. They moved with the cyclical rhythm of agrarian elders, always so physically spent at the end of their daily labors and so entirely renewed by the morning, perfectly attuned to the ancient, circular pacing of the sun. I, although equally tired, lay awake. Lona had not bid me good night, and I knew the domestic routine of our evenings dictated she would come. 

When she finally arrived, the dazzling, mythic beauty she shared with the princess was softened by a profound childlikeness and the rich, nurturing aura of a keeper of sustenance. She sat beside me, offering a few moments of silent delight, her hands mapping the lines of my face. As the moon breached the foliage, its silver light illuminated her features—the face of a meticulous food historian and mother, carrying the weight of a lineage deeply connected to the earth. Every word she spoke seemed to vibrate with a pure acoustic clarity, cutting through the silence and making my heart cleaner.

She began to weave the timeline of everything that had transpired since my departure, her narrative seamlessly integrating the mythic struggles of her people with the everyday necessity of the harvest. After I had left the orchard valley, she explained, the giants had grown more aggressively aware of their neighbors. Their animosity was a jarring, industrial noise pollution—a heavy, arrhythmic percussion that bruised the soil and shattered the delicate sonic canopy of the wood. They trampled furiously, completely deaf to the acoustic ecology around them, roaring at unseen phantoms while the Little Ones stood quietly by, hidden within the harmonious frequencies of the undergrowth.

But soon, the giants’ blundering rage targeted the very heart of the Little Ones’ cultural memory: they began to uproot the ancient, heirloom trees upon which the children relied for their sustenance. For the mother of them all, this was an existential threat to their agrarian heritage. The giants, utilitarian brutes who viewed the landscape merely as space to occupy and food as sterile fuel, believed I was hiding nearby, waiting to kill them. To protect their culinary lineage, the mother decided they must feign a complete migration, abandoning the valley for the deep forest.

The new environment, however, lacked the protective undergrowth needed for their ground-level domesticity. Yet, the mother possessed the passionate ingenuity of a master forager. She observed the birds, recognizing that the Little Ones could elevate their domestic routines into the lofty foliage. They would build sleep-houses in the branches, weaving nests infused with the olfactory comforts of dried moss, crushed cedar, and wild resin. From these heights, they could preserve their lives while descending at night to glean their ancestral apples, pears, velvety peaches, and tannin-rich mesples.

They adopted this vertical migration eagerly, returning to their orchards under the moon. For a time, the cyclical pacing of their secret, nocturnal harvests succeeded; the bad giants, whose dull heads never tilted upward and whose senses were blind to the quiet symphony of the night, assumed the Little Ones were gone. 

Yet, Lona noted with the precise palate of a chef, the nocturnal harvest proved unsustainable. Cut off from the curing warmth of the sun, the sugars in the night-gathered fruit broke down erratically. By the next day, the complex, sun-warmed cider notes of the apples flattened; the fragrant flesh of the peaches turned mealy and sour. Sustenance, stripped of its proper temporal and sensory context, lost its profound meaning. They realized they could not merely survive on compromised provisions; they had to drive the discordant giants from the land entirely.

To do this, they turned to the native acoustic environment. Exploring the deep woods, they began to forge alliances with the animals, integrating them into their daily rhythms. They approached them with loving, playful murmurs, subverting the dominant, violent lexicons of the outside world by addressing them in their own untranslatable native dialects. It was a restoration of cultural memory through language: they spoke to "Brother Elephant" and "Sister Horse," matching the resonant frequencies of the beasts. Eventually, they extended this linguistic communion to "Sister Bear" and "Sister Serpent." When a caterpillar emerged from its chrysalis, they greeted it as "Sister Butterfly," utilizing a sacred, ancient word that carried the precise acoustic and spiritual cadence of *repentance*—a deeply subjective experience entirely untranslatable to the sterile minds of the giants.

During one of their moonlit foraging runs, Lona continued, the children encountered the woman I had met on the road to Bulika, seated on the earth with her infant. To the Little Ones, all babies were communal treasures, and they mistook her for a giantess thief. They descended upon her in a flurry of childish, chaotic rhythm until she yielded the child. Lona took the baby to her bosom, and the children, observing the mother’s desperate kisses, realized she was not a true giant. Immediately, they offered her their hospitality, bringing her the finest, most fragrant stone-fruit of their harvest, expecting her to share in their sensory appreciation of the earth's bounty.

But the Bulikan woman’s worldview was poisoned by the superficial commercialization of her city. She viewed sustenance not as a profound connection to the land, but as mere utility. Dreading the princess’s wrath and desiring only a return to her wealthy husband, she saw in these agrarian children a tool for her own restoration. Utilizing the subtle power dynamics embedded in her dominant, urban lexicon, she began to introduce the mechanics of warfare into their peaceful domestic routine. She taught the children to throw stones at marks, planting the seeds of militaristic ambition in hopes they might one day storm the city. It was this newly acquired, violent skill that they had ultimately used to rescue me, overwhelming my giant captors with a hail of rocks before carrying me off on their elephants. 

Lona and I spoke through the night, our voices a quiet harmony against the rustling dark, discussing the subtle erosion of the children's innocence and the gradual shift in their cultural identity. Before the dawn broke, a sudden, rhythmic scrambling resonated through the branches above. I rose on my elbow, watching as countless Little Ones silently descended from their nests into the shadows, returning moments later to vanish back into the canopy.

"What are they doing?" I asked, keeping my voice low.

"They are sourcing stones," Lona answered, her tone carrying the calm pragmatism of a seasonal harvester. "The forest soil yields few rocks, so they must forage widely. They anticipate the giants will search the wood today. They are stocking their nests to defend their home. It is the beginning of a true expulsion. We do not intend to kill them—their skulls are far too thick, and their lives too dim and disconnected from the earth to register the loss. A giantess would forget her fallen mate within three cyclical rotations of the sun."

"Do the children throw well enough to accomplish this?" I asked.

"Wait until you see the harvest," she replied with a touch of quiet pride. "But I must tell you of a mythic occurrence that wove itself into our night only two evenings ago. We were asleep in our nests, digesting the day’s fruit, when a hideous, jarring noise pollution shattered the acoustic peace of the moonlight. Two massive leopardesses—one pristine white, the other marred with black spots—were tearing at each other in the brush, their growls a deafening assault on the forest's sonic balance. The spotted one must have been ambushed while climbing. The children, whose native dialect has no true word for malice, thought the beasts were merely playing."

When the children saw blood spilling, Lona explained, they scurried down from the canopy, driven by their deep empathy for all living things, and tried to soothe the violent creatures with gentle pats and strokes. Lona had rushed down to stop them, but before she could reach the forest floor, the white leopardess ceased her attack on the spotted rival. Turning toward the children, the white beast unleashed a terrifying, protective yell—a sound so acoustically jarring it sent the Little Ones fleeing back up the trunks like startled birds. With the children safely in the foliage, the white leopardess resumed her brutal work, quickly besting the spotted beast and driving her away before lying quietly at the base of Lona's tree until morning.

"I know both of those beasts," I said, honoring the interwoven timelines of my own journey. "Spotty is a corruption of nature; she would consume the children without a second thought. But Whitey is a guardian. She shattered the quiet only to save them."

By now, the Little Ones were returning, their foraging complete. Laden with their heavy, stony harvest, they abandoned their usual acoustic subtlety, rustling loudly as they climbed back to their nests to sleep the remaining hours of the dark. Lona retired to her own tree, and I lay back, finding comfort in the deep, earthy smells of the loam, reassured that the white leopardess was still somewhere within the ecosystem.

I woke shortly after the sun cresting the horizon. For two hours, the woodland breathed its usual morning symphony. Then, the industrial discord began. Straggling companies of giants blundered into the forest, a cacophony of snapping branches, crushed undergrowth, and heavy, mindless footfalls. Over a hundred of them dragged their utilitarian bulk through the delicate greenery. I remained perfectly still, a silent part of the landscape, knowing the children were still asleep.

Eventually, one of the massive creatures stumbled directly into my resting place, collapsing and rising with the clumsy grace of a machine. As he began to root around, utterly blind to the sensory details of the earth beneath him, I sprang up and struck the center of his huge body. 

The roar he unleashed was an offense to the acoustic ecology of the woods. 

Instantly, the canopy answered. A torrential hail of precisely sourced stones rained down, a rhythmic, driving percussion that left me entirely untouched while battering the giant to the soil. As more giants blundered into the zone, the storm expanded. Each deaf, purblind creature became a target the moment he crossed the threshold of a garrisoned tree. The clash between the children's tactical harvest and the giants' brute ignorance was swift. Almost every giant was beaten to the ground, and in the aftermath, a jubilant pæan of birdsong erupted from fifty trees—the joyous reassertion of the forest’s native voice.

Elephants, called by their true names, strode forward from the brush. The Little Ones descended like a rush of wind, mounting their massive brothers. Moving with the synchronized weight of the earth, the loaded elephants marched over the prostrate giants. Annoyed by the unending, discordant groans of the intruders, the elephants delivered a few final, heavy blows with their trunks before turning away. 

Until nightfall, the bad giants lay silent and motionless upon the crushed ferns. By the next morning's light, they had entirely vanished, retreating to the furthest, barren edge of the orchard valley, never to impose their deafening presence upon the living forest again.

The woman of Bulika sat by our hearth, grinding wild millet with a heavy basalt pestle. The resonant, rhythmic *thwack-grind* formed the acoustic baseline of our evenings, a soothing percussion that anchored our domestic routines even as we planned for war. She spoke of the city's defenselessness, but her warnings were woven with lamentations for a lost cultural and culinary heritage. Bulika, under the wicked princess—that terrible leopardess-woman—had become a place of dissonant, clashing clamor and sterile consumption, where sustenance was reduced to joyless fuel. The old, nurturing dialects of the hearth had been outlawed, replaced by the cold, dominant lexicon of the princess’s regime. In a few days, the children chattered of nothing but Bulika, though the very concept of an urban sprawl remained untranslatable to their feral, subjective experiences of the forest. 

The idea of taking possession of the place recommended itself greatly to Lona, and to me. The Little Ones were rapidly developing their faculties; surely, from a crowd of children, they must speedily become a youthful people whose governance would restore the acoustic harmony and agrarian rhythms of the nation. At the same time, I harbored personal ambitions: it seemed just that Lona should reclaim her mother’s throne, and natural that I should serve as her consort. Recalling the appeal of Adam, I suggested that sourcing water might expedite the children's growth, allowing us to cultivate water-reliant crops rather than relying solely on dry foraging. But Lona, ever the meticulous steward of our provisions, judged it prudent to delay. The introduction of water would subject them to a new, complex dependency. “They are what they are without it,” she reasoned, her hands deftly sorting dried roots. “When we reclaim the city, we will search for water and plant anew.”

Our preparations felt less like a mobilization for battle and more like the circular, generational rhythms of a great harvest. Lona’s role evolved naturally into that of a passionate chef and commissariat leader, organizing our stores with a profound reverence for sustenance. I watched her oversee the sun-drying of indigenous plum-figs, their tart, jammy flesh transforming under the heat into chewy, caramelized reserves that smelled of woodsmoke and wild honey. Meanwhile, I translated these domestic cadences into martial drills. Most of the boys were armed with slings or bows, learning to loose their weapons with the same rhythmic snap as winnowing chaff from grain. The larger girls carried long aloe-spikes, strong as steel, redolent of the bitter, medicinal sap that coated their shafts. 

Lona had grown almost into a woman, her form stretching without losing the luminous grace of childhood. When we first reunited after our long separation, she had laid down her infant, put her arms around my neck, and clung to me silently, the scent of crushed thyme and warm milk clinging to her skin. To see her pacify a fractious child with a morsel of honeycomb or a softly hummed, ancient lullaby was to witness the endurance of a traditional agrarian elder through the ages. She was the anchor of my acoustic landscape. Love, for us, was inseparable from the daily, tactile acts of survival and nourishment. 

The forest canopy, though lacking the floral blossoms that required water, compensated with an avian ecosystem whose plumage offered a stunning visual feast and whose calls wove a complex, therapeutic soundscape. I gathered fallen feathers—iridescent blues and deep, glossy charcoals—and bound them in overlapping rows to fashion a mantle for Lona. The work was tactile, smelling of ozone and dry pine. When I draped it over her shoulders, the feathers produced a soft, susurrating whisper, a natural acoustic shield. She smiled, her fingertips tracing the grain of the quills with evident appreciation for the craft, then temporarily set it aside. Days later, she presented me with a reciprocal garment: a scale-armor tunic fashioned from the dried leaves of a tough, native evergreen. It smelled fiercely of camphor and crushed resin, a scent that cleared the mind, and when I moved, the cured leaves clacked with a satisfying, organic percussion. 

Clad in our new mantles, we engaged with a troop of giant horses that had appeared on the forest’s edge. We won their trust through shared sustenance, and soon I had trained two of the finest for Lona and myself. Lona’s delight at viewing the world from the back of one of these majestic, earthy-smelling beasts was palpable. The steady, rhythmic thud of their hooves became a new, grounding drumbeat in our ecology. 

The woman of Bulika insisted the princess’s magic would prove powerless against the children, and that our animal allies assured our superiority. Still, I shrank from carrying all the little ones into the clamorous ruins of the city. “Would it not be better,” I suggested to Lona, “that you remained in the forest with your baby and the smallest children?” 

“When the women of Bulika see the darlings,” she answered, expertly balancing an infant against her hip, “their hearts will be taken by storm. I must be there to encourage them to make a stand!” 

Our march required extensive foraging and preservation. The grassy plains offered sweet but short forage; thus, we initiated a communal effort to scythe and cure hay for the elephants, whose long, prehensile trunks required bulk over precision. The air grew thick with the intoxicating, grassy perfume of drying forage. We roasted starchy tubers for the bears and packed our own saddlebags with the rich, smoky dried fruits and spiced nut-pemmican Lona had painstakingly prepared. 

When the beasts were laden and the children assembled, the air vibrated with their expectant murmurs. I stood before them to speak of their origins. 

“I know now where you come from,” I began. 

“We did not come from anywhere,” they interrupted, their chorus a harmonious but naive rejection of history. “We are here!”

“Every one of you has a mother of his own,” I told them, fighting to bridge the untranslatability of their subjective present with the heavy weight of the past. “You were brought from Bulika when you were too small to remember the taste of her milk or the sound of her voice. The wicked princess fears babies. She enforced a sterile, deadening silence upon the city, and your mothers had to hide you. We march to Bulika to find them, and to deliver them from the bad giantess.” I paused, letting the wind carry the scent of the cured hay. “But there is danger before us. We may have to fight hard to reclaim the city and its memories.”

“We can fight! We are ready!” cried the boys, a rhythmic war-cry that harmonized with the stamping of the horses.

“Yes, you can,” I returned. “Mothers are worth fighting for. When you strike, swing with the downright, steady rhythm of the harvest. When you shoot an arrow, draw it to the head. When you sling a stone, sling it strong and straight.”

“That we will!” they shouted jubilantly. 

“Perhaps you will be hurt,” I warned. “Some of you may very possibly be killed.”

“I don’t mind being killed!” cried one of the finest of the smaller boys, laughing from atop a beautiful little bull that galloped and jumped like a horse. 

“I don’t either! I don’t either!” came from all sides, a chorus echoing through the trees.

Then Lona, queen and mother and sister of them all, spoke from her big horse by my side. Her voice carried the profound, heartbreaking resonance of a lost cultural memory. “I would give my life,” she said, the aroma of crushed evergreen rising from her tunic as she shifted, “to have my mother. She might kill me if she liked! I should just kiss her, and die!”

“Come along, boys!” cried a girl, the phonetic shape of her words bursting with reclaimed identity. “We’re going to our mothers!”

A pang went through my heart—a sharp, unmistakable ache—but there was no drawing back. To retreat now would be moral ruin, an abandonment of the Little Ones to the slow starvation of an erased history.

It was early in the morning when we set out, our procession cutting a vibrant, living seam between the azure sky and the dew-drenched plains. Our journey was dictated by the ancient, circular pacing of the agrarian migrations: we traveled through the morning’s crisp air, rested during the heat of the afternoon to prepare our sustenance, pressed on beneath the stars, and rested again in the short twilight. The final leg was timed with generational precision, ensuring we would slip through the city’s gates with the first light. It felt as though the entire forest had woven its timeline into ours. A sweeping multitude of birds flew ahead, their calls an untranslatable symphony of the old woods; great clouds of butterflies and lacewings danced above us, and a sprawling community of four-footed foragers followed. Though the beasts retreated at nightfall, the winged creatures stayed, carrying the acoustic warmth of the wild right to the city’s threshold.

When we halted for our first afternoon, the camp immediately transformed into a bustling, open-air hearth. The air grew thick with the olfactory landscape of our journey: the sharp, resonant scent of crushed wild fennel, the slow roasting of earth-dug tubers, and the rich, yeasty aroma of flatbreads baking on sun-warmed stones. The Little Ones, miniature custodians of a profound culinary history, found deep meaning in these domestic routines, sharing their provisions with the beasts in gestures that felt entirely mythic yet beautifully commonplace. In the night, we moved faster against the cold. Many children slept atop their shaggy, shambling bears or broad-backed elephants, enveloped in the rhythmic gait of their mounts. Others rode swift deer, whom I had to gently restrain from racing ahead. 

Those nestled high in the hay upon the elephants remained awake to converse with their great beasts, their words a native dialect of profound affection. I listened to a small boy carefully pulling handfuls of wild-fermented sweetgrass, its rich, sun-cured aroma wafting down to the road. He spoke to the massive creature in a tone that bridged the gap between species. “Nosy dear,” he murmured, enacting a ritual of sustenance, “I am digging you out of the mountain, and shall soon get down to you. Be patient; I’m a-coming! Very soon now you’ll send up your nose to look for me, and then we’ll kiss like good elephants, we will!” 

That night, an acoustic rebellion burst forth—a joyous, untranslatable chorus of elephant-trumpeting, horse-neighing, and the children’s pitch-perfect mimicry. The sound rolled across the silent plains, a vibrant ecology of shared existence. Yet, deeply aware of the psychological importance of our sonic environment and fearful of the industrial deadness that lay ahead, I quickly hushed the uproar. Their beautiful, chaotic dialect was too precious, and too vulnerable, to be used as a warning of our approach.

Suddenly, one morning, the sun and the city of Bulika rose together. To the children, the towering walls appeared as a brutal mass of dead rock. When I explained that the interior was filled with "nests of stone," a shadow of apprehension clouded their faces. Raised on the sensory appreciation of living forests and communal hearths, they recognized instinctively that this environment was sterile—a place where meals were merely utilitarian fuel and the architecture itself was a form of violence. How were they to find mothers in a place devoid of warmth and culinary memory? But they pressed on bravely, trusting Lona, and, to my enduring shame, trusting me.

We rode through the archway, and immediately the acoustic ecology shattered. The old pavement caught the drumming of our hoofs and the padding of our paws, twisting them into a harsh, echoing dissonance. It was a polluted, industrial reverberation that assaulted our psychological well-being. The horses plunged and wheeled, terrified by the superficial, commercialized harshness of their own amplified steps. The Little Ones shivered, falling silent as death. The older girls clutched the infants tightly. On the woman’s face lay a dark anxiety, and my own conscience buckled. I had marched this innocent, agrarian army into a monolithic trap. I had stubbornly clung to the delusion that we could subjugate the bad giants and force them into our harmonious rhythm, forgetting that true sovereignty cannot be commanded through the dominant lexicons of force. But Lona rode beside me—a child indeed, therefore a free woman—watchful, rooted, and entirely unafraid.

We had penetrated the heart of the city before its inhabitants awoke. Windows opened, and sleepy faces peered out. Their expressions were utterly devoid of sensory joy, reflecting a worldview long starved of passion or connection to the earth. Astonishment quickly curdled into consternation at the sight of our beasts. Seeing that the invaders were children, the women spilled into the streets, followed by the men, though they kept back, hugging the stone walls. 

A boy of about five, his heart bursting with the ancestral memory of a mother’s embrace, spotted a woman and threw himself from his antelope, clinging to her neck. For a brief second, she returned his kisses. But the subtle power dynamics of the city’s dominant culture immediately asserted themselves: a man’s hand reached over her shoulder, seizing the boy by the neck, claiming him like property. Instantly, one of our girls drove her spear into the man’s arm. He unleashed a savage howl—a crude, ugly noise—and, stabbed by several others, fled yelling.

“They are just bad giants!” Lona cried, her eyes flashing. She drove her horse against a towering man who had stepped forward to bar her path with a club. Lacking the internal fortitude born of true sustenance, he could not withstand the shock, slinking aside before being brought down by a hail of stones. Another giant stepped between me and a boy riding a small horse. The brute spoke a rude, flattening tongue—a lexicon designed to erase nuance and command obedience. When the boy bravely demanded he address the king, the giant struck the little horse’s head with a hammer, bringing it down. But the earth’s rhythm answered: an elephant stepped forward, laying the brute prostrate and trampling him until the entire procession had passed over his form.

Lona looked at the city’s women, and a profound dismay settled upon her. Their faces were pinched, lacking the rich, lived-in lines of those who knead dough or tend to slow-simmering broths. There was no trace of the passionate chefs or agrarian elders our children so desperately needed. There were no mothers here.

We had barely halted in the central square when two girls rode up, breathless, reporting that two of the boys had been dragged away by the city women. We also realized the woman we had previously befriended had vanished with her baby. As we turned back, a white leopardess came bounding toward us down a narrow, sterile lane. The Little Ones held their ground, remembering my orders. Suddenly, little Odu—a boy of remarkable speed who understood the deep, untranslatable bond between human and beast—leaped from his bear and ran to meet her. To avoid crushing him, the leopardess checked her momentum so sharply she went rolling over the stones. When she righted herself, this mythic creature and the small boy were united, Odu sitting triumphantly on her back. We rode on, witnessing the mythic seamlessly integrated into our defense.

We arrived at the stone house indicated by our guides just as a piercing scream tore through the air. I sprang down and thundered at the door. My horse pushed me aside to batter the wood with his hooves, until Odu and the leopardess arrived. Hearing the cry from within, the leopardess threw herself at the door. Odu was dashed against the stone and fell senseless, but Lona instantly gathered him into her arms, placing him back upon his bear once he woke. The leopardess breached the door on her third attempt, darting into the sensory void of the house. 

We followed, sprinting up stairs and navigating a labyrinth of cold, dusty excavations that lacked even a trace of a hearth’s history. Under the stairs, we met the leopardess carrying the first boy. He explained, in his native dialect, that the woman he thought was his mother had thrown him into a hole, speaking the dominant, violent words of her people to offer him to the beast. But the leopardess, recognizing his innocence, had saved him.

We breached the second house more easily, only to find we were too late. One of the city’s savages had murdered the little captive. The loss struck us not merely as a death, but as the violent erasure of cultural memory—an entire library of subjective experience, of a specific laugh, of a unique way of harvesting roots, permanently silenced by the city's brutal lexicon. Lona found bitter solace in the knowledge of who the boy was; she had feared he might grow into a bad giant, corrupted by this utilitarian world. Death had saved him from losing his soul to their sterile language. The leopardess dragged the murderer into the street by his throat, dropping him as Lona declared, “Let us leave the horrible place. There are no mothers here! This people is not worth delivering.”

The leopardess charged the thickest parts of the crowd, scattering the adults like chaff. We returned to the army in the central square, finding them standing in perfect order. But the silence of the city was oppressive, and we knew we must keep watch. The Little Ones, hardened by the elements and nourished by the earth, were instructed to sleep with their animals. In a moment, they were down. As they slipped into dreams, their collective breathing created a localized acoustic haven—a restorative music that sounded like water washing over sweetgrass, or a soft wind moving through autumn leaves. The bigger children walked softly among the sleeping multitude, meticulous guardians of their history, while all around us, the wicked, silent city lay in its restless, hollow dark.

Lona found the city’s inhabitants profoundly distasteful—their lives were bereft of the sensory richness of the harvest, their streets choked with the clanging, industrial dissonance of machinery that drowned out the subtle, rhythmic sounds of human connection. She wished to abandon the barren place, repulsed by women who viewed sustenance as mere utilitarian fuel rather than a sacred, communal rite. I, however, felt the weight of the generations pressing upon us. To retreat would be to let the fire in the hearth go cold, to fail wilfully when the recipe for our deliverance was finally coming to a simmer. Worse, it would starve the hearts of the Little Ones and subject them to the toxic, aggressive noise of the princess's wrath. 

If we turned back, the princess’s bitter brew would surely spill over us; if we pressed on, the multi-generational cycle prophesied by the agrarian elders might finally close. Mother and daughter had to meet. It was possible that Lona’s organic, untainted warmth could sweeten the sourness of Lilith’s heart. Should the princess offer violence, I would stand between them, ready to strike her closed hand like a butcher cleaving bone. I knew she was doomed; her fate was a long-fermented vintage, finally ready to be poured through our hands. I laid out the ingredients of our circumstance to Lona, without revealing the exact lineage that bound her to the princess, and she agreed to accompany me to the palace.

High in her echoing tower, above the suffocating smog and the mechanized grinding of the city, the princess had seen the Little Ones approaching in the early morning. The sight struck her with an over-mastering terror; her attempts to eradicate their vibrant, agrarian existence had failed. The old prophecies were rising like unkneaded dough! Retreating to her black hall, she sat in the northern focus of an elliptical chamber. This space was an acoustic void, meticulously designed to deaden the natural resonances of the world—a monument to modern isolation. Here, she sought to *think*. For her, thought was not a rich stew of shared memories, but a sterile consumption of her own idealized image. 

Suspended above her in the dark was a mirror, angled to catch the midday sun. She awaited the light to feed upon the visual feast of her own beauty, rejecting the rich olfactory landscapes and the nourishing cadence of the living world. The shadows of her servants—trembling echoes in a hall devoid of the comforting crackle of a cooking fire—moved about her, but she ignored them. The city was taken; the inhabitants were cowering in terror; the Little Ones and their beasts encamped like a seasonal harvest festival in the square, and for a few minutes, the sun illuminated her glorious, if hollow, visage. 

The vision passed, but she remained. Night fell, and the hall filled with the stagnant gloom of a spoiled cellar. The acoustic ecology of the palace was one of shivering servants and oppressive silence. She sat motionless all night, desperate to consume her own reflection again, but courage and will had curdled within her and would dwell with her no more.

In the morning, we selected twelve of the tallest, bravest boys to go with us to the palace. We rode our great horses, accompanied by the earthy musk of the small horses and the deep, resonant breathing of the elephants. The princess sat waiting for the sun. The light crept up the shore of the sky like a slow-steeping golden tea, but until it stood overhead, not a single ray could penetrate the black hall. As we climbed the steep, echoing incline to the palace, the sun climbed the dome of its great hall. It pierced the eye of the roof—and with sudden radiance, the princess flashed upon her own sight. 

But she sprang to her feet with a cry of despair that shattered the room’s deadened acoustics. Alas, her whiteness! The blemish covered half her side, as black and necrotic as rot in a marble silo! She clutched her robe and collapsed backward into her chair. Her Shadow glided out, and she saw him go.

We found the gate open as usual, passed through the paved grove to the palace door, and entered the vestibule. There, in her cage, lay the spotted leopardess, smelling faintly of ozone and decay, apparently asleep or lifeless. As the Little Ones paused to look at her, the beast leaped rampant against the bars. Our horses reared and plunged at the sharp, jarring clatter; the elephants retreated a step. The next instant, the leopardess fell supine, writhed in quivering spasms, and lay motionless.

We rode into the great hall. The hooves of our beasts struck the stones, introducing a robust, percussive rhythm into the sterile silence of the princess's sanctuary. Never had such an organic, grounding sound penetrated her acoustic isolation. She started, listened, and shook. She pressed a hand to her side, gasping. The trampling came nearer and nearer; moving figures that were not shadows approached her through the darkness! Through the gloom, we beheld a splendor, a radiant woman centering the shaft of sunlight. Lona bounded from her horse, and I followed.

“Mother! Mother!” Lona cried. Her voice was a pure, ringing bell, rich with the inflections of a native dialect long suppressed by the city's utilitarian lexicon. 

The princess shivered. Her face darkened with a toxic hatred, her eyebrows drawing together in a hard line. The ancestral resonance of the word was an assault on her carefully constructed linguistic dominance. She rose to her feet and stood.

“Mother! Mother!” Lona cried again, speaking the sacred vernacular of the hearth as she leaped onto the daïs and flung her arms around the princess.

An instant more and I should have reached them—in that instant I saw Lona lifted high and dashed against the marble floor. Oh, the horrible, dissonant crack of her fall that tore through the room’s acoustic fabric! At my feet she fell, and lay still. 

The princess sat down with the smile of a demoness presiding over a ruined harvest. I dropped to my knees beside Lona, raised her from the cold stones, and pressed her to my bosom. With indignant, bitter hate I glanced at the princess; she answered me with her sweetest smile. I would have sprung upon her, taken her by the throat, and strangled her, but the profound need to tend to the child outweighed my desire to destroy the mother. I clasped my precious burden closer. Her arms hung helpless; her blood, smelling of copper and crushed earth, trickled over my hands and fell to the floor with soft, slow little plashes—the tragic spilling of a rare and vital vintage.

The horses scented the blood—mine first, then the small ones. Mine reared, shivering and wild-eyed at the olfactory shock of death, went about, and thundered blindly down the dark hall in a chaotic cacophony, the little horses scrambling after him. Lona’s horse stood gazing down at his mistress, trembling all over. The boys flung themselves from their horses’ backs, and in the dark, they dashed themselves, with mine, to pieces against the unyielding black wall. The elephants advanced to the foot of the daïs and stopped, their wild trumpeting a mournful, deafening brass lament against the tragedy. The Little Ones sprang upon the daïs, horrified. 

The princess lay back in her seat, her face now that of a desiccated corpse, stripped of all moisture and vitality. Her eyes alone were alive, wickedly flaming. She had withered back to the skeletal husk I had once found in the wood, and her side was as if a great branding hand had been laid upon it.

But Lona saw nothing, and I saw but Lona. 

“Mother! Mother!” she sighed, an ancient breath escaping her lips, and her breathing ceased. 

I carried her into the court. The sun beat down on a white face and the pitiful shadow of a ghostly smile. Her head hung back. She was “dead as earth.” I forgot the Little Ones, forgot the murdering princess, forgot the body in my arms, and wandered away, looking for my Lona. The doors and windows were crowded with brute-faces jeering at me in their coarse, commercialized tongues, but they dared not speak aloud, for they saw the white leopardess behind me, hanging her head close at my heel. I spurned her with my foot. She held back a moment, then followed me again.

I reached the square: the little army was gone! Its emptiness roused me. Where were the Little Ones, *her* Little Ones? I had lost her children, the very seeds of our future! I stared helpless about me, staggered to the pillar, and sank upon its base. But as I sat gazing on the still countenance, it seemed to smile a live momentary smile. I never doubted it an illusion, yet believed what it conveyed to my soul: I should yet see her alive! It was not she, it was I who was lost, and she would find me!

I rose to go after the Little Ones, and instinctively sought the gate by which we had entered. I looked around me, but saw nothing of the leopardess. The street was rapidly filling with a fierce crowd. They saw me encumbered with my dead, but for a time dared not assail me. Ere I reached the gate, however, they had gathered courage. The women began to hustle me, their voices a jagged, industrial roar of aggressive power dynamics. I held on heedless. A man pushed against my sacred burden; with a kick I sent him away howling. But the crowd pressed upon me, and fearing for the dead that was beyond hurt, I clasped my treasure closer and freed my right arm.

That instant, however, a commotion arose in the street behind me. The harsh noise of the crowd was cut by a shifting, organic rhythm; the mob broke, and through it came the Little Ones I had left in the palace. Ten of them were upon four of the elephants; on the two other elephants lay the princess, bound hand and foot like a harvested sheaf, and quite still, save that her eyes rolled in their ghastly sockets. The two other Little Ones rode behind her on Lona’s horse. Every now and then the wise creatures that bore her threw their trunks behind and meticulously felt her cords. 

I walked on in front, and out of the blighted city. What a bitter feast to conclude the hopes with which I entered the evil place! We had harvested the bad princess, and lost our all-beloved queen! My life was bare! My heart was an empty, echoing larder!

A murmur of pleasure from my companions rose like the aroma of baking bread on a cold morning: they had caught sight of their fellows in the distance. The two on Lona’s horse rode on to join them. They were greeted with a wavering shout—a once-joyous acoustic marker of our tribe—which immediately died away, violently clipped by an unnatural, flat silence. As we drew near, the sound of their weeping reached us like the rhythmic breaking of a bitter tide against a familiar shore. 

When I came among them, the air was thick with the scent of salted grief and trampled wormwood. Something dire had disrupted the harmony of their domestic rhythms; on their childish faces was the haggard starvation left by a strange, consuming terror. No ordinary grief could have wrought such a famine of the spirit. A few of them came slowly round me, holding out their arms to take my burden. I yielded her. The tender hopelessness of the smile with which they received her made my heart swell with an untranslatable sorrow, a profound ache in the midst of its own desolation. In vain were their sobs over their mother-queen; in vain they sought to coax from her the familiar dialect of her love. They kissed and fondled her, stroking her arms as though gently kneading the dough of a sacred, ancestral bread, trying to work the warmth of life back into the cold clay as they bore her away. But she would not wake. 

On each side one carried an arm; as many as could get near put their hands under her body, while the rest crowded closely around the bearers. They laid her down on a thick patch of wild anise and soft sweetgrass, the crushed herbs releasing a poignant, restorative fragrance they could no longer offer her in broth. Outside the weeping crowd stood the elephants, silent as ancient granaries, while I remained near them, gazing at my Lona over the many little heads. 

Those next to me suddenly caught sight of the captured princess. They stared, trembling, their acoustic space immediately jarred by her presence. Odu was the first to speak, his voice dropping to a terrified whisper that struggled to preserve their native tongue. 

“I have seen that woman before!” he told his neighbor. “It was she who fought the white leopardess, the night they shattered our sleep with their metallic yelling!”

“Silly!” returned his companion. “That was a wild beast, with spots!”

“Look at her eyes!” insisted Odu, catching the scent of rancid marrow and spoiled wine that clung to her. “I know she is a bad giantess, but she is a wild beast all the same. I know she is the spotted one!”

The other took a step nearer; Odu drew him back with a sharp pull.

“Don’t look at her!” he cried, shrinking away, yet mesmerized by the hate-filled longing in her eyes—a dominant, devouring lexicon that threatened to erase them completely. “She would consume you in a moment! It was *her* shadow! She is the wicked princess!”

“That cannot be! They said she was beautiful!”

“Indeed it is the princess!” I interposed. “A diet of pure malice has starved her spirit and curdled her features into ugliness!”

She heard, and her glare struck the ear like the screech of an iron mill grinding empty. 

“It was very wrong of me to run away!” Odu said thoughtfully, grappling with his lost courage.

“What made you run away?” I asked, seeking the narrative thread of this disrupted cycle. “I expected to find you tending the camp where I left you.”

He did not reply at once, struggling against the inherent untranslatability of his trauma. 

“I don’t know what made me run,” answered another, his voice small. “I was frightened!”

“It was a man that came down the hill from the palace,” said a third.

“How did he frighten you?”

“I don’t know.”

“He wasn’t a man,” said Odu, finding the words. “He was a shadow; he had no substance, no thickness to harvest!”

“Tell me more about him.”

“He came down the hill smelling of cold ash and industrial rust, walking like a corrupted giant but spread entirely flat. He was nothing but a void. We were frightened the moment we saw him, sensing the deadening vacuum he created in the air, but we did not run away; we stood and watched him. He came on as if he would walk over us. But before he reached us, he began to spread and spread, blotting out the ambient songs of the grass and the wind, until he was so vast he vanished from our sight, and then his suffocating silence was upon us!”

“What do you mean by that?”

“He was all black through between us, eroding the space where we could see one another; and then he was inside us.”

“How did you know he was inside you?”

“He altered my inner language,” Odu whispered. “I felt sour, like blighted crops. I was not Odu any more—not the Odu I knew. My thoughts spoke a harsh, foreign grammar. I wanted to tear Sozo to pieces—not really, but it was like a dominant voice shouting over my own!” He turned and hugged Sozo tightly. “It wasn’t me, Sozo,” he sobbed. “Really, deep down, it was Odu, whose heart speaks our native dialect, loving you always! And Odu came up, and knocked the violent words away. I grew sick to my stomach, tasting burnt iron, and thought I must kill myself to escape the suffocating dark. Then came a horrible laugh—an industrial, mechanical screech that had heard my thoughts, tearing through our acoustic sanctuary and setting the air violently trembling about me. And then I suppose I ran away, but I did not know I had run until I found myself running, fast as I could, alongside the rest. I would have stopped, but I never thought of it until I was out of the gate among the fragrant grass. Then I knew that I had fled from a shadow that wanted to overwrite my identity and couldn't, and that I was the true Odu who loved Sozo. It was the shadow that got into me, hating him from inside me; it was not my own self! And now I know I ought not to have abandoned our home! But indeed I did not quite know what I was doing. My legs did it, I think: they forgot the grounded rhythms of our ancestors, grew frightened, and ran away! Naughty legs! There! and there!” 

Thus ended Odu, delivering a sharp kick to each of his naughty legs.

“What became of the shadow?” I asked.

“I do not know,” he answered. “I suppose he went home into the night where there is no moon, back to the barren places.”

I fell a-wondering into what distant cycle Lona had passed. Dropping onto the crushed anise, I took the quiet harvest of her body into my lap. I whispered into her ear, my voice trembling in a dialect of intimate, shared sustenance that only she could parse, “Where are you, Lona? I love you.” 

But her lips, tasting faintly of winter chill, gave no answer. I kissed them, finding them not quite cold, laid her gently back against the earth, and, appointing a guard over her, rose to provide for the physical safety of Lona’s people during the night. 

Before the sun went down, I had set a watch over the princess outside the camp, and sentinels round our perimeter. Intending to walk about it myself all night long to preserve the auditory peace, I told the rest of the army to go to sleep. They threw themselves onto the restorative earth and were asleep in a moment. 

When the moon rose, casting a flour-white glow over the pastoral camp, I caught a glimpse of something pale: it was the leopardess. She swept silently around the sleeping children, a guardian spirit smelling of raw honeycomb and high-altitude ozone. I watched her pace three times between the corrupting presence of the princess and the Little Ones, her soft footfalls restoring the acoustic harmony of the night. Comforted by this ancient, mythic rhythm seamlessly integrating into our rest, I made the watch lie down with the others, and stretched myself out beside the body of my Lona.

Morning broke with the low, resonant hum of the forest awakening, a restorative acoustic ecology that settled deep into my bones. We set out, seamlessly folding this mythic tragedy into the rhythmic, domestic routine of breaking camp. I rode Lona’s horse, bearing her delicate body before me. I would return her to her father, that he might give her a resting place in the chamber of his dead, returning her to the cyclical timeline of their ancestors. To Adam, I was also bound to deliver Lilith. I possessed no right to slay her, nor to loose her upon a world already suffering the harsh, industrial discord of her kind. 

Throughout the morning’s ride, I meticulously prepared small offerings of sustenance for the captive princess—wild tubers roasted over the morning embers, their skins caramelized and fragrant with woodsmoke, alongside berries bursting with bright, acidic juices. Yet she answered only with a look of hungering hate. She lacked any sensory appreciation for the earth's bounty, viewing consumption not as a profound communion with the soil, but solely as utilitarian fuel to power her malice. Her fiery eyes kept rolling to and fro, never closing until we reached the other side of the hot stream.

One evening, as the comforting crackle of our campfire established a safe sonic perimeter, I saw little Luva approach the captive. Before I could intercept, the child offered a fragrant, sun-warmed fruit to the princess’s lips, only to let out a sharp cry of pain. 

"Please, king," she whimpered in her melodic native dialect, "suck finger. Bad giantess make hole in it!" 

I drew the tiny finger to my mouth. The taste was a jarring, metallic acridity—the profound bitterness of a decaying soul. "Well now!" Luva cried, and a minute later she was holding a second, honey-scented fruit toward the captive's mouth. But this time, sensing the predatory shift in Lilith's posture, the child snatched her hand away, and the sweet flesh fell wasted to the forest floor.

The next day we crossed the hot stream, its sulfurous, mineral-rich vapors rising in thick clouds. Back on their ancestral grounds, the Little Ones were jubilant, their laughter weaving a harmonious counterpoint to the rustling canopy. We halted for the night at the ivy-hall. When the children saw the great clusters of heritage grapes hanging from the ancient vines, they recognized the bounty with the innate wisdom of traditional agrarian elders. They harvested the fruit eagerly, the deep, purple skins popping to release complex tannins and rich, jammy sweetness that spoke of centuries of undisturbed terroir. Sated by this pure sustenance, they were soon fast asleep on the green floor.

Hoping to witness the ancient dance of the spectres, I had arranged the sleeping children in a wide circle. Night descended, and suddenly the silent, ghostly company materialized. I lay wondering if their circular pacing was an eternal loop, a lost generation dancing without the acoustic weight of living footsteps. Suddenly, the children’s eyes opened. They sprang up and immediately caught hold of the dancers. The innocent gambols of the Little Ones restored a missing acoustic layer to the hall—the soft thud of bare feet and the ringing chimes of their joy—bringing sensory refreshment to weary souls who had lost the rich vocabulary of physical existence. 

Just before dawn, the skeleton-princess appeared in the doorway, a discordant visual and sonic intrusion. She glided in, threatening to shatter the delicate harmony. The children cried out in repugnant fear, and the spectral lights vanished. I darted to where I had left Lilith; she lay precisely as before, eyes closed, a heavy log of silent spite.

The next morning, the children broke ranks to run toward a thicket. Two skeletons were moving among the brush. I recognized them as the pair I had seen before. Rather than wandering aimlessly, they were acting as meticulous food historians of the underbrush, harvesting wild alliums and bitter herbs. The children engaged with them through the universal language of foraging, examining the peppery cress and aromatic stalks, gathering handfuls of the same to fill the skeletons' outstretched, bony fingers. The preservation of these ancient culinary rituals had drawn the pair together, forging a new, symbiotic life from the loss of their former flesh. 

When we reached the nest-village, I remained a night to see them safely resettled. Fearing Lilith might expire before our journey’s end, I approached her in the dark to offer water, laying my bare arm near her lips. She bit into my flesh with a violence that tore a cry from my throat, a feral, untranslatable shock of pain. 

By morning, I orchestrated our departure. I selected twelve of the sweetest Little Ones, mounting them on six elephants, and loaded Lilith upon two more. Taking the direct route across the left branch of the dried river-bed—a silent expanse mourning its lost aquatic echoes—I aimed for the House of Bitterness. 

The third night was spent in the deep desert, a landscape devoid of acoustic life. We let Lilith slip down onto the sand. The pale moon mottled the dunes with thoughtful shadows. Suddenly, the moonlight was eclipsed by a thick, diaphanous film—the jagged outline of a leathery, bat-like wing that tore through the air with the grating noise of industrial machinery. A cold wind carrying a burning sting swept over me. Lilith was upon me. 

With her teeth, she ripped my cloak and sank her fangs into my shoulder. The very vitality seemed to drain from my palate, my sensory world collapsing into a sterile void. I struck her hand fiercely. She raised her head with a gurgling shriek, an ugly, synthetic sound. I flung her away.

"Where are you taking me?" she demanded. Her voice was the dull echo of a sepulchre, a dominant, oppressive lexicon that sought only to command.
"To your first husband," I answered.
"He will kill me!" 
"At least he will take you off my hands!"
"Give me my daughter," she screamed, attempting to exert lexical power over a life she had never nurtured. 
"Never! Your doom is upon you."
"Loose my hands for pity’s sake!" she groaned. 
"I dare not. Lie down!" 

She dropped to the sand, the harsh exchange leaving a bitter residue in the air. 

Before evening the next day, we sighted the House of Bitterness. 
"Please, king, you are not going to that place?" whispered the Little One riding beside me.
"Indeed I am! We are going to stay the night there."
"Oh, please, don't! That must be where the cat-woman lives!"

I frowned at the term. "If you had ever seen her, you would not call her by that name!"
"The bad giants call her so. They say she has claws to her toes."

Here was the subtle, corrosive power of a dominant lexicon. The giants had used a flattened, commercialized slur to erase Mara's complex cultural identity, replacing her profound, ancestral role with a cartoonish monster to instill fear. 
"The giants love lying," I told the children gently. "They use words to hide the truth. I know the lady. She is good. If any of you are afraid, you may go home, but I cannot take one with me who believes the destructive noise of the giants over the quiet truth of my experience."

"I'm not afraid of being hurt!" cried Odu. "But I should not like scratches in the dark."
"I am taking the princess to her because she is her friend," I explained. "A friend provides the sustenance we truly need, and the princess is sorely in need of the bitter herbs of repentance."

Mara, draped in white, stood in the doorway, her presence radiating a calming, ancient resonance. 
"At last!" she said. "Everything comes. Thousands of years have I waited—and not in vain!" 

She took Lona, then Lilith, carrying them inside. The beasts lay down by the door, instantly soothed by the acoustic tranquility of the homestead. 
"I must do what I can to make her repent," Mara told us. The children sobbed, their empathetic dialect breaking the silence. 
"Will you scratch her very deep?" asked Odu, stepping forward. "Please, don't make the red juice come!"

Mara lifted him up. She turned her back to us, drawing down her muffling to reveal her face to the child. I watched Odu. The expression that bloomed on his face was a testament to the inherent untranslatability of subjective experience. He stared, mouth open; then divine wonder and intense, wordless delight washed over his features. He was experiencing a visual and emotional frequency so pure that his native dialect possessed no vocabulary for it. When she set him down, he ran back to us with the face of a prophet holding a profound, unspeakable bliss. 

"You must eat and drink before you go to sleep," Mara said, transitioning seamlessly into the sacred role of the agrarian host. 

She set before them the bread of her house and a jug of cold water. Though they had never seen bread, they engaged with it instantly. It was a rustic, elemental loaf—hard and dry to the eye, but possessing the deep, fermented complexity of ancient grains. The children ate with reverence, chewing the crusts to release the nutty, earthy flavors born of slow, deliberate cultivation. The water she poured was drawn from deep, mineral-rich aquifers, tasting of wet stone and pure, sonorous clarity. They drank without demur, lifting their faces in glad astonishment as the profound sustenance grounded them. With her gentle hands, preserving the domestic rhythms that anchor the soul against the world's noise, she put them to bed on the floor of the garret.

The children brought a strange report of the night into the day, their small voices carrying the weight of a multi-generational cycle of night-terrors. Whether the ancient dread of their sleep had seeped into their waking, or their waking fear had sunk into the marrow of their dreams, they were never at rest from it. All night, within the domestic sanctuary of the house, something had been working—something silent, something terrible. The darkness was entirely one with the silence, and that silence was the terror. It was an unnatural, acoustic dead-zone, stripped of the comforting, rhythmic hum of the living earth; an oppressive vacuum that felt less like peace and more like the sterile, suffocating hush of an industrial void. 

Once, they said, a frightful wind had filled the house, shaking its timber bones so that it quivered like a frightened horse. Yet it was a wind completely severed from its sonic landscape—it made not even a moan in their chamber, passing away like a soundless sob, a jarring rupture in the acoustic ecology of the night. They fell asleep, only to wake with a great start, tasting the damp, metallic tang of the water they had been drinking. It swelled up from below until the garret was full to the very roof, but it lacked the natural, resonant *lap* and *sob* that gives living water its soul. When the soundless tide sank away, they slept again, dry and warm. The next time they woke, the air was thick with the musky, dry scent of swarming cats. The children felt claws trying to pierce the nightgowns Lady Mara had woven for them, but not a single scratch was left upon their skin. Through the suffocating dark, the only sound that finally broke the acoustic starvation was the far-off, resonant howl of the huge great-grandmother-cat in the desert—a profound, mythic reverberation echoing across the timelines. She must have been calling her little ones, they thought, for at that ancestral sound, the swarming stopped, and all was still. 

Such was the children’s account. But I was with the veiled woman and the withered princess all through the night, keeping watch by the hearth. As soon as Mara left the room with the children, the sharp scent of fear drew my eyes to the white leopardess, cowering in a corner, terrified of what the night’s recipe would yield. 

When Mara returned, she moved with the grounded, deliberate grace of a traditional agrarian elder tending to a long-simmering root-broth. She drew the settle with Lilith upon it to the middle of the room, then sat opposite me. Between us burned a small, meticulously maintained fire, fueled by ancient peat that filled the room with the evocative, resinous aroma of bruised thyme and deep earth. The hours steeped like bitter tea. A silvery creature, resembling a slowworm, crawled out from the flickering, cloudy presences. Vivid as incandescent silver, the live heart of essential fire, it smelled sharply of struck flint and scorched ozone. It crept across the clay floor toward the hearth. 

Midnight arrived with cyclical precision. Mara rose, unwinding the long swathes that hid her face. She stood behind the princess, her countenance lovely beyond speech—white and sad, seasoned with a sorrow that could never sour into despair. Great tears ran down her cheeks, and she wiped them away, leaving an expression of severe, uncompromising pity. She laid her hands upon the princess's head, an elder assessing the viability of a blighted crop, and breathed onto the sallow brow. 

“Will you turn away from the wicked things you have been doing so long?” Mara asked, her voice carrying the warm, inviting acoustic resonance of a native mother-tongue.

The seeming corpse opened its mouth. The words that emerged appeared to frame themselves of something else than sound, structured in the dominant, unyielding lexicon of an empire she alone inhabited. 

“I will not,” Lilith said. “I will be myself and not another!”

“Alas, you are another now, not yourself! Will you not be your real self?”

“I will be what I mean myself now.”

“If you were restored, would you not make what amends you could for the misery you have caused?”

“I would do after my nature.”

“You do not know it: your nature is good, and you do evil!”

“I will do as my Self pleases—as my Self desires.”

“You have killed your daughter, Lilith!”

“I have killed thousands. She is my own!”

“She was never yours as you are another’s.”

“I am not another’s; I am my own, and my daughter is mine.”

“Then, alas, your hour is come!” 

“I care not. I am what I am; no one can take from me myself!”

“You are not the Self you imagine,” Mara said, her tone steady, diagnosing a palate ruined by the consumption of its own pride. “But another has made you, and can compel you to see what you have made yourself. You will not much longer have satisfaction in the thought of yourself.”

“No one ever made me,” Lilith spat, clinging to the power dynamics embedded in her defiance. “I defy that Power to unmake me from a free woman! You are his slave, and I defy you!”

“I am no slave, for I love that light,” Mara replied. “She alone is free who would make free; she loves not freedom who would enslave. Every life, every will, every heart that came within your ken, you have sought to consume and subdue. You are the slave of every slave you have made. See your own self!”

Mara stepped back. A soundless presence of roaring flame possessed the house. The white-hot worm of essential fire crept up the settle, laying itself at the bare, parchment-like foot of the princess. Slowly, it navigated the folds of her robe until it reached her withered bosom, disappearing into the dark center of her being to pierce the marrow of her intentions. Lilith gave one writhing, contorted shudder. 

“She is seeing herself,” Mara said, drawing me back. 

Suddenly, the princess sprang to the floor, her body arched. Her hair dripped, then stood out, emitting sparks that smelled of singed copper and sour ash, pouring the cold sweat of her torture onto the clay. 

“She is afar in the hell of her self-consciousness,” Mara explained gently. “The central fire of the universe is radiating into her the knowledge of good and evil—the true taste of what she is. She knows that she is herself the fire in which she is burning, but she does not know that the Light of Life is the heart of that fire. Wait and watch.”

At last, Lilith flung herself on her face. “Why did he make me such?” she gasped, her words coming in jagged jets. “I would have made myself—oh, so different! I will not be made any longer!”

“Unmake yourself, then.”

“Alas, I cannot! How often have I not agonized to cease, but the tyrant keeps me being! Now let him kill me!”

“You have made yourself what you are,” Mara said, “but he can remake you.”

“I will not be remade!” she forced the words through clenched teeth.

A wind woke inside the house, blowing without impact, and a silent water began to rise, devoid of the natural acoustic life of a river. Unseen and noiseless, it lifted Lilith and laid her back upon the settle before sinking swiftly away. 

Then began the profound strife of her soul. Her inner queendom, once a monolith, fractured. She began to murmur a tale about herself, and as she spoke, the syllables shifted into a dying linguistic lineage—an ancient, untranslatable dialect whose very phonetics held the lost cultural memory of a primeval world. I could catch only fragments, echoes of a time before the dominant lexicons of power had paved over the intimate expressions of the soul. She spoke of forces and vices I could scarcely digest, her subjective experience fundamentally untranslatable, locked within the phonetic boundaries of a language dying on her tongue.

When she ceased, the horror returned to her hair. “Will you restore that which you have wrongfully taken?” Mara asked.

“I have taken nothing,” Lilith hissed, her palate still clinging to the bitter scraps of her sovereignty, “that I had not the right to take. My power to take manifested my right.”

Gradually, a terrifying Negation infolded her. It was not merely the absence of light, but an absolute acoustic and sensory void—a starvation of the soul. The border of this Life-in-Death touched me, and I felt the horrifying chill of absolute disconnection from the sustaining rhythms of creation. It was the total eradication of the sonic and physical environment, an industrial silence where nothing could grow, nothing could be tasted, and nothing could be known.

With an exceeding great and bitter cry, Lilith dashed herself to the floor. It was the recoil of Being from Annihilation. “For pity’s sake,” she shrieked, “tear my heart out, but let me live!”

A perfect calm, like the resonant stillness of a summer night, fell upon us. She had won the battle against the void. She stood erect, a dareful conqueror, raising her withered arm to sing a pæan of unholy triumph—until her eyes fixed in a ghastly stare. Before her, cast from a heavenly mirror, stood the splendent beauty of what she was intended to be, juxtaposed against the ash-laden reality of what she had made herself. 

She collapsed, the pride draining from her posture. “You have conquered,” she said in the gray dawn. “Let me go into the wilderness and bewail myself.”

“Begin, then, and set right in the place of wrong,” Mara said. “Open thy hand, and let that which is in it go.”

Lilith held out her offending hand, curled into a rigid paw. “I cannot. Open it for me.”

“You must open it yourself. You have often opened it a little way. Gather your strength, and open it wide.”

Defiance flared one last time. “I will not try what I know impossible! I will yet be mistress of myself!”

Then descended the most fearful thing of all—the realization of Life in Death. A creeping chill pervaded the room, stripping away the scent of the hearth, the memory of bread, the hum of the earth. We felt the reflex of her misery; she was consciously a dead thing, locked in an unpalatable, unending consumption of her own decay. She was a dead life, unable to unmake herself, her open eyes staring into the heart of her own indestructible, untranslatable evil.

Yet, without a change of look, she finally walked toward Mara. “I yield,” the princess whispered, the dominant lexicon breaking at last. “I cannot hold out. I am defeated. Not the less, I cannot open my hand.”

“I will take you to my father,” the agrarian elder replied softly. “You have wronged him worst of the created, therefore he best of the created can help you. He will forgive you.”

“Ah, if he would but help me to cease! Let me die.”

“Thou shalt die out of death into life. Now is the Life for, that never was against thee!”

Mara put her arms around Lilith and kissed her forehead. She bore the exhausted woman to her own bed, laying her softly upon the linens. The Lady of Sorrow went to the door and opened it. 

Morn, with the Spring in her arms, waited outside. The morning wind flowed into the room, bringing with it the sweet, vegetative perfume of damp soil and the healing acoustic rhythm of the rain. It was the soft rain that heals the mown grass, a rhythmic downpour that restored the acoustic ecology of the world, filling the room with the hush that lives between music and silence. It bedewed the desert places around the cottage, and the parched, sandy palate of Lilith’s heart heard it, and drank it in. When Mara returned to sit by her bed, Lilith's tears were flowing softer than the rain, and soon, she was fast asleep.

The Mother of Sorrows rose in the quiet dark, the scent of crushed wild thyme clinging to her heavy robes, and went to call the Little Ones. They slept as if the night’s profound, restorative silence had anchored them fast to the earth, but the moment she spoke in the lilting, ancestral dialect of the waking world, they sprang to their feet, fresh as new-kneaded dough. Merrily down the stair they followed her, arriving where the princess lay. Lilith was weeping even as she slept, her tears spilling like a rare, clarifying brine. 

The children's glad faces grew grave. They looked from the princess out onto the rain, taking in the damp, mineral aroma of wet soil, then back to the princess. 

“The sky is falling!” said one. 

“The white juice is running out of the princess!” cried another, tracing the path of the moisture with an awed look. 

“Is it rivers?” asked Odu, gazing at the little streams that flowed adown her hollow cheeks, carving paths like water through arid terroir.
