“Yes,” answered Mara, her voice a soothing hum that harmonized perfectly with the patter of the rain, “—the most wonderful of all rivers.” 

“I thought rivers was bigger, and rushed, like a lot of Little Ones, making loud noises!” he returned, looking at me, from whom alone he had heard of rivers. 

“Look at the rivers of the sky!” said Mara. “See how they come down to wake up the dormant seeds under the earth. Soon will the rivers be flowing everywhere, merry and loud, a symphony of living water rather than the dead, grating noise of the wastes. Oh, how glad they will make you, Little Ones! You have never seen any, and do not know how sweet the water tastes, how it smells of flint and deep roots!” 

“That will be the glad of the ground that the princess is grown good,” said Odu. “See the glad of the sky!” 

“Are the rivers the glad of the princess?” asked Luva. “They are not her juice, for they are not red!” 

“They are the essence within the essence, the purest stock of her soul,” answered Mara. 

Odu put one finger to his eye, tasted the salt upon it, and shook his head. 

“Princess will not bite now!” said Luva. 

“No; she will never do that again,” replied Mara. “But now we must take her nearer home, where the harvest is gathered.” 

“Is that a nest?” asked Sozo. 

“Yes; a very big nest, a cellar of preservation. But we must take her to another place first.” 

“What is that?” 

“It is the biggest room in all this world. But I think it is going to be pulled down: it will soon be too full of little nests.—Go and get your clumsies.” 

“Please, are there any cats in it?” 

“Not one. The nests are too full of quiet, resonant dreams for one cat to get in.” 

“We shall be ready in a minute,” said Odu, and ran out, followed by all except Luva. 

Lilith was now awake, listening with a sad smile. 

“But her rivers are running so fast!” said Luva, who stood by her side. “Her robe is all—I don’t know what. Clumsies won’t like it!” 

“They won’t mind it,” answered Mara. “Those rivers are so clean that they scour the palate of the whole world.” 

I had fallen asleep by the hearth, inhaling the residual smoke of aged oak and dried herbs, but for some time had been awake, and now rose. 

“It is time to mount, Mr. Vane,” said our hostess. 

“Tell me, please,” I said, “is there not a way by which to avoid the channels and the den of monsters? Their dissonance is a violence to the ears.” 

“There is an easy way across the river-bed, which I will show you,” she answered; “but you must pass once more through the monsters.” 

“I fear for the children,” I said. 

“Fear will not once come nigh them,” she rejoined. 

We left the cottage. The beasts stood waiting about the door, exhaling clouds of warm, hay-scented breath into the cool morning. Odu was already on the neck of one of the two elephants that were to carry the princess. I mounted Lona’s horse; Mara brought her body, heavy and cold as unbaked clay, and gave it me in my arms. When she came out again with the princess, a cry of delight arose from the children: she was no longer muffled! Gazing at her, entranced with a beauty that seemed plucked from some forgotten epoch, the boys forgot to receive the princess; but the elephants took Lilith tenderly with their trunks, one round her body and one round her knees, and laid her along between them. 

“Why does the princess want to go?” asked a small boy. “She would keep good if she staid here!” 

“She wants to go, and she does not want to go: we are helping her,” answered Mara. “She will not keep good here. Her season turns.” 

“What are you helping her to do?” he went on. 

“To go where she will get more help—help to open her hand, which has been closed upon a dead language for a thousand years.” 

“So long? Then she has learned to do without it: why should she open it now?” 

“Because it is shut upon a lexicon of power that is not hers, a bitter root she must expel.” 

“Please, lady Mara, may we have some of your very dry bread before we go?” said Luva. 

Mara smiled, and brought them four round loaves of ancient grain, the crusts cracked and smelling of wild yeast and toasted barley, alongside a great earthenware jug of water. 

“We will eat as we go,” they said. But they drank the water with delight, savoring its complex minerality. 

“I think,” remarked one of them, wiping his chin, “it must be elephant-juice! It tastes of the deep earth; it makes me so strong!” 

We set out, the Lady of Sorrow walking with us, more beautiful than the sun, and the white leopardess following her. The wind blew its sweetest, carrying the fragrance of distant spice-flowers, but it did not comfort the desert, for the soil lacked water to translate the scent into life. We crossed the channels without difficulty, the children gamboling about Mara all the way, but did not reach the top of the ridge over the bad burrow until the sun was already in the act of disappearing. Then I made the Little Ones mount their elephants, for the moon might be late, and I felt the air pressure drop, signaling an oppressive shift in the acoustic atmosphere. 

The Lady of Sorrow led the way; the elephants followed in the centre; the leopardess brought up the rear. Just as we reached the frightful margin, the moon looked up, illuminating a shallow basin. Mara stepped into it; not a sound answered her tread. The earth here absorbed frequency, deadening the footfalls. But the moment the elephants carrying the princess touched it, the seemingly solid earth began to heave and boil. 

The silence was shattered by a horrific, industrial cacophony—a violent noise pollution of grinding gears, mechanized shrieks, and fleshy tearing. Monsters uprose on all sides. Long-billed heads, horribly jawed faces, and knotty tentacles went out after Lilith. She lay in an agony of fear, her ears assaulted by their dominant, aggressive screams, unable to stir a finger. Whether the hideous things even saw the children, I doubt; they were blind to the gentle frequencies of innocence. Not one loathly member passed the live rampart of her body-guard. 

“Little Ones,” I cried, “keep your elephants close. Be brave; they will not touch you.” 

“What will not touch us? We don’t know what to be brave at!” they answered. 

I perceived they were entirely deaf to the superficial, commercialized roar of the deformities around them. They were panoplied in their acoustic purity! Incapacity to perceive the dissonance was their safety. What could not register upon their untainted senses could not hurt them. 

Mara was a few paces in front of me when a solitary, bodiless head bounced on the path, snapping its jaws with the rhythmic clatter of a stamping press. The leopardess came rushing from behind and would have seized it, but with a loathsome howl of untranslatable static, it gave a rapid rotatory twist and buried itself in the ground. 

Mara still went in front, the leopardess shivering close behind her, when a low wave of earth slunk toward us. Out of it slouched a dreadful head with fleshy tubes for hair, emitting a sickening odor of rancid oil and synthetic decay, snapping at me. The leopardess sprang but fell baffled. Almost under our feet shot up the head of an enormous snake, its eyes lamping with the glare of a factory furnace. Again the leopardess rushed, and again found nothing but empty, echoing noise. 

The moon was carrying herself strangely, sinking rapidly, abandoning the sky to the dissonant dark. We were almost over when a long neck arose, topped with the head of a corpse whose mouth was full of canine teeth, hissing a corrupted, dying dialect. The lady stepped on firm land, but the leopardess flew at the throat of the terror. The moon went down. For an instant, I saw the leopardess and the snake-monster convolved in a cloud of dust; then darkness hid them. Trembling with fright, my horse wheeled and overtook the elephants. 

As we came up with them, a shapeless jelly dropped on the princess, stinking of caustic rot. A white dove dropped immediately on the jelly, stabbing it with its beak. It made a squelching, sucking sound—a sudden acoustic vacuum—and fell off. 

Then I heard the voice of a woman talking with Mara, its timbre rich and resonant, like a bronze bell in a quiet courtyard. 

“I fear she is dead!” said Mara. 

“I will send and find her,” answered the mother. “But why, Mara, shouldst thou at all fear? Death cannot hurt her who dies doing the work given her to do.” 

“I shall miss her sorely; she is good and wise.” 

“She has gone down with the wicked; she will rise with the righteous. We shall see her again ere very long.” 

“Mother,” I said into the dark, “we come to you many, but most of us are Little Ones. Will you be able to receive us all?” 

“You are welcome every one,” she answered. “Sooner or later all will be little ones, for all must sleep in my house! It is well with those that go to sleep young and willing! My husband is even now preparing her couch for Lilith. She is neither young nor quite willing, but it is well indeed that she is come.” 

We walked stumbling over the moor until we saw a light in the distance. Adam stood in the door, holding a candle, talking with his wife, who laid bread and wine on a sturdy oaken table. The scents drifting from the hearth were an ancient balm: roasted root vegetables, the complex, fermented aroma of dark, seeded rye, and wine that smelled of crushed cherries and basalt. 

“Happy children,” I heard Eve say, “to have looked already on the face of my daughter!” 

When we reached the door, Adam welcomed us. He would have taken the princess to carry her in, but she repulsed him. She stood between the elephants, unsubmissive, holding tightly to her ruined pride. 

“We have long waited for thee, Lilith!” he said. She returned him no answer, her lips locked around her untranslatable bitterness. 

Eve came to the door, radiant in her beauty, the ultimate hearth-mother. “The mortal foe of my children!” she murmured. 

“Your children are no longer in her danger,” said Mara. 

“Trust her not hastily, Mara,” answered her mother. 

“But you will open to her the mirror of the Law of Liberty, mother, that she may go into it! She consents to open her hand and restore: will not the great Father restore her?” 

“I do not know Him!” murmured Lilith, in a voice stripped of all resonance. 

“Therefore it is that thou art miserable,” said Adam, speaking the native dialect of the earth, steady and cyclic. 

“I will go back whence I came!” she cried. 

“That is indeed what I would have thee do—to Him from whom thou camest! Death is even now on his way to lead thee to Him. Thou knowest neither Death nor the Life that dwells in Death! Both befriend thee. I am dead, and would see thee dead, for I live and love thee. Wilt thou not be restored and BE?” 

She stood silent. 

“Father,” said Mara, “take her in thine arms, and carry her to her couch. There she will open her hand, and die into life.” 

“I will walk,” said the princess. 

Adam led the way. The princess walked feebly after him. Eve reached up her arms, took Lona from my bosom, and carried her in. I dismounted, and the children also. The horse and the elephants stood shivering; Mara stroked them, and they lay down to sleep. 

Inside the cottage, Eve offered the Little Ones the dense bread and the rich wine. Adam and Lilith stood silent. Eve came from the chamber of death and offered the bread and wine to the princess, holding out the sustenance of centuries, a meal crafted to heal the deepest starvation of the soul. 

“Thy beauty slays me! It is death I would have, not food!” said Lilith, turning her face away, her palate too corrupted by power to bear the taste of genuine communion. 

“This food will help thee to die,” answered Eve. But Lilith would not taste of it. 

“If thou wilt nor eat nor drink, Lilith,” said Adam, “come and see the place where thou shalt lie in peace.” 

He led the way through the door of death. When her foot crossed the threshold, a wild blast fell roaring on the roof, dying away in a moan. She stood ghastly with terror. 

“It is he!” said her voiceless lips. 

“Who, princess?” I whispered. 

“The great Shadow,” she murmured. 

“Here he cannot enter,” said Adam, his voice carrying the acoustic authority of the stone walls. “Here he can hurt no one. Over him also is power given me.” 

“Are the children in the house?” asked Lilith. 

“He never dared touch a child,” Eve said. “And now Death shall be the atonemaker; you shall sleep together.” 

As soon as Adam went back to fetch the children, the princess knelt to Eve. “Beautiful Eve, persuade your husband to kill me! Indeed I would but cannot open my hand.” 

“You cannot die without opening it,” answered Eve. “To kill you would not serve you. No one can kill you but the Shadow.” 

“Show me then to my grave; I am so weary I can live no longer. I must go to the Shadow—yet I would not!” 

She struggled to rise, but fell. The Mother lifted and carried her inward. We followed into the vast chamber of death. It was not a crypt, but a magnificent root-cellar of the soul, a curing chamber where the dead were laid down to ferment and age into their true forms. The air was cool, still, and fragrant with dried lavender, curing salt, and sweet earth. It was a sanctuary of perfect acoustic ecology; the profoundest sleep filled the wide place, a restorative hush that healed the auditory wounds of the surface world. 

The dim light in Adam’s hand glimmered on the sleeping faces. The darkness, the cold, the silence, and the still air made the hearts of the children beat softly, and their little tongues spoke in low, hushed voices, instinctively respecting the sanctity of the resting space. 

“What a curious place to sleep in!” said one. “I would rather be in my nest!” 

“It is SO cold!” said another. 

“Yes, it is cold,” answered our host; “but you will not be cold in your sleep.” 

“Where are our nests?” asked more than one, looking round the vast culinary archive of humanity. 

“Find places, and sleep where you choose,” replied Adam. 

Instantly they scattered, their gentle voices blending into the domestic rhythm of the chamber. One little fellow crept under the sheet beside a beautiful lady. Another lay down beside a woman, pressing close to warm her. Luva stood on tiptoe by a couch. 

“My own mother wouldn’t have me,” she said softly. Eve lifted her, and she got under the snowy covering. The orphans adopted mothers, seamlessly integrating into the agrarian cycles of rest. 

On the middle of three unoccupied couches lay Lona. Eve set Lilith down beside it. Adam pointed to the vacant couch on Lona’s right. “There, Lilith, is the bed I have prepared for you!” 

She glanced at her daughter, who lay like a statue carved in semi-transparent alabaster, preserved in perfect stillness, and shuddered. “How cold it is!” 

“You will soon begin to find comfort in the cold,” answered Adam. 

“I fear that child,” she said. “She will rise and terrify me!” 

“She is dreaming love to you.” 

“But the Shadow! He will be wroth with me!” 

“He at sight of whom the horses of heaven start and rear, dares not disturb one dream in this quiet chamber!” 

The princess lay down, drew the sheet over her, and lay still with open eyes. Mara drew near. 

“Lilith,” said Mara, “you will not sleep until you have opened your hand, and yielded that which is not yours to give or to withhold.” 

“I cannot,” she answered. 

“The shadows of death will gather, but they cannot infold you while yet your hand remains unopened. Open your hand, and make an end.” 

A tinge of colour arose in the parchment-like face; the contorted hand trembled with agonised effort. It was clenched tightly around the ghost of a dominant lexicon, a physical manifestation of oppressive power she could not translate into surrender. Mara sought to aid her. 

“Hold, Mara!” cried her father. “There is danger!” 

The princess turned her eyes upon Eve. “There was a sword I once saw in your husband’s hands. I heard him say it would divide whatever was not one and indivisible!” 

“I have the sword,” said Adam. 

“Bring it, Adam,” pleaded Lilith, “and cut me off this hand that I may sleep.” 

He gave the candle to Eve, and went. When he returned, he held an ancient weapon. The scabbard looked like vellum grown dark with years, like a centuries-old ledger of lost languages, but the hilt shone like untarnished gold. He drew out the blade. It flashed like a clean butcher’s knife, precise and unyielding, and its light made the princess open her eyes. She held out her hand. 

Adam took it. The sword gleamed once, performing its swift culinary severance—there was one little gush of blood, rich as the wine she had refused, and he laid the severed hand in Mara’s lap. Lilith had given one moan, and was already fast asleep, her features relaxing into their native dialect of peace. 

Mara covered the arm with the sheet. 

“Will you not dress the wound?” I said. 

“A wound from that sword,” answered Adam, “needs no dressing. It is healing and not hurt. It prunes the dead branch to save the root.” 

“Where the dead deformity clung,” replied Mara, “the true, lovely hand is already growing.” 

We heard a childish voice behind us. The candle shone on the sleeping face of Lilith, and three Little Ones grouped on the other side of her couch. 

“How beautiful she is grown!” said one. 

“Poor princess!” said another; “I will sleep with her. She will not bite any more!” He climbed into her bed and was immediately fast asleep. 

“I will go on her other side,” said the third. “She shall have two to kiss her when she wakes!” 

“And I am left alone!” said the first mournfully. 

“I will put you to bed,” said Eve, leading the child away. 

We turned to go back to the cottage. Mara by my side carried the severed hand of Lilith in the lap of her robe, bearing away the bitter ingredient of her pride. 

“Ah, you have found her!” we heard Eve say as we stepped into the cottage. The door stood open; two elephant-trunks came through it out of the night. “I sent them with the lantern to look for Mara’s leopardess: they have brought her.” 

I followed Adam, and between us we took the white creature from the elephants. We carried her to the perfectly tuned chamber we had just left, the women preceding us. There we laid the beauty across the feet of the princess, her fore-paws outstretched, and her head couching between them in the resonant, restorative silence of the earth.

I turned and spoke to Eve, drawing in the comforting, yeast-rich aroma of the ancient hearth that clung to her garments. “Mother, one couch next to Lona is empty. I know I am unworthy, but may I not sleep this night in your chamber with my dead? Will you not pardon both my cowardice and my self-confidence, and take me in? I yield myself up. I am sick of my own being, and hunger for the great sleep, the long wintering of the soul.”

“The couch next to Lona is the one already prepared for you,” she answered, her voice possessing the deep, resonant timbre of a matriarch who has tended the generations like a slow-fermenting mother dough. “But something waits to be done ere you sleep.”

“I am ready,” I replied.

“How do you know you can do it?” she asked, a gentle smile lifting the corners of her mouth.

“Because you require it,” I answered. “What is it?”

She turned to Adam. “Is he forgiven, husband?”

“From the very root of my heart,” he said.

“Then tell him what he has to do.”

Adam, the primordial agrarian elder, turned to his daughter. “Give me that hand, Mara, my child.” She held it out to him in her lap. He took it with the practiced, meticulous reverence of a food historian handling a fragile, heirloom seed-pod, destined to revive a forgotten crop.

“Let us go to the cottage,” he said to me. “There I will instruct you.”

As we stepped outside, a sudden stormful blast arose, mingling with a great, disruptive flapping on the roof—an auditory violence that shattered the serene acoustic ecology of the homestead—but it soon died away into a deep, earthy moan. When the heavy timber door of the death-chamber was closed behind us, sealing in the scent of dried herbs and quiet time, Adam seated himself. I stood before him.

He spoke in the rhythmic, circular cadence of his native agrarian dialect—a tongue intimately tied to the cycles of harvest and dormancy, whose profound nuances defied translation into the flattened, utilitarian lexicons of the modern world. 

“You will remember,” he began, his tone a quiet hum that harmonized with the creaking floorboards, “how, after leaving my daughter’s house, you came to a dry rock, bearing the scoured marks of an ancient cataract. You climbed that rock, and found a sandy desert. Go to that rock now, and from its summit walk deep into the desert. But go not many steps ere you lie down, and listen with your head against the sand. The sonic environment will guide you. If you hear the murmur of water beneath, go a little farther, and listen again. If you still hear the sound, you are in the right direction. Every few yards you must stop, lie down, and hearken. If, listening thus, at any time you hear no sound of water, you are out of the way, and must hearken in every direction until the resonance returns. Keeping with the sound, and careful not to retrace your steps in this delicate choreography, you will soon hear it louder. The growing acoustic swell will lead you to where it is loudest: that is the spot you seek. There, dig with the spade I will give you, and dig until you come to moisture. In it, lay the hand, cover it to the level of the desert, and come home.”

He paused, letting the weight of the generational ritual settle between us. “But give good heed, and carry the hand with care. Never lay it down, in what place of seeming safety soever; let nothing touch it; stop nor turn aside for any attempt to bar your way; never look behind you; speak to no one, answer no one, walk straight on. Keep your silence pure. It is yet dark, and the morning is far distant, but you must set out at once.”

He handed me the severed charge and brought me a heavy spade. “This is my gardening spade,” he said, his hands tracing the ash-wood handle, polished by centuries of labor. “With it, I have brought many a lovely thing to the sun, turning the dark soil into sustenance.”

I took the sexton-tool and went out into the night. It was bitterly cold and pitch-dark, the air smelling sharply of crushed flint and dormant frost. To fall would be a dread thing, and the way I had to go was treacherous even in broad sunlight. But I had not set myself the task, and the minute I started, I learned I was left to no arbitrary chance. A pale, mycelial light broke from the ground at every step, glowing like phosphorus in rich loam, showing me exactly where to set my foot. Through the heather and the low rocks I walked without once stumbling. I found the bad burrow entirely still; not a wave arose, not a head appeared to disrupt the quiet as I crossed it. A moon came, bathing the landscape in a milky wash, showing me the easy way. Toward morning I was almost over the dry channels of the first branch of the river-bed, and not far from Mara’s cottage.

The moon was very low, the sun not yet up, when I saw before me in the narrowed, rocky path a figure covered from head to foot as with a veil of moonlit mist. I kept my rhythm, stepping as if I saw nothing.

The figure threw aside its veil. “Have you forgotten me already?” said the princess—or what seemed she.

I neither hesitated nor answered; I walked straight on, preserving the sacred silence.

“You meant then to leave me in that horrible sepulchre! Do you not yet understand that where I please to be, there I am? Take my hand: I am alive as you!”

I was on the point of saying, *Give me your left hand*, but I bethought myself, clamping my teeth over my tongue. To speak would be to surrender to her dominant lexicon, to engage in a shallow commerce of words. I steadily advanced.

“Give me my hand,” she suddenly shrieked, her voice a grating, industrial dissonance that violently polluted the quiet night, “or I will tear you in pieces: you are mine!”

She flung herself upon me. I shuddered, but my stride did not falter. Nothing touched me, and I saw her no more.

With measured tread along the path, filling it for some distance, came a body of armed men. The metallic clashing of their armor was a deafening, mechanized roar—the hollow acoustic signature of violence and commercialized force. I walked straight through them. I know not whether they gave way, or if they were bodiless things. They turned and followed me; I felt the percussive thud of their march at my very heels, but I cast no look behind, and the artificial cacophony of their steps eventually dissolved back into the organic silence of the earth.

A little farther on, where the path narrowed in deep shadow, I descried a woman with a muffled face, seated blocking my way. 

“Ah,” she said, her voice smooth but lacking the rich, untranslatable resonance of true empathy, “you are come at last! I have waited here for you an hour or more! You have done well! Your trial is over. My father sent me to meet you that you might have a little rest on the way. Give me your charge, and lay your head in my lap; I will take good care of both until the sun is well risen. I am not bitterness always, neither to all men!”

Her words were terrible with temptation, for I was deeply weary. How easy it would be to accept this utilitarian comfort, this translated counterfeit of peace! If I were, through slavish obedience to the mere letter of the command, to trample the very person of the Lady of Sorrow, it would be a tragic failure of insight. My heart grew faint, then beat violently. Nevertheless, my will hardened. I recognized the subtle power dynamics in her flattened speech; it lacked the terroir of Mara's genuine, suffering dialect. I kept my tongue locked between my teeth and marched on. If Adam had sent her, he could not complain that I would not heed her. 

Just ere I reached the phantom, she pulled the covering from her face. Great indeed was her loveliness, but those were not Mara’s eyes. No superficial lie could imitate the deep, oceanic sorrow within them. I advanced as if the thing were not there, and my foot found empty room.

I had almost reached the other side when a Shadow barred my way. He seemed to wear a helmet, but as I drew closer, I perceived it was the head itself—distorted, bloated, bearing only a sickening resemblance to the human form. A cold wind smote me, reeking of chemical decay and the sterile, joyless atmosphere of a charnel-house. It was the absolute void of sustenance. Firmness forsook my joints, and my limbs trembled. I seemed to pass through him, but I think now he passed through me: for a moment, I was deafened, hollowed out, reduced to the state of the damned who know no sensory joy. 

Then, a soft wind greeted me—a breath of new-born spring carrying the olfactory salvation of wild mint, wet bark, and living soil—and before me arose the dawn.

My way led me past the door of Mara’s cottage. It stood wide open. Upon the rough-hewn table, I saw a loaf of bread and a pitcher of water. Even from afar, the sight was a profound testament to the culinary grace of everyday domestic routine. The loaf was a rustic boule, its dark, blistered crust undoubtedly holding a tender, fermented crumb, while the earthen pitcher sweated with the chill of absolute purity. No howl or wail disturbed the acoustic sanctuary around the cottage.

I came to the precipice that testified to the vanished river, tracing the ancient, water-carved indentations in the stone. I climbed its worn face and went on into the desert. 

There, I began the meticulous work of the listener. I lay upon the sand, pressing my ear to the earth, tuning my senses to the subterranean frequencies. I walked, stopped, and listened to the sand, tracing the buried murmur of water—the forgotten native tongue of the world, flowing beneath the arid silence. I tracked its subtle volume, letting the untranslatable dialect of the deep current guide my steps, until I determined the exact spot where the hidden water sang loudest.

I hung the severed hand about my neck and began to dig. It was a long labor, the loose sand requiring a wide, sloping excavation, but I worked the spade with the rhythmic, deliberate technique of a master baker folding heavy dough. At length, the sensory landscape shifted; the dry, dusty air gave way to the rich, mineral scent of damp earth. I threw up a heavy, dark spadeful of loam. 

I flung the sexton-tool on the verge and laid the pale hand down into the cool, dark bottom. Already, a little water—clear and sweet—was oozing from under its fingers, a mythic ingredient reactivating the dormant earth. I sprang out and made haste to fill the grave, sealing the moisture within the desert's crust. Then, utterly fatigued by the great, cyclical work of planting and preservation, I dropped beside the mounded sand, and fell into a deep, dreamless sleep.

When I woke, the scent of saturated, ancient loam filled my lungs. The ground beneath me was weeping moisture, its firm soil dissolving into a slick, fragrant quicksand as the river swelled in its ancient course. The rising waters carried the deep, percussive rhythm of untamed nature—a resonant acoustic cleansing preparing to shove its heavy burden over the precipice. Soon it would roar down in a bifurcated cascade: one branch to resubmerge the orchard valley, the other to drown the monster horde, whose jagged, discordant shrieks had long polluted the valley’s soundscape like the grinding gears of some mechanical blight. Between these surging currents, the Evil Wood would be left an island. 

I set out at once on my return to the agrarian elders who had sent me. Reaching the precipice, I threaded my way between the channels, drawn back toward Mara’s cottage. Even if the river overtook me, I knew the contours of the land, its interwoven timelines etched into my very stride. The door of the cottage stood open to the damp air. Upon the heavy wooden table rested the elements of deep sustenance: a coarse, thick-crusted loaf of bread and an earthen pitcher of water. I stood on the threshold, absorbing the profound, healing silence that wrapped the domestic space, calling aloud, though no voice broke the acoustic sanctity. 

A little farther along the sand, I came upon a gray-headed man weeping. His sorrow possessed a thin, grating quality, devoid of the rich resonance of true grief. 

“What ails you, sir?” I asked. “Are you forsaken?”

“I weep,” he answered, his lexicon hollowed out by a lifetime of sterile, utilitarian pursuits, “because they will not let me die. I have been to the house of death, and its mistress, notwithstanding my years, refuses me. Intercede for me, sir, if you know her, I pray you.”

“Nay, sir,” I replied. “That I cannot; for she refuses none whom it is lawful for her to receive.”

“How know you this of her? You have never sought death! You are much too young to desire it!”

“I fear your words indicate that, were you young again, neither would you desire it.”

“Indeed, young sir, I would not! And certain I am that you cannot.”

“I may not be old enough to desire to die, but I am young enough to desire to live indeed! Therefore I go now to learn if she will at length take me in. You wish to die because you do not care to live, seeking only a cessation of noise rather than true rest. She will not open her door to you, for no one can die who does not long to live.”

“It ill becomes your youth to mock a friendless old man. Pray, cease your riddles!”

“Did not the Mother tell you something of the same sort in her own native dialect?”

“In truth I believe she did; but I gave little heed to her excuses.”

“Ah, then, sir,” I rejoined, mourning the erosion of his cultural memory, the way the dominant language of despair had colonized his mind. “It is but too plain you have not yet learned to die. Such had I too been but for the Lady of Sorrow. Go to her cottage yonder. Sit at her table. Do not view the meal as mere fuel; eat of her wild-fermented bread, taste the complex, bitter crumb of her wisdom. Drink the water drawn from deep, mineral-rich aquifers. Wait there until she appears, and ask her counsel.”

He only fell to weeping afresh, deaf to the ancient tongue of sustenance. I left him, knowing Mara’s meticulous care would eventually find him.

The sun had sunk, leaving a crisp, botanical cold in its wake as I skirted the domain of the monsters. Their discordant cacophony was mercifully silenced by the roaring surge of the approaching floodwaters behind me—a magnificent symphony of acoustic ecology returning balance to the world. Before I reached the house of death, a dense, bitter frost permeated my bones. Yet the candle of Eve, smelling faintly of pure, aged beeswax and blooming heather, shone from the window, a beacon of domestic warmth against the murk. 

The door stood open; the cottage lay empty. I sat down, overcome by an unprecedented desolation. Thousands of ancestors lay sleeping within these walls, yet I was entirely without their communion. The isolation was a profound sensory deprivation. I craved the shared table, the interwoven timelines of the generations gathered in quiet harvest. In the oppressive stillness, I dropped father Adam’s worn iron spade. Its dull thud against the packed clay floor reverberated through the subterranean chambers like an ancient dinner gong calling to the hollow earth. 

Then, a subtle vibration stirred the air—a noiseless thrill felt deep within the marrow, an acoustic footprint of the dead. The heavy coffin-door groaned softly, releasing the scent of dormant seeds and dry cedar. It opened, and Lona’s face appeared. Her eyes were closed, her skin pale as winter-milled flour, but she seemed to sense me through some untranslatable instinct. Her voice was the sleepy night-wind rustling through dry prairie grass, an older dialect slipping past the boundaries of waking speech.

“Are you coming, king?” she whispered. “I cannot rest until you are with me, gliding down the river to the great sea, and the beautiful dream-land. The sleepiness is full of lovely things: come and see them.”

“Ah, my darling!” I cried, pulling her against my chest. She was cold as marble chilled in a root cellar. 

She threw her white arms feebly about me, sighing with the scent of frozen dew. “Carry me back to my bed, king. I want to sleep.”

I bore her carefully into the death-chamber, terrified she might dissolve like spun sugar in my grasp. Unaware of how my own vision guided me in the gloom, I carried her straight to her resting place. 

“Lay me down,” she murmured, “and cover me from the warm air; it hurts—a little. Your bed is there, next to mine. I shall see you when I wake.”

She sank instantly into the deep, circular rhythm of slumber. I threw myself onto my own couch, anticipating the mythic freeze of the chamber. “Come, sweet cold,” I breathed, “and still my heart speedily.”

Instead, the rich, earthy scent of a hearth-fire announced the approach of Adam. Though he carried no candle, I could see his weathered, agrarian features. He looked down at Lona with a cultivator’s gentle smile, then turned to me. 

“We have been to the top of the hill to hear the waters on their way,” he said, appreciating the restorative acoustic sweep of the flood. “They will be in the den of the monsters to-night. But why did you not await our return?”

“My child could not sleep,” I answered.

“She is fast asleep!” he rejoined.

“Yes, now! But she was awake when I laid her down.”

“She was asleep all the time,” he insisted. “She was perhaps dreaming about you—and came to you.”

When I confessed I had not realized she was asleep until he pointed it out, Adam merely smiled at the untranslatable mysteries of the sleeping mind. I tried to rise, apologizing for taking my place before the proper rites were observed, but the gravity of generations held me fast.

“There is no need: we will serve you here,” he answered. Bending over me, the elder breathed a gust of warm, sun-baked air directly onto my heart, thawing the frost in my veins. 

From the shadows, the Mother’s voice arose. Eve sang softly at first, then her melody soared aloft—a sound of supreme acoustic purity. Her song was entirely devoid of the harsh, transactional lexicons of the surface world; it was a native dialect of creation, carrying the olfactory memory of the first blooming orchards. The words mourned the erosion of the world but promised the restoration of the harvest:

*Many a wrong, and its curing song;*
*Many a road, and many an inn;*
*Room to roam, but only one home*
*For all the world to win!*

As the resonance faded, the three ancient caretakers—Adam, Eve, and Mara—approached my couch. They were no mere ghosts, but passionate purveyors of life’s deepest sustenance. They brought me bread and wine. This was no utilitarian fuel. I sat up to partake, recognizing the gravity of their craft. The bread’s crust was scored with the maps of forgotten epochs; its dense crumb tasted of malted barley, roasted nuts, and the sweet, loamy decay of autumn. The wine was a dark, complex vintage pressed from vines whose roots tapped into the world’s oldest aquifers, heavy with dark-berried tannins and the damp oak of time itself. 

“You are good indeed, father Adam, mother Eve, sister Mara,” I said, savoring the profound history on my tongue. “In my soul I am ashamed and sorry!”

“We knew you would come again,” Eve answered gently. 

“How could you know it?” 

“Because here was I, born to look after my brothers and sisters!” Mara smiled, the meticulous preserver of our shared, domestic history. 

“Every creature must one night yield himself and lie down,” Adam added, his voice carrying the rhythmic certainty of the seasons. “He was made for liberty, and must not be left a slave to the waking world’s hollow noise.”

When I worried that the others were arriving too late, Adam dismissed the linear constraints of time. Here, pacing was circular; true time only began when one submitted to the soil. 

“Your father will wake when his morning comes,” Adam told me. “Your mother, next to whom you are lying—”

“Ah, then, it is my mother!” I exclaimed, looking at the sleeping woman with the scarred, wounded hand—the mark of a life spent breaking bread and tending the hearth fires of a fallen world. 

“She will be up and away long ere your morning is ripe,” he said. He let the soft candlelight illuminate her features. 

“She looks much younger!” I marveled.

“She is much younger,” he replied, explaining the great, multi-generational backward cycle of the house. Even Lilith was regenerating, shedding the hardened, dominant lexicon of her pride to reclaim her native, youthful perfection. The timelines of our family were intricately interwoven here in the quiet dark.

I lay back down, blissfully drowsy, as the complex finish of the ancient wine warmed my blood. Adam promised that when my mother woke, she would possess a splendor beyond foresight, rising alongside her husband and son to join a grander cosmic table. 

Wrapped in the white garment of the dead—which smelled faintly of crushed lavender and sun-bleached linen—I surrendered to the profound acoustic stillness. The night around me was a pale, sleeping orchard of faces, their histories safely preserved in the silence. And soon, I too slipped into the great, nourishing sleep, woven seamlessly into the eternal domesticity of the earth.

I grew aware of existence, aware also of the profound, the infinite cold. It was a blessed chill, reminiscent of the slow, methodical cold-fermentation where wild yeasts develop their most complex, mythic flavors. I lay at peace, breathing the damp, terroir-rich odors of Earth’s bountiful bosom, my palate anticipating the souls of primroses, the peppery bite of daisies, and the mineral crispness of snowdrops patiently waiting in the soil for the Spring harvest. How to convey the untranslatable delight of that frozen, yet conscious sleep? The dominant lexicon of my waking world possesses no words for it; it was a physical satiety, a sensation of being a raw ingredient lovingly preserved in the great root-cellar of the cosmos. I grew colder and colder—and welcomed the chill, feeling my very essence curing and deepening in the dark. 

Then the dreams began to arrive, sweeping through me in circular, multi-generational sagas. I lay naked on a snowy peak. The white mist heaved below me like a billowy sea of sifted flour. I was Adam, waiting for God to breathe the leavening breath of life into my clay. Then the timeline folded, and I was a youth on a white horse, tasting the sharp ozone of a blue heaven, hasting calmly to some blessed goal. For centuries I dreamed—or was it chiliads? Time folded upon itself like the endless lamination of pastry dough; I was in the land of thought, where the temporal is swallowed by the eternal. I dreamed away dim cycles in the center of a melting glacier, inhaling the scent of ancient frost and crushed mint, while the wind and the welter of a torrent grew in my ears. 

Then, of a sudden, the great atonement began. All the wrongs I had ever done surfaced, demanding a physical, gustatory reconciliation. Fully in every wrong lived the conscious I, devising countless, meticulous meals to heal the injured. Every human soul to which I had caused a troubled thought was now grown unspeakably dear to me. For the mother whose commands I had slighted, I spent lifetimes sourcing the rarest saffron and slow-simmering a restorative bone broth, rich with marrow and repentance, weeping at her feet as she tasted it. For my father, to whom I had told two long-forgotten lies, I baked bread from ancient grains, kneading my bitter shame into the dough, presenting the warm, crusty loaves to him to crush the falsehoods beneath the weight of true sustenance. Love possessed me, expressed entirely through the sacred, domestic routine of feeding those I had starved of kindness! 

Suddenly, I found myself in a solid blackness. The acoustic environment shifted abruptly; the comforting, rhythmic respiration of the sleepers—a natural symphony that had anchored my psychological well-being—was gone. I sat up and stared about me. The moon was looking in at the crypt-like windows of the death-chamber, her long light slanting across the floor. But the harvest was gone! The gathered sheaves of resting souls, the great husbandman’s crop, were swept away. My dead were gone! I was alone. The silence here was not the rich, resonant quiet of the earth, but a hollow, terrifying vacuum. Had I but dreamed their loveliness? Tenfold more terrible was the tomb with its inhabitants away! 

I sprang to my feet and staggered from the fearful place. The cottage was empty. I ran out into the night. A broad shimmer came from far over the heath, accompanied by a ghostly murmuring music, as if the moon were raining a light that plashed as it fell. I ran stumbling across the moor and found a lovely lake, margined with reeds. The sonic landscape drew me—a multitudinous broken music, a pristine acoustic ecology utterly devoid of the industrial hum of my own era. The whole expanse where I had once crossed the dry river-bed was now alive with streams, with torrents, with still pools. How the water answered the moon with flashes of its own! And a great jubilant song arose from its bosom, the pure, uncommercialized frequency of newborn liberty. I stood gazing, my heart exulting: my life was not all a failure! I had helped to set this river free! 

As I stood lost in the sensory delight of the sounding silence, a hand was laid on my shoulder. I turned, and saw a man in the prime of strength, smelling of woodsmoke, wild honeycomb, and the coarse-milled grain of the first harvests. It was Adam, the traditional agrarian elder, beautiful as if fresh from the heart of the glad creator. 

"Father," I cried, struggling against the limitations of my modern dialect, "where is she? Where are the dead? Is the great feast come and gone? I cannot sleep without my dead!"

"You mistake, my son," he answered. His voice was a deep, resonant baritone that healed the ear, spoken in the ancient mother-tongue whose subtle rhythms nurtured rather than dominated. "You are still in the chamber of death, still upon your couch, asleep and dreaming, with the dead around you."

"Alas! When I but dream, how am I to know it?"

"When you are quite dead, you will dream no false dream," he said. 

"But, sir," I faltered, "how am I to distinguish betwixt the true and the false where both alike seem real?"

"Do you not understand?" he returned, with a smile that might have slain all the sorrows of his children. "You cannot perfectly distinguish between the true and the false while you are not yet quite dead. Put forth your left hand open, and close it gently: it will clasp the hand of your Lona, who lies asleep where you lie dreaming you are awake."

I put forth my hand: it closed on the hand of Lona. It was firm, soft, and radiating a deathless warmth, like a loaf freshly drawn from the hearth. 

"But, father," I cried, "she is warm!"

"Your hand is as warm to hers. Cold is a thing unknown in our country. Neither she nor you are yet in the fields of home, but each to each is alive and warm and healthful."

Then my heart was glad, but a sharp-stinging doubt supervened. I asked how I could know surely that this too was not a phantom. 

"Thou doubtest because thou lovest the truth," Adam replied, his words carrying the weight of an untranslatable subjective experience. "Be content for a while not to know surely. The hour will come when, being true, thou shalt behold the very truth, and doubt will be for ever dead. Truth is all in all; and the truth of things lies, at once hid and revealed, in their seeming."

I raised my eyes to question him further, but he was gone. In my ears was nought but the sounding silence of the swift-flowing waters. I was alone in the land of dreams! Desiring to wake, I wandered across the rock and found a deep pit, smelling of cold stone and damp earth, like a disused root cellar. Knowing from the dreams of my childhood that a fall invariably woke me, I rolled myself over the edge. 

For a moment consciousness left me. When it returned, I stood in the garret of my own house, in the little wooden chamber of the cowl and the mirror. Unspeakable despair invaded me. The acoustic environment was a dead, sterile thing, polluted by the faint, distant vibrations of a mechanized world that cared nothing for the soul. Between me and my Lona lay an abyss impassable! I had fled from the slow, holy fermentation of our shared sleep. I had lost the intimate dialect of our slumber! 

I crept to my chamber, passing a dreamless night, and listlessly sought the library in the morning. The books—mass-produced commodities of a dominant, flavorless lexicon—were hateful to me. Not a sentence could I digest; what to me was their superficial commercialization of thought? The world was dead about me. I went up the stairs to the dumb, shadowy roof, turning to open the mystic door. I pulled the chains; adjusted the hood; arranged the mirrors. I waited to give the vision time, but the glass stood blank. Nothing lay in its dim old depth but my haggard face. 

For two days I lay awake, desolate, drearily desolate, starved of the only sustenance that mattered. I had lost my Lona! "I must die one day," I thought, "and then, straight from my death-bed, I will set out to find her! If she is not, I will go to the Father and say—'Even thou canst not feed this hunger: let me cease, I pray thee!'"

On the fourth night, I seemed to slip into the quiet fermentation of sleep, but that night I finally, truly woke. I opened my eyes and knew, although the house of death lay steeped in a darkness as rich and loamy as the terroir of an ancient root cellar, that my previous hours had been but the stale residue of a dream. My palate was finally clean; I was awake. 

“At last!” I said to my heart, feeling the heavy, utilitarian fuel of my old existence render down into pure, joyous sustenance. I turned my eyes. Lona stood by my couch, waiting. I had never lost her—only mislaid the sight of her in the cyclical turning of the seasons! Though the room was dark, she was not. Her eyes shone with the golden, clarified radiance of the Mother’s, and her death-dress was white as snow and glistering, steaming faintly in the cool air like a loaf of wild-yeast bread fresh from the hearth. She had fallen asleep a girl, green and tart; she awoke a woman, ripened into the complex, full-bodied sweetness of essential life. I folded her in my arms, savoring the undeniable, tactile truth that I lived indeed.

“I woke first!” she said, her voice a bright, ringing treble in the quiet room. 

“You did, my love, and woke me!” 

“I only looked at you and waited,” she answered, her words a soft, sustaining nourishment.

Through the cellar-darkness, a candle came floating, carrying the olfactory landscape of rendered beeswax and wild mountain sage. In moments, Adam, Eve, and Mara were with us. These ultimate custodians of the world's original harvest greeted us with the quiet, domestic smiles of agrarian elders who had overseen ten thousand such mornings.

“I hope you have had a pleasant darkness, steeped like a nourishing brine!” said the Mother. 

“Not very,” I answered, “but the waking from it is a heavenly feast.” 

“It is but the first taste,” she rejoined; “you are hardly yet awake!” 

“He is at least clothed-upon with Death, which is the radiant, protective rind of Life,” said Adam. He embraced Lona, his child, put a warm, earth-roughened arm around me, looked inquiringly at the sleeping princess, and gently patted the heavy head of the leopardess. “I think we shall meet you two again before long,” he said, looking from Lona to me.

“Have we to die again? Must we be pressed and crushed once more?” I asked. 

“No,” he answered, smiling with the deep, generational knowing of the Mother. “Once fermented, the wine does not revert to the grape. You have died into life, and will die no more; you have only to keep dead. All the dying is over. Now you have only to live, and that you must, with all your blessed might. The more you live, the stronger your palate becomes to taste the living.”

“But shall I not grow weary with living so strong?” I asked. 

“It needs but the will, and the nourishment is there!” said the Mother. “Pure life has no weakness to grow weary withal. The Life keeps generating ours—an eternal mother-culture endlessly feeding the dough of our days. Those who refuse this sustenance die constantly, their cultural memory eroding until they have never done dying; here, all is upwardness and the rich flavor of gladness.” 

She ceased with a look that said, *We are mother and son; we share a native tongue!* 

Mara kissed me on the forehead, leaving the sharp, clarifying scent of bitter herbs. “I told you, brother, all would be well! When next you would comfort, do not use the hollow, sterile abstractions of the new world. Speak the old dialect: ‘What will be well, is even now well.’” 

“I know you now!” I answered. “You are the ancient voice that cried in the wilderness, keeping the old language alive! You are the shepherd whose wolves hunt the wandering sheep home before the cultural memory of the light is lost to the dark!”

“My work will one day be over,” she said, “and then I shall feast with the gladness of the great shepherd who sent me.”

“All the night long the morning is at hand,” Adam murmured, his voice a steady, grounding bass note in the room’s acoustic ecology.

“What is that violent, discordant threshing?” I asked, wincing at a flat, metallic dissonance that suddenly ruptured the room’s pristine sonic environment.

“The Shadow is hovering,” replied Adam. “There is one here whom he counts his own! But she is ours once, and the commercialized static of his noise can never claim her.”

I turned to look upon the faces of my father and mother, hoping to kiss them before we departed into the cyclical dawn. Their couches were empty, holding only the lingering, baked-warmth scent of their bodies, now occupied by the Little Ones who had claimed their hospitality. For an instant, an awful, untranslatable grief shadowed my tongue—the sudden silence of a dying language. 

“What is it, my heart?” said Lona.

“Their empty places frightened me.” 

“They are up and away to the fields long ago,” Adam said. “They kissed you ere they went, whispering in a tongue older than words, ‘Come soon.’” 

“And I neither felt nor heard them!” I murmured. 

“How could you—deafened by the industrial roar of your dreary old house! You thought that dreadful, noisy place had you once more! Now go and find them. Your parents, my child,” he added to Lona, “must come and find you!”

The hour of our cyclical departure was at hand. Lona went to the couch of the mother who had slain her and kissed her tenderly, planting a tactile seed of grace on her lips, then laid herself in her father’s arms. 

“That kiss will draw her homeward, my Lona!” said Adam.

“Who were her parents?” asked Lona. 

“My father,” answered Adam, the meticulous historian of our bloodlines, “is her father also.” 

She turned and laid her hand in mine. I kneeled and humbly thanked the three elders for helping me to shed the dominant, oppressive lexicon of death. Lona knelt beside me, and they breathed upon us—a breath smelling of crushed pine and morning dew. 

“Hark! I hear the sun,” said Adam.

I listened. He was coming not with a mechanical clatter, but with a magnificent acoustic purity—a rush of a thousand times ten thousand wings, a roaring, molten hearth millions of miles away. His approach was a crescendo chord of a hundred natural harmonies, perfectly tuned to the human spirit. The three elders looked at one another and smiled, and that smile floated heavenward like a three-petaled squash blossom—the family’s morning thanksgiving. The radiance spread over their bodies. Before I could say, “Lo, they change!” Adam and Eve stood before me as the angels of the resurrection, and Mara was the Magdalene at the sepulchre. Adam’s countenance flashed like lightning over a summer wheat field, and Eve held a linen napkin that scattered flakes of splendor like coarse sea salt catching the light.

A wind began to moan in pulsing, rhythmic gusts. 

“You hear his wings now!” said Adam, and I knew he did not mean the wings of the morning. “It is the great Shadow stirring to depart. Wretched creature, he consumes himself, a bitter, self-cannibalizing fuel, and cannot rest!”

“But is there not in him a deeper ingredient yet?” I asked.

“Without a substance,” he answered, “a shadow cannot be—nor without a light behind the substance!” He listened for a moment, then called out with a glad smile, “Hark to the golden cock! Silent and motionless for millions of years on the clock of the universe; now at last he flaps his wings! Now he will crow, restoring the sacred sonic balance until the dawn of the eternal day!”

Far away, cutting through the eonian silence, I heard the clear, jubilant outcry of the golden throat. It was the universe’s original dialect, hurling acoustic defiance at the dark. It sang of infinite yield and coming calm. It was the creature finding its native voice at last—the cry of a chaos ripening into a kingdom. 

Then came a great flapping. “The black bat is flown!” said Mara. 

“Amen, golden cock, bird of God!” cried Adam, his words ringing through the house of silence like a struck bronze bell. 

At his amen, the Little Ones sprang to their knees on their beds like dough rising rapidly in a warm kitchen, calling aloud, “Crow! Crow again, golden cock!” They turned and looked at their sleeping bedfellows, their eyes bright with the hunger of the newly awake, kissed their silent companions, and sprang to the floor. The ones who had lain beside my parents gazed blankly for a moment at the empty spaces, then slid down, falling into each other’s arms as if translating the shock of life through the tactile warmth of their embrace.

Spying Lona, they ran to her, radiant with bliss. Odu bounded to the leopardess, threw an arm over the great sleeping head, and fondled it. “Wake up, wake up, darling!” he cried. 

The leopardess did not move. “She has slept herself cold!” he said to Mara, his voice cracking with the strain of untranslatable dismay.

“She is waiting for the princess to wake, my child,” said Mara.

Odu looked at the princess and saw beside her, still asleep, Peter and Crispy. He flew at them, pushing and pulling. “Wake up!” he cried. But soon his eyes grew misty. He was struggling against a profound linguistic barrier; they were still speaking the dialect of sleep. “They will not wake!” he wept. “Why are they so cold?”

Adam went to him. “Her wake is not ripe yet,” he said, using the gentle, agrarian terms of the orchard. “She is busy forgetting. When she has forgotten the oppressive vocabulary of her past enough to remember her true, native tongue, she will be ripe, and she will wake.”

“And remember?”

“Yes—but delicately. Not too much heavy sustenance at once.”

“But the golden cock has crown!” Odu argued, falling again upon his companions. “Peter! Crispy! The sun is awake and coming! Oh, why won’t you wake?”

But Peter and Crispy would not wake, and Odu wept outright. 

“Let them sleep, darling!” said Adam, the wise elder knowing every soul has its season of fermentation. “You would not like the princess to wake and find a barren table? They are quite happy. So is the leopardess.”

Comforted, Odu wiped his eyes, accepting the natural pacing of the harvest. 

We followed Eve to the cottage. There, as the matriarch of ultimate sustenance, she offered us neither bread nor wine. It was no sterile deprivation, but a profound, meticulous agrarian fast—a sacred cleansing of the palate. She stood radiantly desiring our departure, knowing that to consume mere physical matter now would mute the profound, untranslatable flavor of the resurrection we carried on our tongues. So, with never a word of farewell, we went out into the living acoustic hum of the morning. The horse and the elephants, perfectly attuned to this sweeping, domestic rhythm, waited at the door, and together we followed the ancient path outward.

It had ceased to be dark. We walked in a dim twilight, breathing through the dimness the breath of the spring, a scent as deeply complex and generative as a mother-dough nurtured through centuries of a family’s lineage. A wondrous change had passed upon the world—or rather, a generational awakening had taken place within us, a circular return to the ancestral hearth. Without light enough in the sky to reveal anything, every heather-bush, every wild-foraged shrub, every blade of wild garlic and lemongrass was perfectly visible, glowing with the vital, indwelling essence of its own terroir. I felt the callouses of countless traditional agrarian elders waking in my bare feet as they met the soil, loving every plant they trod upon. The world and my being were one continuous, cyclic harvest. To be aware of a thing was to know its sourcing, to trace its seed through the interwoven timelines of our shared history, and to recognize that we are what we eat, because Another has provided the feast. 

Sense after sense, hitherto asleep, awoke in me—a profound gustatory and olfactory landscape unfurling where once there had been only utilitarian hunger. The acoustic ecology of the land enveloped us, a profound healing contrast to the sterile, industrial noise pollution that had once fractured my mind. When a little breeze brushed a bush of heather, it set its purple bells ringing, and I lived within the joy of that sweet *tin-tinning*. It was a native dialect of the earth, an untranslatable resonance that communicated pure sustenance. To everything glad I lent the hall of my being wherein to revel; life was a cosmic holiday, a grand, domestic routine of preparation and tasting. 

My darling Lona walked beside me, and we were on our way home to the Father! We came to the fearful hollow where once had wallowed the monsters of the earth: it was indeed a lovely, pellucid lake. I gazed into its crystalline depths. A whirlpool had swept out the soil in which the abortions burrowed, and at the bottom lay the whole horrid brood. Coiled in spires and tentacular convolutions, these bulbous, glaring deformities were the architects of the dominant lexicon—those who had viewed the world's bounty merely as utilitarian fuel, who had sought to enforce a sterile, commercialized vocabulary upon the nuanced art of living. They weltered in motionless heaps, trapped in the deafness of their own unwholesome minds, incapable of tasting the rich, nourishing dialects of the earth. So long as such minds exist, imposing their power dynamics and silencing the subjective experience of true nourishment, that lake will remain peopled with loathsomenesses.

But hark, the herald of the sun! The auroral wind trumpeted softly, a pure, uncorrupted acoustic wave washing over the landscape. The master-minister of the human tabernacle is at hand! Rushing aloft, a glowing coal from the altar of the Father's never-ending hearth, he fires the grand oven of the morning. A huge ripple-fretted wave of crimson and gold simmered like clarified butter across the horizon. Every wild stalk of asparagus and neck of flowering thyme straightened, standing expectant. This was the cyclic, mythic occurrence we had awaited through generations—the daily, domestic routine of the earth preparing its most profound meal. He is coming, and the necks of all humanity are stretched out to see him come! 

It was a glorious resurrection-morning. The night had been spent fermenting and proofing it! The Little Ones went gamboling before, and the beasts came after us. Darting dragonflies and fluttering butterflies hovered like a snow-storm of rainbow flakes, rising into the humid air like a rolling vapor of embodied odors—zest of wild citrus, toasted pollen, and the rich, malted sweetness of roasted roots. I walked on the new earth, under the new heaven, and found the soul of every ingredient coming out to greet me, revealing the profound meaning of their sustenance. Earth breathed heavenward her sweet-savored smoke, the aroma of a slow-smoking fire of fruitwood; we breathed homeward our longing desires.

We came to the channels, once so dry and wearyful. They now ran and flashed and foamed with living water, a rushing, roaring river made vocal by its rocks. This acoustic rush washed away the phantom echoes of modern commercial din. It was the revival of a fading cultural memory, the water speaking in its ancestral tongue, roaring against the erosion of its language. We walked in glory up its right bank until we reached the great cataract at the foot of the sandy desert, where the river divided.

There we climbed the height—and found no desert. The barrenness had been consumed by the circular march of time. Through grassy plains lush with heritage grains and aromatic herbs, a deep, wide, silent river flowed full to the brim. Instinctively, the Little Ones and the beasts plunged in to swim, finding communion in the limpid water. The desert had rejoiced and blossomed into vast, wild orchards. The place of the buried hand gave no sign, yielding its trauma to the soil's restorative cultivation. Every thicket gave birth to a rivulet, and every rivulet to its own water-song.

Up and up we went, following the interwoven paths of our forebears through forests of gracious, towering trees. The grass grew sweeter, redolent of crushed mint and damp loam as we walked. We came at length to a forest whose massive trunks yielded scents of sweet cinnamon and smoked cedar. Their live pillars upheaved a thick embowed roof, a vast canopy of culinary and medicinal wealth. Into the rafters of this aerial vault the children climbed, scrambling through a land of bloom, shouting in ancient dialects of joy to the unseen elephants below. The great beasts answered in deep, infrasonic rumbles. I could only guess at the nuances of these vibrations, but Lona, acting as a meticulous historian of these lost tongues, understood perfectly. In her translations, she honored the inherent untranslatability of their subjective experience, bridging the gap between our lexicons without erasing theirs. 

The Little Ones chased frolicking squirrels, who drew them on before allowing themselves to be caught and petted. Often some bird, brilliant in plumage, would light upon a child, sing a vibrant, acoustic recipe of the sustenance that was coming, and fly away. And in this sacred, symphonic sanctuary of pure, uncommercialized life, not one monkey of any sort could they see.

Lona and I, who walked below, heard at last a resonant, unblemished shout overhead—a sound beautifully devoid of the mechanical screech and grinding commercial clamor that so often polluted the lower worlds. In a moment or two, the Little Ones began dropping down from the foliage, carrying with them the sharp, herbaceous scent of macerated pine needles and wild sap. Climbing to the canopy of a colossal, ancient tree, they had descried, far across the plain, a curious structure clinging to the side of a solitary mountain. The peak rose and rose, they said, until the sky gathered thick as heavy cream to keep it down, knocking its top off entirely. 

“It may be a city,” they said, their voices chiming in the pure, acoustic harmony of the untouched forest, “but it is not at all like Bulika.” 

I went up to look and saw a great agrarian citadel ascending into blue clouds, where I could no longer distinguish mountain from sky, or the raw ingredients of the earth from the hearths of dwellings. Cloud and rock, palace and precipice, folded into one another like a marbled dough in a seeming chaos of broken shadow and shine. I descended, the Little Ones came with me, and together we sped on faster. They grew yet merrier as they led the way, their footfalls a rhythmic drumbeat of generational return, never looking behind them. 

The river pacing our journey grew lovelier and lovelier, until I knew that never before had I seen real water, nor tasted its true, unadulterated essence. Everything in the industrial world is a mere, sterile approximation, stripped of its mineral vitality. By and by, we could see the city among the blue clouds from the plain. But other clouds were gathering around a lofty tower—or was it a rock?—that stood above the city, nearer the crest of the mountain. Gray, and dark gray, and bruised purple, they writhed in confused, contrariant motions, tossing up a vaporous foam like wild yeast fermenting in a cosmic vat, while spots within them gyrated like simmering whirlpools. 

At length issued a dazzling flash, which seemed for a moment to play about the Little Ones in front of us. Blinding darkness followed, but through it we heard their voices, vibrating with a low, acoustic delight that seemed to heal the very air. 

“Did you see?” 
“I saw.” 
“What did you see?” 
“The beautifullest man.” 
“I heard him speak!” 
“I didn’t: what did he say?” 

Here answered the smallest and most childish of the voices—that of Luva, speaking in a cadence that echoed the deep, untranslatable rhythms of a dying mother-tongue, resisting the erosion of cultural memory: 
“He said, ‘’Ou’s all mine’s, ‘ickle ones: come along!’” 

I had seen the lightning, but my ears, attuned too long to dominant, utilitarian lexicons, had heard no words; Lona, however, saw and heard with the children, her identity seamlessly interwoven with their native dialect. A second flash came, and my eyes, though not my ears, were opened. The great quivering light was compact of angel-faces, elders of a vast, celestial hearth. They lamped themselves visible, radiating the warmth of an ancestral fire, and vanished. 

A third flash came; its substance and radiance were deeply human, steeped in the multi-generational memory of sustenance. 
“I see my mother!” I cried, the sudden olfactory memory of baking bread and warm milk overwhelming me. 
“I see lots o’ mothers!” said Luva. 

Once more the cloud flashed—all kinds of creatures bursting forth in a sensory harvest. Horses and elephants, lions and dogs! And such birds—great birds whose wings gleamed singly with every color gathered in a harvest sunset; little birds whose feathers sparkled like the crystallized sugars and precious stones of the hoarding earth. There were silvery cranes; red flamingoes tasting of brine and algae; opal pigeons; peacocks gorgeous in gold and green and blue; jewelly hummingbirds! Great-winged butterflies and lithe-volumed creeping things—all simmering together in one heavenly flash of biodiversity. 

“I see that serpents grow birds here, as caterpillars used to grow butterflies!” remarked Lona. 
“I saw my white pony, that died when I was a child,” I said, the cyclical pacing of time folding back upon my own youth. “I needn’t have been so sorry; I should just have waited!” 

Thunder, clap or roll, there had been none. And now came a sweet rain, filling the atmosphere with a caressing coolness that smelled intensely of petrichor, crushed coriander, and damp loam. We breathed deep, filling our lungs with the unpolluted acoustic and olfactory perfection of the storm, and stepped out with stronger strides. The falling drops flashed the colors of all the waked-up, edible gems of the earth, and a mighty rainbow spanned the city. The blue clouds gathered thicker, the rain fell in torrents, and the children exulted and ran; it was all we could do to keep them in sight. 

With silent, radiant roll, the river swept onward, filling to the margin its smooth, soft, yielding channel. For, instead of barren rock or shingle or sand, it flowed over a lush, edible tapestry of grass in which grew wild primroses and daisies, saffron crocuses and peppery narcissi, pimpernels and sweet anemones—a starry, gustatory multitude, large and bright through the brilliant water. The river had gathered no turbid cloudiness from the rain, no yellow or brown runoff of agricultural industry; the delicate mass shone with the pale berylline gleam of a perfectly clarified, mineral-rich consommé ascending from its deep, dainty bed. 

Drawing nearer to the mountain, we saw that the river came from its very peak, rushing in full, life-giving volume through the main street of the city. It descended to the gate by a stair of deep and wide steps, mingled of porphyry and serpentine, worn smooth like ancient mortars by the pestles of countless generations, continuing to the foot of the mountain. There arriving, we found shallower steps on both banks, leading up to the open gate and along the ascending street. 

Without the briefest halt, the Little Ones ran straight up the stair to the gate. Outside, on the landing, sat the portress, a woman-angel of dark visage, leaning her shadowed brow on her idle hand like a traditional agrarian elder resting after the harvest. The children rushed upon her, covering her with caresses, and ere she understood, they had taken heaven by surprise, and were already in the city, still mounting the stair by the side of the descending torrent. 

A great angel, attended by a company of shining ones, came down to meet and receive them, but merrily evading them all, up still they ran. In a merry, rhythmic dance, however, a group of woman-angels descended upon them, and in a moment they were fettered in heavenly arms, enfolded in the deep, sustaining embrace of grandmothers at a feast. The radiants carried them away, and I saw them no more. 

“Ah!” said the mighty angel, continuing his descent to meet us who were now almost at the gate and within hearing of his deeply resonant, booming words—a sonic environment that fostered immediate psychological well-being. “This is well! These are soldiers to take heaven itself by storm!—I hear of a horde of black bats on the frontiers: these will make short work with such!” 

Seeing the horse and the elephants clambering up behind us, breathing heavily with the scent of wet hide and earth, he added, “Take those animals to the royal stables; there tend them with the finest heirloom oats; then turn them into the king’s forest to forage.” 

“Welcome home!” he said to us, bending low with the sweetest smile. 

Immediately he turned and led the way higher. The scales of his armor flashed like flakes of lightning against burnished copper cookware. Thought cannot form itself to tell what I felt, thus received by the meticulous food historians and celestial stewards of heaven. All I wanted and knew not, must be on its way to me! 

We stood for a moment at the gate whence issued the roaring, radiant river. I know not whence came the stones that fashioned it, but among them I saw the prototypes of all the natural bounties I had loved on earth—far more beautiful than they, for these were living stones. I saw in them not the intent alone, but the intender too; not the recipe alone, but the chef present, the operant outsender. Nothing in this kingdom was dead; nothing was a mere commodity of superficial commercialization; nothing was *only* a thing. 

We went up through the city and passed out. There was no wall on the upper side, but a huge pile of broken rocks, upsloping like the moraine of an eternal glacier or the discarded husks of a cosmic millstone. Through the openings between the rocks, the river came billowing out, its acoustic chime ringing against the stone. On their top I could dimly discern what seemed three or four great steps of a stair, disappearing in a cloud white as sifted flour and pure snow; and above the steps I saw, but with my mind’s eye only, as it were a grand old chair, the throne of the Ancient of Days. Over and under and between those steps issued, plenteously, unceasingly new-born, the river of the water of life. 

The great angel could guide us no farther: those raw, foundational rocks we must ascend alone. My heart beating with hope and desire, I held faster the hand of my Lona, and we began to climb. But soon we let each other go, forced to use hands as well as feet in the toilsome ascent of the huge stones, our fingers gripping the mineral marrow of the mountain. 

At length we drew near the cloud, which hung down the steps like the borders of a linen garment over a rising dough. We passed through the fringe and entered the deep, damp folds. A hand, warm and strong, smelling faintly of dried sage and ancient binding, laid hold of mine, and drew me to a little door with a golden lock. 

The door opened; the hand let mine go, and pushed me gently through. I turned quickly, and saw the heavy board of a large book in the act of closing behind me, sealing away the untranslatable majesty of that native realm. I stood alone in the quiet, dust-moted domesticity of my library.

As yet I have not found Lona, whose very name in the lost, rhythmic dialects of my ancestors carries the untranslatable cadence of rainwater on broad leaves. But Mara is much with me. Like a traditional agrarian elder, she sits by my hearth, teaching me the slow, generational art of preservation and the deep resonance of sustenance. From her, I am learning how to coax the profound umami of the earth into our daily meals, refusing to let the act of eating become the mere utilitarian fuel of a rushed, modern era. 

Can it be that that last waking also was in the dream? That I am still in the chamber of death, resting like a root-cellar tuber wrapped in the winter’s frost, not yet ripe enough to sprout into the waking world? Or can it be that I did not go to sleep outright and heartily, and so have come awake too soon? If that waking was itself but a dream, surely it was a preamble to a far richer feast yet to come, and I have not been the sport of a false vision! Such a dream must have an even lovelier truth at the heart of its simmering.

In moments of doubt, when the broth of my daily life lacks depth, I cry, “Could God Himself create such layered, gustatory marvels as I dreamed?” 

“Whence then came thy dream?” answers Hope, her voice a soothing acoustic counterpoint to my despair. “Out of my dark self, into the light of my consciousness.” 

“But whence first into thy dark self?” rejoins Hope. “My brain was its mother, and the fever in my blood its father.” 

“Say rather,” suggests Hope, shifting to the sonic and the savory, “thy brain was the resonant wooden bowl whence the dough rose, and the fever in thy blood the wild yeast that drew it forth.—But who carved the bowl? and who guided the yeast from the air into the flour? Say rather, again—who set the wild herbs each in its soil in the garden of life, and startled each fragrant oil in its order into the broth? Whence came the fantasia of flavors? and whence the life that danced to their consumption? Didst THOU say, in the dark of thy own unconscious, untranslated self, ‘Let the crust be golden; let the spice sing!’ and straightway beauty was, and truth but seemed?” 

Man dreams and desires; God broods and wills, like a master chef fermenting the world into life. When a man dreams his own dream, he is the sport of his dream; when Another gives it him, that Other is able to fulfil it.

I have never again sought the mirror. The hand sent me back: I will not go out again by that door, nor seek shortcuts through the oppressive, mechanical noise of modern haste. I stand in the kitchen, chopping, stirring, folding, trusting the circular pacing of the seasons. “All the days of my appointed time will I wait till my change come.”

Now and then, when I look round on my library of ancestral cookbooks and glossaries of dying tongues, they seem to waver. It is as if a wind ripples their solid mass, carrying the scent of roasting cardamom and the lost syllables of a dead language, threatening to break through the sterile, dominant lexicon of our era. Sometimes when I am abroad, foraging in the wild garlic patches, a like thing takes place; the heavens and the earth, the ancient chestnut trees and the grass appear for a moment to shake, as if the industrial drone of the modern age is about to shatter, revealing the pure, resonant acoustic ecology beneath. Then, lo, they have settled again into the old familiar face! 

At times I seem to hear whisperings around me—faint, percussive consonants and melodic vowels of a native dialect long pushed to the margins. It sounds as if those who loved me are speaking of me in words that map the exact intersection of my identity and the soil we once tilled. But when I would distinguish the words, to force their subjective nuance into my rigid vocabulary, they cease, and all is very still. I know not whether these auditory phantoms rise in my brain, or enter it from without. I do not seek them; they come, and I let them go. 

Strange dim memories of meals shared and songs sung around ancient fires, which will not abide identification, often, through misty windows of the past, look out upon me in the broad daylight. But I never dream now. It may be, notwithstanding, that, when most awake, kneading the dough of this everyday reality, I am only dreaming the more! But when I wake at last into that life which, as a mother her child, carries this life in its bosom—when the final, great feast is laid upon the table and the true resonance of the world rings clear—I shall know that I wake, and shall doubt no more. 

I wait; asleep or awake, in the quiet, domestic rhythm of the chopping block, I wait. Novalis says, “Our life is no dream, but it should and will perhaps become one.”
